# Stage 1 detailed curriculum: 1.3 Calculus

Blueprint work file, Stage 2 Phase 1. Draft 2, 6 October 2026.

**Source of truth.** `docs/curriculum.md` for stages, topics, hours and exit tests. This file expands item 1.3 Calculus (200 h) into courses, levels, lessons, homework, the topic exam and reading, and rebuilds the 1.3 exit test in the app.

**Draft 2** (same day) keeps every section number, level code, lesson number and lesson id of draft 1, because `assessment.md`, `canon-gap.md`, `stage1-linear-algebra.md`, `stage1-probability.md` and `stage1-statistics-ts.md` cite them (1.3 lessons 27, 28, 47, 72, 73, 75, 85, 87, 92, 97, 98, 99 to 104, 106 to 137, levels I6 and M4, decisions 2, 7 and 12, open items 2 and 5). It reconciles this file with what was written after draft 1: the final 0.1 and 0.2 lesson lists in `stage0-number-algebra.md` and the 0.3 draft 2 lists in `stage0-precalc-computing.md`, against which sixteen 1.3 lessons repeated a hook, a trap or an edge case and now extend the earlier lesson instead; `arch-decision.md` (Pyodide on both targets, MicroPython dropped); `assessment.md` draft 2 (section bars, the `sets` instrument, Mastered at 75%); `content-format.md` revision 2 (feedback fields, tutor fields, element references, `needs`, `Level.companion`); `canon-gap.md` revision 2 (source ids, the OpenStax licence); `bertie.md` (states and placements); and `spikes/README.md`. It also fixes seven defects found on a second reading. Section 0.6 lists every change and its reason.

**Built on.** `docs/design-research.md` sections 5, 8 and 9 (lesson anatomy, curriculum architecture, teaching style) and 10 (critiques); `docs/blueprint/work/widgets-calc-linalg.md` (the calculus widget catalogue; its working outline 1.3.1 to 1.3.8 is replaced by this file's lesson list); `docs/blueprint/work/widgets-stage0.md` 15.1 (the id reconciliation); `docs/blueprint/work/content-format.md` revision 2 (ids, schema, screen types, checks, feedback and tutor fields, verification); `docs/blueprint/work/assessment.md` draft 2 (assessment ladder, the `finals` paper kind, section bars, exit parts); `docs/blueprint/work/ai-tutor.md` (Bertie's availability and marking); `docs/blueprint/work/arch-decision.md` (decision 15, Python on both targets); `docs/blueprint/work/bertie.md` (states and where he appears); `docs/blueprint/work/canon-gap.md` revision 2 (the 1.3 resource canon, its `src.*` ids and the proposals that border this item); `spikes/README.md`; the sibling `docs/curriculum/stage1-python-tools.md` draft 2 (the runtime differences it measured on Pyodide); the sibling files `docs/curriculum/stage0-number-algebra.md` and `docs/curriculum/stage0-precalc-computing.md` (whose limits, partial fractions, parametric curves, series and optimisation by completing the square this file builds on and does not repeat); the owner's lesson-creator, maths-teaching, code-craft and project-forge skills.

**Tags.** [our choice] marks a design decision. [check] marks a resource detail (a chapter, section, lecture or unit number or title, a problem-set name) that I recall but could not confirm from this environment; confirm it before the content cites it (`checked: false` in `Citation` and `Level.companion` until then). [recalled] marks a fact from memory that I am confident of but did not verify here. [measured] marks a fact read from a measurement recorded in this repository. [searched] marks a fact `canon-gap.md` revision 2 confirmed by a web search.

**Answers.** Every numeric answer in sections 2 (hooks that state a number), 3, 4 and 7 was computed on 6 October 2026 with Python 3.11.15, SymPy 1.14.0, NumPy 2.4.6, SciPy 1.17.1 and mpmath 1.3.0 (symbolic limits, derivatives, integrals and series; exact fractions for Riemann sums, quotients and iterates; `brentq` and `nsolve` for numeric roots). Draft 2 re-ran the draft 1 numbers it keeps, computed the new hooks and edges, and ran a reference solution and every known-wrong solution of all 33 code drills against the hidden tests section 3 states (175 checks). The re-run found one defect: lesson 32's bond prices hold for a continuously compounded yield, which the hook now says.

**A conflict with the brief.** The brief for this file says Pyodide cannot run in the Artifact and that WebAssembly there was still being measured. `spikes/README.md` records both as measured on the owner's iPhone on 6 October 2026: every probe check passed except service workers, and a repackaged Pyodide 314.0.7 loaded NumPy and pandas and computed correctly [measured]. `arch-decision.md` decision 15 then put Pyodide on both targets and records the owner dropping MicroPython. This file follows the measurement and the owner's decision. The brief's fallback still holds for any device that cannot load Python, and for packages a build does not ship: the drill hands off to the web app and returns by result code (`assessment.md` 14.3).

**At a glance.**

| Item | Hours | Courses | Levels | Lessons | Lesson hours | Homework sets | Topic exam |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1.3 Calculus | 200 | 5 | 25 | 137 | 34.25 (17.1%) | 25 | Finals-style paper (`exm.calc.finals`, kind `finals`): 180 min, 11 multi-part questions, 100 marks; Section A in the style of an MIT 18.01 final (70 marks), Section B in the style of the 18.02 partial-derivative and Lagrange problem sets (30 marks); pass 70% overall and 70% on Section A |

1.3 exit (`1.3.exit`): the finals-style paper and the four multivariable homework sets gate it in the app; a real 18.01 final at 70% and the 18.02SC Unit 2 problem sets confirm it (section 4.3).

---

## 0. Scope, decisions and conventions

### 0.1 Decisions

| # | Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Reason |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Unit of lesson time | **15 minutes per lesson, quiz included**, the same unit as every curriculum file | 10 or 12 minutes | One unit across the path keeps hours tables comparable; design research 5.8 allows 10 to 15 minutes of screens, and the quiz is part of the sitting |
| 2 | Reading spine | **OpenStax Calculus volumes 1 to 3** (`src.openstax-calculus`) for reading and as the problem bank; **MIT 18.01 and 18.02 lectures** (the Jerison Fall 2006 and Auroux Fall 2007 videos that 18.01SC and 18.02SC use) for the lecture parallel; **18.01SC and 18.02SC** problem sets, unit exams and the final for external problems and the exit; **3Blue1Brown's Essence of Calculus** before each course, as the curriculum says. Stewart (9th edition, Early Transcendentals) is the alternative column in section 5 | Stewart as the spine; Strang's Calculus as the spine | The curriculum allows "Stewart, or OpenStax". OpenStax is free on every device and has answers to odd-numbered exercises. Its licence is CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 [searched], so its exercises are cited and mirrored (`problem-inspired`), never adapted (`canon-gap.md` 6, `content-format.md` 9.4). `canon-gap.md` revision 2 adopted this choice |
| 3 | Boundary with Stage 0 | **Where the final 0.2 and 0.3 lesson lists teach an idea, 1.3 extends it and never re-teaches it.** 0.2 lesson 109 estimated gradients from shrinking chords (with the $\frac00$ trap and the corner of $\lvert x \rvert$); 0.3 level R5 taught limits from tables, one-sided limits, limit laws, limits at infinity, continuity and the intermediate value theorem; 0.3 levels G1 to G5 taught convergence of sequences, $e$, the slope of $e^x$, the growth hierarchy and geometric and telescoping series; 0.2 level Q4 optimised by completing the square. Sixteen draft 1 lessons that repeated one of these now open on a new problem and teach what the earlier lesson could not (section 0.2 lists each). Lesson numbers and ids are unchanged | Recap lessons; repeating R5 as a full level | A lesson the learner has already done is 15 minutes of the 200 hours spent twice, and a repeated hook kills the surprise a hook depends on (design research 9). Recaps happen anyway: level reviews draw 30% from earlier levels, the Redo queue returns misses, and the level check (`assessment.md` 2.7) lets a learner who tests out of D1's opening skip it |
| 4 | Course order | **D, U, I, X, M**: the derivative, using the derivative, the integral, series, many variables | Series before integrals; multivariable interleaved | It follows 18.01 then 18.02, the exit's order. Series follow integrals because the integral test, Stirling's estimate and the remainder bounds use integrals. Many variables come last because the second derivative test needs Taylor's theorem and a fluent single-variable toolkit |
| 5 | Rigour | **Two $\varepsilon$-$\delta$ lessons, proofs of the differentiation rules, the mean value theorem, both parts of the fundamental theorem, the contraction condition for iteration, and the irrationality of $e$**, each with a long-form item. No uniform convergence and no proof that continuous functions are integrable | Computation only (18.01's emphasis); a full analysis course | 18.01 finals do not demand $\varepsilon$-$\delta$, but a top programme does (Cambridge IA Analysis I, MIT 18.100) and interviewers ask "prove it". 0.3 R1 and R2 gave the proof methods. Full analysis is `canon-gap.md`'s optional P16 |
| 6 | Topics the 18.01 final tests but quants rarely use | **One lesson each**: volumes by discs and shells, arc length and surface area, polar area, work and centre of mass (level I5) | Dropping them; a full level each | The exit is a real 18.01 final at 70%; leaving them out costs marks the learner cannot afford. One lesson each, taught as "slice, approximate, add", is the minimum |
| 7 | Differential equations | **One level of four lessons (I6)** on first-order equations: slope fields, separable, Euler's method, linear with an integrating factor, including continuous compounding at a varying rate and mean reversion | None; a full course | 18.01 includes separable equations [recalled: lecture 16 of the Fall 2006 course]; continuous compounding and mean reversion are daily quant tools. Second-order equations and systems wait for `canon-gap.md`'s P8 (Differential equations, now 45 h) |
| 8 | Multivariable scope | **Through partial derivatives, gradients, the chain rule, the Hessian test, Lagrange multipliers, double and triple integrals, polar, cylindrical and spherical coordinates and the Jacobian.** No line integrals, Green's, Stokes' or divergence theorems | 18.02 in full | The curriculum's cover list stops at multiple integrals and Lagrange multipliers. Vector calculus is physics-facing and rarely used in quant or ML work; it would cost about 20 hours |
| 9 | Quant and ML transfer | **Every level has at least one transfer screen** into finance, statistics or machine learning, and fifteen lessons are built around a quant or ML use: maximum likelihood and the median (47, 123), the markup rule (46), Kelly and minimum variance (48, 128), the volatility drag of a fund that triples each day's move (52), densities and expectations (72, 73, 135), Breeden-Litzenberger (74), mean reversion (85), Taylor for duration, convexity, delta and gamma (105), backpropagation (116), gradient descent (53, 117) | Pure calculus with applications left to 1.5 to 2.1 | The owner's goal is quant and ML-lab thinking; these are the places where calculus is the job. Each is still taught from a problem the learner can attempt cold |
| 10 | Code | **One code drill in every homework set and eight practice drills (section 3.3), all standard library only, so every drill declares `needs: []`** and runs under Pyodide on both targets (`arch-decision.md` decision 15, `assessment.md` decision 30). Seeded drills use `random.Random(seed)` and only its `random()` method, whose sequence for a given integer seed Python guarantees not to change across versions [recalled: the `random` module's documentation on reproducibility]. NumPy, SciPy and matplotlib only in the Calculus Lab project | NumPy drills throughout; draft 1's `micropython-ok` tag and a home-made generator | The drills check calculus, not library use, and the standard library keeps the first Python load on a phone to the core groups. MicroPython is gone (`arch-decision.md` 0.3), so the generator draft 1 needed for it is not needed |
| 11 | Calculators | Lesson screens mark calculator use per screen; homework allows the in-app scientific calculator except on items marked "exact"; **the finals-style paper allows none** and asks for exact forms (`assessment.md` 3.1) | A calculator everywhere | MIT finals are sat without calculators [recalled]; exact answers are what the exit tests |
| 12 | Topic exam | **The finals-style paper is the topic exam**: Section A rebuilds the 18.01 final, Section B the 18.02 partial-derivative and Lagrange work, with a double integral so the paper covers the whole item. Pass: 70% overall **and** 70% on Section A, set by `sectionPass` on the paper (`assessment.md` decision 27) | Two papers (an 18.01 paper and a multivariable paper); one paper with no section bar | One three-hour sitting matches the `finals` kind. The section bar stops a strong multivariable section from carrying a weak 18.01 section past the curriculum's named bar |
| 13 | Sample lesson | **Lesson 1, "Speed at an instant"** (`lsn.deriv.instant-speed`), rewritten in draft 2 so that it extends 0.2 lesson 109 instead of repeating it | The fundamental theorem (lesson 58); the chain rule (lesson 15) | Every later lesson rests on the difference quotient and its limit. After 0.2 lesson 109's estimate and 0.3 R5's limits, what remains is the exact rate, the rate as a function of time, and the exact error of a one-window reading, which is where 1.3 begins |
| 14 | Display codes | **D, U, I, X, M, unique within 1.3; the app shows codes with their topic ("1.3 D1") wherever two topics can appear together** | Two-letter codes (`stage1-statistics-ts.md` decision 2); renaming to avoid 1.1's D | Single letters ran out across the path: 1.1 and 1.3 both use D, and 1.4 and 1.5 share N, H, V and Z. Scoping codes by topic removes every clash at once and keeps the codes other files already cite ("1.3 level I6", "M4"); ids, which learner progress refers to, never depended on them |
| 15 | Exit hours | **Charged to the exams block** (15 h): two practice papers, the finals-style paper, a real 18.01 final, and marking. The 18.02SC problem sets that confirm the exit sit in the external-problems block | A separate block | The curriculum's 200 hours must hold everything |
| 16 | Companion work (new in draft 2) | **Every level lists its outside reading and problems with hours (`Level.companion`), and the 25 levels sum exactly to the external block, 61.5 h** (section 5.0). Lectures count 40 minutes each at 1.25 times speed, rounded to the quarter hour per row; an OpenStax exercise counts 7.5 minutes; an 18.01SC part problem set 30 minutes; an 18.01SC unit exam 1 hour; an 18.02SC exit problem set 1 to 2 hours. Rows marked stretch, optional or preview carry no hours | Draft 1's block total with exercise counts that did not fit it (about 290 OpenStax exercises in 4.5 hours) | `content-format.md` 3.8 makes companion hours content that CI sums against the curriculum's hours, so they must add up per level, and the counts must be doable in the time they claim |
| 17 | The 18.01SC unit exams (new in draft 2) | **All four, timed and self-marked against MIT's solutions, one at the end of the levels that finish each unit** (D5, U5, I5, I6), **and at X3, where 18.01SC has no Unit 5 exam, the 18.01 Fall 2006 Problem Set 8B on series and Taylor series sat timed in one hour** | Only the final; the unit exams as stretch | The exit is a real 18.01 final at 70%. The unit exams are the same department's papers with solutions (Exams 1, 3 and 4 and the final have posted solutions [searched]; Exam 2 [check]), and five early calibrations against the real standard are worth more than the same hours of OpenStax exercises |
| 18 | Practice drills (new in draft 2) | **Eight specified drills of 30 minutes (section 3.3)**: Richardson extrapolation, a bond yield by the secant method, golden-section search, adaptive Simpson, Heun's method, a Taylor polynomial by Horner's rule with its remainder count, a Lagrange check by scanning a constraint, and Monte Carlo volume in $d$ dimensions | Draft 1's "8 extra code drills" with no content | An hours row with nothing behind it is a defect; each drill is a numerical method a quant uses, one per level that has no second drill |

### 0.2 What 1.3 inherits from Stage 0 and 1.1, and does not repeat

Checked against the final lesson lists of `stage0-number-algebra.md` (0.1 lessons 1 to 42, 0.2 lessons 1 to 111) and `stage0-precalc-computing.md` draft 2 (0.3 lessons 1 to 146). "0.2 lesson 109" means lesson 109 of item 0.2.

| Topic | Taught in | 1.3 does |
|---|---|---|
| Gradient of a curve from shrinking chords; the $\frac00$ trap; the corner of $\lvert x \rvert$ | 0.2 lesson 109 | Lesson 1 opens on a radar reading's exact error and the rate as a function of time; its trap is the centred window, its edge a zero rate without rest. Lesson 8 opens on the tangent triangle of $\frac1x$; lesson 9 zooms on a cusp and a vertical tangent, not a corner |
| Distance-time and speed-time graphs; area under a curve by trapezia | 0.2 lessons 43 and 110 | Lesson 54 asks for guaranteed bounds from rectangles |
| Iteration and cobwebs; change of sign and bisection | 0.2 lessons 107 and 108 | Lesson 86 proves the contraction condition with the mean value theorem; lesson 7 assumes bisection and spends its time on the extreme value theorem |
| The mean minimises squared error; AM-GM by completing the square; revenue maximised by completing the square; the largest rectangle for a fence | 0.2 lessons 73 to 77 (level Q4) | Lesson 46 derives the markup rule for constant elasticity; lesson 47 opens on maximum likelihood; lesson 51 derives AM-GM for $n$ numbers from Jensen |
| Circles and the tangent from the perpendicular radius | 0.2 lesson 72 | Lesson 22 recovers the same slope implicitly and then does what the radius trick cannot (the folium) |
| Binary floating point; `math.isclose` | 0.1 lesson 42 | Lesson 27 adds round-off against truncation in finite differences |
| Limits from tables and graphs, one-sided limits, limit laws and techniques, limits at infinity, continuity, the intermediate value theorem | 0.3 level R5 (lessons 142 to 146) | Lesson 2 adds the conditions behind the laws (continuity for substitution, existence for the product law); lessons 3 to 5 add the squeeze theorem and $\varepsilon$-$\delta$; lesson 7 the extreme value theorem |
| What "tends to" means; the fixed-point caution | 0.3 lesson 43 | Lesson 6 proves convergence (monotone convergence, the bound on $(1 + \frac1n)^n$); lesson 86 proves when iteration converges |
| $e$ from compounding; $e^x$ as its own gradient, found by drawing tangents | 0.3 lessons 46 to 48 | Lesson 6 proves $e$ exists; lesson 19 proves the slope is proportional to the height and that $Ce^{kx}$ are the only solutions of $f' = kf$ |
| Log laws, log returns, the growth hierarchy by tables, underflow | 0.3 levels G3 and G4 (lessons 49 to 59) | Lesson 20 differentiates $\ln$; lesson 41 proves the ordering and handles indeterminate powers; lesson 52 uses log growth for volatility drag |
| Geometric, arithmetic and telescoping series, sums to infinity, $\sum kr^{k-1}$, present value | 0.3 level G5 (lessons 60 to 65) and lesson 136 | Lesson 87 asks when an infinite sum exists at all; lesson 95 reads $\sum kr^{k-1}$ as a derivative; level X1 builds the tests |
| Small-angle approximations | 0.3 lesson 86 | Lesson 3 proves $\frac{\sin x}{x} \to 1$; lessons 30 and 32 generalise the approximations |
| Euler's formula as a limit of compounding rotations | 0.3 lesson 97 | Lesson 104 derives it from the series |
| Binomial expansion and the binomial series for small $x$ | 0.3 level C3 | Lesson 11 uses $(x + h)^n$ for the power rule; lesson 101 proves the binomial series with its radius |
| Trigonometry in radians, identities, $R\sin(x + \alpha)$, inverse trigonometric functions | 0.3 levels T1 to T4 | Lessons 3, 18, 21 and 67 rely on them |
| Complex numbers, the Argand diagram, $e^{i\theta}$ as rotation | 0.3 level T6 | Lesson 96 uses complex singularities; lesson 104 derives Euler's formula |
| Vectors, dot product, projection; distance in 3 and $n$ dimensions | 0.3 level S8, lesson 11 | Level M1 adds the cross product and planes; M2 the gradient |
| $2 \times 2$ matrices, determinants as area scale factors | 0.3 level T5 (lesson 91) | Lesson 134 (the Jacobian) |
| Rational functions, oblique asymptotes, partial fractions | 0.3 level R3 (lessons 133 to 136) | Lesson 41 finds asymptotes of non-rational functions; lesson 68 integrates partial fractions |
| Parametric curves, conics | 0.3 level R4 | Lessons 23 and 79 differentiate and measure them |
| Proof by contrapositive, contradiction, induction; $\sum k^2$ by induction | 0.3 levels R1 and R2 (lesson 130) | Every long-form proof; homework I1 example A cites lesson 130 |
| Area by cutting and rearranging | 0.3 lesson 5 | Lesson 77's Cavalieri edge |
| Python functions, loops, floats, `math` | 0.4, 1.1 | Every code drill; NumPy in the Calculus Lab |
| Git and pytest | 1.2 | The Calculus Lab lives in the learner's repository with tests (curriculum 1.2 exit) |

### 0.3 Conventions for every lesson

**Ids** follow content-format.md section 4.1. Courses: `crs.deriv`, `crs.deriv-apps`, `crs.integral`, `crs.series`, `crs.multivar`. Levels `lvl.<course>.<slug>`, lessons `lsn.<course>.<slug>`, homework `set.<course>.<level-slug>`, topic exam `exm.calc.finals`. Libraries use the topic namespace `calc`: `skl.calc.*`, `mc.calc.*`, `gen.calc.*`, `drl.calc.*`, `crd.calc.*`, `gls.calc.*`. The `#` column is the lesson's number within 1.3; display numbers are computed from position and never stored. Six titles changed in draft 2 (lessons 19, 41, 46, 47, 86 and 87); titles are copy, ids are identity.

**Every lesson** runs the design research 8.3 sequence: a hook on a canvas, free manipulation, the name for what the learner just did, a ladder of 2 to 4 screens adding one constraint each, the trap, the rule stated as a description of the learner's own steps with at least one derived line the learner completes, a transfer screen where a true quant, statistics or ML context exists, the summary card, then a quiz of 3 to 5 items. From the owner's skills, every lesson also carries the "why this exists" frame, one common trap, at least one edge case, a worked example with a decision point where the naive route fails, and a motivated leap wherever a creative step appears (`derive` screens carry `leap: naive, fails, pointsTo`).

**Widgets** use the ids of widgets-calc-linalg.md sections 3 to 6 (`plot-2d`, `plot-3d`, `marks`, `drag-point`, `slider`, `stepper`, `value-table`, `live-formula`, `number-line`, `region-select`, `token-tray`, `sketch-pad`, `choice`, `numeric-input`, `expression-input`, `code-cell`, `sim-runner`, `matrix`, `derivation-steps` and the calculus composites of section 5), with `projection-lab`, `transform-grid` and `determinant-lab` from its linear algebra section, and `unit-circle`, `complex-plane`, `iteration-cobweb`, `sum-visualiser`, `card-sort` and `free-response` from widgets-stage0.md. Every id in section 2 was checked against the catalogues' headings on 6 October 2026. Where widgets-stage0.md 15.1 merges ids, its alias map applies. Section 7.5 lists the modes the sample lesson asks the catalogue to add.

**Quiz item types** (content-format.md 3.4 maps each letter to a check kind; the letters are shorthand in these documents and are never stored): N numeric, X expression, C single choice, M multi-select, O order the steps, S sort or match, I interval or solution set, P point or curve on a graph (curves graded by band and features), R region selection, A matrix entry, K code with tests. Long-form items appear in homework and exams, never in quizzes. Single and multiple choice stay at or below 40% of a course's quiz items (design research 10); the course totals in section 2 are within that bound.

**Mastery and feedback.** A lesson is Mastered at 75% of its quiz marks on the first completion (`assessment.md` decision 26): 3 of 4, 4 of 5. Every graded screen carries the content-format.md 3.3 feedback set: a `reason` line for a correct answer, `diagnose` lines tied to misconception ids, an `otherwise` line, the three-rung hint ladder (nudge, point, step), a Why? of at most three sentences with its representation (`why.rep`), and a solution line. Every task screen carries `tutor.focus` (content-format.md 3.10), and no tutor field may contain a value the screen accepts. Hint and Why? highlights name elements as `<widget id>/<kind>:<name>` (content-format.md 3.5).

**Bertie** follows ai-tutor.md 2.2: hints and Bertie's lines open after the lesson's first Check, Why? and "Explain another way" after a screen is resolved, nothing on homework until the set is submitted, and `away` app-wide during the finals-style paper and every timed paper. On a trap screen's reveal the lesson player plays his `surprised` one-shot (bertie.md 5.3). Lesson art is course art; Bertie appears only in his own slots (bertie.md 7), never as a character in lesson prose.

**Level review.** Every level ends with a Level review of 8 items at difficulty 2 to 4, interleaved, with up to 30% drawn from earlier levels of the same course (assessment.md 2.4). Homework follows (section 3).

**Columns.** Objective is one measurable sentence. Hook is the puzzle the lesson opens with. Trap is the misconception the trap screen is built around; Edge is the fringe case the lesson must show.

**Notation** (design research 9.4, extended): Leibniz $\frac{dy}{dx}$ and Lagrange $f'(x)$ both from lesson 10; $\ln$ for the natural logarithm; radians always; $h$ for a small increment in difference quotients, $\Delta x$ for a finite change, $dx$ only inside integrals and differentials; partial derivatives $f_x$ and $\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}$; vectors bold; the gradient $\nabla f$ as a column vector; the Hessian $H$; $\lvert \det J \rvert$ for the Jacobian factor.

## 1. Item overview

### 1.1 Purpose in a quant or ML career

- **Every model is fitted by calculus.** Maximum likelihood sets a derivative of a log-likelihood to zero; least squares sets two partial derivatives to zero; every neural network is trained by gradient descent on a loss whose gradient comes from the chain rule run backwards (backpropagation). A researcher who cannot differentiate a log-likelihood by hand, check a gradient numerically, or see why a learning rate diverges is not able to debug a model.
- **Risk is a derivative.** A position's delta is $\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}$, its gamma $\frac{\partial^2 V}{\partial S^2}$, a bond's duration and convexity the first two Taylor terms of price against yield. Hedging, P&L attribution and stress tests are Taylor expansions. Banks compute Greeks by bumping and re-valuing, which is a finite difference with a step size to choose (lesson 27).
- **Probability for continuous quantities is integration.** Densities, distribution functions, expectations, variances, joint densities, changes of variable with a Jacobian, and the normal distribution's constant $\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}$ (the Gaussian integral) are 1.3 content that 1.5 assumes from its first week.
- **Optimisation is the daily verb.** Portfolio construction is constrained optimisation (Lagrange multipliers, minimum variance); bet sizing is Kelly (maximise expected log growth); market making, execution and calibration minimise cost functions. Convexity, Jensen's inequality and volatility drag explain why arithmetic and geometric returns differ.
- **Interviews test it directly.** Quant interviews ask for $\frac{d}{dx}x^x$, $\int_0^\infty x^ne^{-x}dx$, $\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-x^2}dx$, whether $e^\pi$ or $\pi^e$ is larger, Lagrange problems and Taylor approximations; Zhou's green book has a calculus chapter [recalled: chapter 3, Calculus and Linear Algebra].
- **Continuous-time finance is built on it.** Continuous compounding, discount factors $e^{-\int r}$, mean reversion, the heat equation behind Black-Scholes, and Itô calculus in 3.2 all extend the single-variable and multivariable calculus of this item.

### 1.2 Learning outcomes

At the end of 1.3 the learner can:

| # | Outcome | Measured by |
|---|---|---|
| CL-O1 | Evaluate limits by algebra, the squeeze theorem and standard limits; prove simple limits from the $\varepsilon$-$\delta$ definition and disprove limits by negating it; apply the intermediate and extreme value theorems with their hypotheses checked | 85% on fresh difficulty-3 items; $\varepsilon$-$\delta$ proofs at 7 of 10 on the rubric |
| CL-O2 | Compute a derivative from first principles, state the exact error of a one-window estimate, decide differentiability (corners, cusps, vertical tangents, oscillation), and interpret a derivative as a rate with units | 85% on difficulty-3 items |
| CL-O3 | Differentiate any elementary function (powers, products, quotients, compositions, exponentials, logarithms, trigonometric, inverse trigonometric and hyperbolic functions) and curves given implicitly or parametrically | 90% on difficulty-3 differentiation items; Section A question A1 |
| CL-O4 | Prove the sum, product and chain rules; estimate derivatives by finite differences with the right step size; compute exact derivatives with dual numbers | Long-form at 7 of 10; homework D5 code drill passes |
| CL-O5 | Solve related-rates problems; approximate with linear and quadratic approximations, differentials and elasticities; run Newton's method and diagnose its failures | 85% on difficulty-3 items |
| CL-O6 | State and prove Rolle's theorem and the mean value theorem, and use them to prove monotonicity results and inequalities | Long-form at 7 of 10 |
| CL-O7 | Analyse and sketch graphs from sign charts of $f'$ and $f''$, asymptotes and growth rates; evaluate indeterminate forms with L'Hôpital's rule | 85% on difficulty-3 items; sketch items graded by features |
| CL-O8 | Model and solve optimisation problems in geometry, economics, finance and statistics (Kelly, minimum variance, least squares, the median, maximum likelihood) and justify that the optimum is global | 85% on difficulty-3 items; Section A question A4 at 7 of 10 |
| CL-O9 | Test convexity, apply Jensen's inequality, explain volatility drag and the value of convex payoffs, and state the stability condition of gradient descent | 85% on difficulty-3 items; written explanation at 7 of 10 |
| CL-O10 | Define the definite integral by Riemann sums, prove and apply both parts of the fundamental theorem, and differentiate integrals with variable limits | 85% on difficulty-3 items; FTC proof at 7 of 10 |
| CL-O11 | Integrate by substitution, parts, trigonometric identities and substitutions, and partial fractions, choosing the method, and recognise integrands with no elementary antiderivative | 85% on difficulty-3 items; Section A question A5 |
| CL-O12 | Decide convergence of improper integrals, compute probabilities and expectations from densities, and differentiate under the integral sign | 85% on difficulty-3 items |
| CL-O13 | Apply the trapezoid, midpoint and Simpson rules and Euler's method, and predict how their errors scale with the step | Homework I4 and I6 code drills pass; error-ratio items at 85% |
| CL-O14 | Compute areas between curves, volumes (discs, washers, shells, slices), arc lengths, polar areas, average values and work | 85% on difficulty-3 items |
| CL-O15 | Solve first-order separable and linear differential equations, including continuous compounding at a varying rate, logistic growth and mean reversion | 85% on difficulty-3 items |
| CL-O16 | Decide convergence of series with a justified test, distinguish absolute from conditional convergence, find radii and intervals of convergence, manipulate power series term by term, use asymptotics including Stirling's formula, and prove when an iteration $x_{n+1} = g(x_n)$ converges and how fast | 85% on difficulty-3 items; written test choices at 7 of 10 |
| CL-O17 | Build Taylor polynomials and series, bound the remainder, derive Euler's formula, and approximate price changes with duration, convexity, delta and gamma | 85% on difficulty-3 items |
| CL-O18 | Compute partial derivatives, tangent planes, gradients and directional derivatives; apply the multivariable chain rule, including with constrained variables; backpropagate through a small computation graph | 85% on difficulty-3 items; Section B question B1 |
| CL-O19 | Find and classify critical points with the Hessian, find global extrema on closed regions, and derive least squares and two-parameter maximum likelihood estimates | 85% on difficulty-3 items |
| CL-O20 | Solve constrained problems with Lagrange multipliers (one or two constraints, two or three variables) and interpret the multiplier as a shadow price | Section B question B2 at 7 of 10 |
| CL-O21 | Set up and evaluate double and triple integrals over general regions, swap the order of integration, change variables with the Jacobian (polar, cylindrical, spherical, linear), evaluate the Gaussian integral, and compute probabilities from joint densities | 85% on difficulty-3 items |
| CL-O22 | Pass the 1.3 exit | `exm.calc.finals` at 70% with Section A at 70%; a real 18.01 final at 70%; the 18.02SC Unit 2 problem sets done (section 4.3) |

### 1.3 Prerequisites

- **0.3 Precalculus complete**, especially level R5 (limits and continuity, lessons 142 to 146), R3 (oblique asymptotes and partial fractions, lessons 133 to 136), R4 (parametric curves), G1 to G5 (sequences and "tends to", $e$, the slope of $e^x$, logarithms, the growth hierarchy, series), T1 to T4 (trigonometry in radians), T5 (determinants), T6 (complex numbers), S8 (vectors and the dot product), C3 (binomial expansion), and R1 and R2 (proof).
- **0.2 Algebra**: functions, inverses, composition, transformations, factorising, completing the square, and in particular lessons 43 and 109 to 110 (rates, chords and areas), 72 (the tangent to a circle), 73 to 77 (optimising by completing the square) and 107 to 108 (iteration and bisection).
- **1.1 Python** for the code drills (functions, loops, floats); NumPy and matplotlib for the Calculus Lab.
- **1.2 Tools**: the Calculus Lab is a Git repository with pytest tests (curriculum 1.2 exit).
- **Alongside:** 1.7 Finance basics runs beside 1.3 to 1.6 (curriculum); the finance transfer screens are self-contained and do not assume 1.7 has reached them. The Daily 10 continues.

**Feeds.** 1.4 (least squares, quadratic forms, the Jacobian as a determinant), 1.5 (densities, expectations, joint densities, the Gaussian integral, series for the geometric and Poisson distributions, Stirling), 1.6 (maximum likelihood, least squares, the delta method through Taylor), 1.7 (continuous compounding, duration and convexity, the Greeks, Kelly, the yield by root-finding), 2.1 (gradient descent, backpropagation, convexity, Lagrange multipliers), 2.2 (mean reversion as the continuous twin of AR(1)), 3.2 (Taylor expansions, differential equations, the heat equation), 3.3 (interview calculus).

Solving time (everything except the 32.5 hours of watching and reading) is 167.5 hours, 84%, above the curriculum's 60% floor (rule 2). Rows marked optional, stretch or preview in section 5 are outside these hours. The content estimates CI sums for this topic (`content-format.md` 3.8: lesson minutes, level reviews, homework sets, the exit part's `estHours`, companion hours and the project and practice drills) come to 186.0 hours, 93% of the curriculum's 200, inside the 10% rule; the other 14 hours (generated practice and the Redo queue) are planned by `assessment.md` 8 rather than declared in content.

**Why the external block grew** (decisions 16 and 17). Draft 1 asked for about 290 OpenStax exercises inside 4.5 hours. Draft 2 counts every row: 72 targeted exercises at 7.5 minutes, the four 18.01SC unit exams and a timed 18.01 problem set as five calibrations against the real standard, and lectures at their real length. The 5 hours came from generated practice, which the planner can still extend from the Review block when the mastery model asks for it (`assessment.md` decision 9).
