## 2. Courses

Five courses, 25 levels, 137 lessons. Each level is 4 to 7 lessons, then a Level review (8 items) and a homework set (section 3).

### Course D: Limits and the Derivative (`crs.deriv`, 5 levels, 29 lessons)

The question that created calculus: how fast is something changing at one instant, when "speed equals distance over time" gives $\frac00$ at an instant? The course answers it with the limit of average rates, makes limits precise ($\varepsilon$-$\delta$), builds the derivative and every rule for computing it from pictures (growing squares and rectangles, stretch factors through a chain of maps, a point running round the unit circle), and ends by proving the rules and making a computer differentiate, numerically and exactly. It starts where 0.2 lesson 109 (gradients from shrinking chords) and 0.3 level R5 (limits and continuity) stop, and never repeats them (decision 3). Before the course: 3Blue1Brown, Essence of Calculus, chapters 1, 2 and 7.

**Level D1: Rates and limits** (`lvl.deriv.limits`, 7 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `lsn.deriv.instant-speed` Speed at an instant | Compute an instantaneous rate exactly as the limit of difference quotients by simplifying while the window is open, give the rate at every instant as a function of time, and state the exact error of a one-window reading | A ball dropped from a tower has fallen $5t^2$ metres after $t$ seconds. A radar gun averages its speed over the 0.1 seconds after $t = 2$ and reports 20.5 m/s. Is the true speed at $t = 2$ higher or lower, and by exactly how much is the gun off? (Lower, by 0.5 m/s) | `secant-tangent` (motion preset, signed $h$ slider, centred-window mode; section 7.5), `value-table`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: "a window centred on the instant gives the exact rate" (true for every quadratic, false for $t^3$, whose centred average at 1 is $3 + h^2$). Edge: the rate can be 0 at an instant without the object being at rest over any window (the top of a throw) | X, N, N, C, X (section 7) |
| 2 | `lsn.deriv.limit-toolkit` Limits by algebra, and why substitution works | Justify substitution by continuity, evaluate $\frac00$ and $\infty - \infty$ forms by cancelling or combining fractions, and use the limit laws for compositions and products only where their hypotheses hold (0.3 lesson 144 taught the techniques; this lesson adds the conditions) | At $x = 1$: $\frac{x^2 + 1}{x + 1}$, $\frac{x^2 - 1}{x - 1}$ and $\frac{1}{x - 1} - \frac{2}{x^2 - 1}$. Substitution works for one, gives $\frac00$ for one and $\infty - \infty$ for one. What licenses the first, and what are the other two limits? (1, 2 and $\frac12$) | `limit-explorer`, `derivation-steps`, `expression-input` | Trap: substituting the inner limit into a discontinuous outer function: $\lfloor 1 - x^2 \rfloor \to 0$ as $x \to 0$, not $\lfloor 1 \rfloor = 1$. Edge: $\lim_{x \to 0}\left(x \cdot \frac1x\right) = 1$ although $\lim_{x \to 0}\frac1x$ does not exist, so the product law, which needs both limits, says nothing there | N, N, X, C |
| 3 | `lsn.deriv.squeeze` The squeeze theorem and $\frac{\sin x}{x}$ | Prove $\lim_{x \to 0}\frac{\sin x}{x} = 1$ from the areas of a triangle, a sector and a larger triangle, deduce $\lim_{x \to 0}\frac{1 - \cos x}{x} = 0$, and squeeze limits such as $x^2\sin\frac1x$ | Shrink the angle $x$ on the unit circle: a small triangle, a sector and a large triangle stay nested, with areas $\frac12\sin x$, $\frac12x$ and $\frac12\tan x$. What does that force on $\frac{\sin x}{x}$? | `unit-circle` (triangle, sector and triangle overlays), `limit-explorer` (bounds mode), `derivation-steps` | Trap: "$\sin x \approx x$, so the limit is 1" offered as proof (it assumes the result); degrees in place of radians (the limit becomes $\frac{\pi}{180}$). Edge: $\sin\frac1x$ has no limit at 0, but $x\sin\frac1x$ has, because $\lvert x \rvert$ squeezes it | O, N, N, C |
| 4 | `lsn.deriv.epsilon-delta` The definition of a limit, played as a game | For a given $\varepsilon$, find a $\delta$ that keeps the graph inside the $\varepsilon$ band, and state the definition of $\lim_{x \to a}f(x) = L$ with its quantifiers in the right order | An adversary draws a band of half-width $\varepsilon = 0.1$ around $L = 7$ for $f(x) = 2x + 1$ at $x = 3$. How narrow must your strip round $x = 3$ be to keep the graph inside? Then $\varepsilon = 0.01$ | `epsilon-delta` (adversary rounds 0.5, 0.1, 0.01), `expression-input`, `token-tray` (build the definition from quantifier tiles) | Trap: the quantifiers swapped (one $\delta$ chosen first that must work for every $\varepsilon$). Edge: the definition never looks at $x = a$ itself ($0 < \lvert x - a \rvert < \delta$), which is why a hole does not matter | N, X, O, C |
| 5 | `lsn.deriv.epsilon-delta-proofs` Writing epsilon-delta proofs | Write $\varepsilon$-$\delta$ proofs for linear and quadratic limits, bounding the extra factor by first taking $\delta \le 1$, and prove a limit does not exist by negating the definition | Prove $\lim_{x \to 2}x^2 = 4$. In $\lvert x^2 - 4 \rvert = \lvert x - 2 \rvert\,\lvert x + 2 \rvert$ the second factor is not a constant. How do we tame it? | `epsilon-delta` (formula mode), `derivation-steps` (fill and order modes), `free-response` | Trap: a $\delta$ that depends on $x$ ($\delta = \frac{\varepsilon}{\lvert x + 2 \rvert}$); a proof written backwards from the conclusion. Edge: showing $\lim_{x \to 0}\sin\frac1x$ does not exist needs the negation: one $\varepsilon$ (say $\frac12$) that defeats every $\delta$ | O, X, O, M |
| 6 | `lsn.deriv.sequences-e` Limits of sequences, and why $e$ exists | Prove convergence from the $N$-$\varepsilon$ definition, apply the monotone convergence theorem, and prove that $\left(1 + \frac1n\right)^n$ increases and stays below 3 (0.3 lessons 43 and 47 found its value from tables) | 0.3 lesson 47 watched $\left(1 + \frac1n\right)^n$ creep up: 2, 2.25, 2.37, and 2.718 by $n = 10{,}000$. A table cannot rule out that it passes 3 at $n = 10^{100}$. Prove that it never does, and that it has a limit | `limit-explorer` (sequence mode), `epsilon-delta` (sequence mode), `derivation-steps` | Trap: "the steps shrink, so the sequence converges" (the partial sums of $\sum\frac1k$ rise by ever smaller steps and pass every bound). Edge: monotone convergence needs the real numbers: the truncations 1, 1.4, 1.41, 1.414, ... of $\sqrt2$ are rational, increasing and bounded, with no rational limit | N, C, O, M |
| 7 | `lsn.deriv.continuity-theorems` Continuity and the theorems it buys | Use the $\varepsilon$-$\delta$ definition of continuity, classify discontinuities (removable, jump, infinite, oscillating), and apply the extreme value theorem and the intermediate value theorem (0.3 lesson 146) with every hypothesis checked | What is the largest value of $f(x) = x$ on $[0, 1)$? On $[0, 1]$? A continuous function on a closed, bounded interval always has a largest value. Drop one hypothesis at a time and build a function with no largest value | `plot-2d` with `drag-point`, `card-sort` (match each counterexample to the hypothesis it breaks), `choice` | Trap: "continuous and bounded, so it has a maximum" ($\frac{x}{1 + x}$ on $[0, \infty)$ stays below 1 and never reaches it). Edge: the theorems say a point exists, not where, and their hypotheses are sufficient, not necessary ($\sin x$ has a maximum on all of $\mathbb R$) | C, M, N, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 1 turns the speed into the delta of a contract (the rate of change of its value with the share price); lesson 6's $e$ is the continuous-compounding factor of 1.7; lesson 7's extreme value theorem is why a best portfolio exists when the weights are confined to a closed, bounded set, and why "the best" can escape to the edge when they are not.

**Level D2: The derivative** (`lvl.deriv.derivative`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 8 | `lsn.deriv.tangent-line` The derivative at a point | Compute $f'(a) = \lim_{h \to 0}\frac{f(a + h) - f(a)}{h}$ from first principles for polynomials, $\frac1x$ and $\sqrt{x}$, and write the tangent line $y = f(a) + f'(a)(x - a)$ | Draw the tangent to $y = \frac1x$ at any point you like. With the axes it cuts off a triangle, and the triangle's area never changes. What is it? (2) | `secant-tangent` (signed $h$), `expression-input`, `numeric-input` | Trap: $f(a + h) - f(a)$ computed as $f(h)$, or as $f(a) + h - f(a)$; $\frac{1}{a + h} - \frac1a$ expanded without a common denominator. Edge: the form $\lim_{x \to a}\frac{f(x) - f(a)}{x - a}$ gives the same number; a tangent can cross the curve ($y = x^3$ at 0) | X, X, N, C |
| 9 | `lsn.deriv.local-linearity` Zooming in: differentiability | Decide differentiability by zooming and by one-sided difference quotients, prove that differentiable implies continuous, and recognise corners, cusps, vertical tangents and oscillation | Zoom in a million times on $x^2$ at 1, on $x^{2/3}$ at 0 and on $\sqrt[3]{x}$ at 0. Which turn into straight lines? (0.2 lesson 109 met the corner of $\lvert x \rvert$; these two fail in other ways) | `local-zoom`, `choice`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: "continuous, so differentiable"; "a tangent touches the curve at exactly one point". Edge: $x^{2/3}$ has a cusp at 0 (the one-sided slopes run off to $-\infty$ and $+\infty$) and $\sqrt[3]{x}$ a vertical tangent; $x^2\sin\frac1x$ (with value 0 at 0) wiggles infinitely often near 0 yet is differentiable there | C, M, O, C |
| 10 | `lsn.deriv.derivative-function` The derivative as a function | Sketch $f'$ from the graph of $f$ and $f$ from $f'$, use Leibniz and prime notation, and state the units of a derivative | Walk along the profile of a hill. Below it, plot the slope under your feet. Which features of the hill show up where? | `derivative-tracer`, `sketch-pad`, `choice` | Trap: $f'$ sketched as a copy of $f$ (peaks of $f$ drawn as peaks of $f'$ instead of zeros). Edge: $f$ and $f + 5$ have the same derivative, so $f'$ does not fix $f$; the units of $\frac{dy}{dx}$ are units of $y$ per unit of $x$ | P, P, C, S |
| 11 | `lsn.deriv.power-rule` The power rule, from a growing square | Derive $\frac{d}{dx}x^n = nx^{n-1}$ for whole $n$ from the binomial expansion of $(x + h)^n$, and use linearity to differentiate polynomials | A square of side $x$ grows by a thin $dx$. Which new pieces of area matter as $dx$ shrinks? Then a cube | `growth-rectangle`, `derivation-steps`, `expression-input` | Trap: $\frac{d}{dx}x^3 = 3x^3$ or $x^2$; the derivative of a constant taken as the constant. Edge: the $dx^2$ corner is not zero, it is small compared with the strips, which is the whole idea; linearity holds for sums but $(fg)' \ne f'g'$ (lesson 14) | X, X, N, C |
| 12 | `lsn.deriv.higher-derivatives` Second derivatives: acceleration and bending | Compute and interpret second and higher derivatives as acceleration, change of slope and bending, in both notations | Two cars both pass a camera at 30 m/s at noon. One is braking, one is speeding up. Which number tells them apart? | `derivative-tracer` ($f$, $f'$ and $f''$ panels), `numeric-input` | Trap: $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}$ read as $\left(\frac{dy}{dx}\right)^2$. Edge: $f''(0) = 0$ does not mean a change of bending ($x^4$); a polynomial of degree $n$ has $f^{(n+1)} = 0$ | N, X, C, M |
| 13 | `lsn.deriv.rates-in-context` Derivatives as rates, sensitivities and margins | Interpret $f'(a)$ in context with units, estimate $f(a + \Delta) \approx f(a) + f'(a)\Delta$ for small changes, and tell average from instantaneous rates in data | A firm's cost is $C(q)$ pounds for $q$ units and $C'(100) = 12$. Say in one sentence what the 12 means. Does the 101st unit cost about £12? | `secant-tangent`, `value-table`, `choice` | Trap: $f'(a)$ read as $f(a)$, or as the exact change over a whole unit. Edge: a derivative from data (prices by the minute) is always an average over a window; the window centred on the moment is the better estimate (lesson 27) | C, N, N, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 13 reads a position's delta as $\frac{dV}{dS}$ and marginal cost as $C'(q)$; lesson 12 reads gamma as the second derivative of value; lesson 9 notes that Brownian paths (3.2) are continuous everywhere and differentiable nowhere.

**Level D3: Rules of differentiation** (`lvl.deriv.rules`, 7 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 14 | `lsn.deriv.product-rule` The product rule, from a growing rectangle | Derive $(fg)' = f'g + fg'$ from a rectangle whose two sides both grow, and use it | Revenue is price times quantity. Price is rising 2% a day and quantity falling 1% a day. Is revenue rising or falling, and how fast? | `growth-rectangle` ($f$ by $g$ mode), `derivation-steps`, `expression-input` | Trap: $(fg)' = f'g'$. Edge: the corner piece $df\,dg$ vanishes faster than the two strips; relative rates add, $\frac{(fg)'}{fg} = \frac{f'}{f} + \frac{g'}{g}$ (here $+1\%$ a day) | X, X, N, O |
| 15 | `lsn.deriv.chain-rule` The chain rule: stretches multiply | Derive $(f \circ g)'(x) = f'(g(x))\,g'(x)$ from local stretch factors and apply it to composed functions | A nudge to $x$ is stretched by 3 when it passes through $g$, then by 5 when it passes through $f$. By how much is it stretched by $f(g(x))$? | `function-mapping` (chain mode), `expression-input`, `numeric-input` | Trap: $f'(g'(x))$, or $f'(x)g'(x)$ (the outer derivative evaluated at the wrong place). Edge: where $g'(x) = 0$ the composite is flat whatever $f$ does; a chain of three maps multiplies three stretches | N, X, X, C |
| 16 | `lsn.deriv.quotient-rule` Quotients and negative powers | Derive the quotient rule from the product and chain rules, and extend the power rule to negative integers | Average cost per unit is $\frac{C(q)}{q}$. When is it falling? | `derivation-steps`, `expression-input`, `secant-tangent` | Trap: the numerator in the wrong order ($\frac{fg' - f'g}{g^2}$); dividing the derivatives. Edge: the rule is often avoidable ($\frac{x^2 + 1}{x} = x + x^{-1}$); average cost falls exactly while marginal cost is below average cost | X, X, C, N |
| 17 | `lsn.deriv.combining-rules` Combining the rules | Differentiate expressions that need several rules in one pass by identifying the outermost operation first, and simplify before or after as convenient | $\frac{d}{dx}\sqrt{\frac{1 + x^2}{1 - x^2}}$: which rule do you apply first, and which last? | `token-tray` (order the rules on the expression tree), `expression-input`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: a product rule applied inside a chain with the inner derivative dropped; lost brackets. Edge: rewriting first can halve the work (logarithms, lesson 20); answers in a different but equal form are accepted by equivalence | X, X, O, X |
| 18 | `lsn.deriv.trig-derivatives` Derivatives of sine and cosine | Derive $\frac{d}{dx}\sin x = \cos x$ from the unit circle and from the limits of lesson 3, then the derivatives of $\cos$, $\tan$ and $\sec$ | A point runs round the unit circle at 1 radian per second. How fast is its height changing when it is at angle $\theta$? | `unit-circle` (velocity arrow), `derivative-tracer`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: $\frac{d}{dx}\cos x = \sin x$ (the sign). Edge: four derivatives bring $\sin$ back to itself; in degrees $\frac{d}{dx}\sin(x°) = \frac{\pi}{180}\cos(x°)$, which is why calculus uses radians | X, X, C, N |
| 19 | `lsn.deriv.exp-derivative` The exponential: slope proportional to height | Prove from the limit definition that $\frac{d}{dx}b^x = c_b\,b^x$ with $c_b = \lim_{h \to 0}\frac{b^h - 1}{h}$, show that $c_b = \ln b$, and prove that the solutions of $f' = kf$ are exactly $Ce^{kx}$ (0.3 lesson 48 found the multiples by drawing tangents) | Find every function that equals its own derivative. $e^x$ is one. Are there others, and how would you know you had them all? | `derivative-tracer` (base slider), `derivation-steps`, `expression-input` | Trap: "$e^x$ is the only one" (every $Ce^x$ works, including 0). Edge: the uniqueness proof differentiates $f(x)e^{-x}$ and uses "zero derivative on an interval means constant", which lesson 37 proves; $c_2 = \ln 2 \approx 0.693$ because $2^x = e^{x\ln 2}$ | X, X, C, N |
| 20 | `lsn.deriv.log-derivative` The logarithm and logarithmic differentiation | Derive $\frac{d}{dx}\ln x = \frac1x$ by inverting $e^x$, differentiate $\ln\lvert x \rvert$, and use logarithmic differentiation for products, powers and $x^x$ | $\frac{d}{dx}x^x$: the power rule says $x \cdot x^{x-1}$, the exponential rule says $x^x\ln x$. Is either right? | `function-mapping` (inverse mode), `derivative-tracer`, `expression-input` | Trap: the power or the exponential rule applied to $x^x$, where base and exponent both vary. Edge: the true answer $x^x(\ln x + 1)$ is the sum of the two wrong ones, a preview of the multivariable chain rule (lesson 112); $\frac{d}{dx}\ln\lvert x \rvert = \frac1x$ for $x < 0$ too | X, X, C, X |

Transfer screens: lesson 14's relative rates add, as daily returns approximately do; lesson 19 reads $e^{rt}$ as continuous compounding growing at rate $r$, and $f' = kf$ as the defining property of every exponential model; lesson 20 reads $\frac{d}{dt}\ln S(t) = \frac{S'(t)}{S(t)}$ as the instantaneous log return.

**Level D4: Inverses, implicit curves and related rates** (`lvl.deriv.implicit`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 21 | `lsn.deriv.inverse-derivatives` Inverse functions and inverse trigonometry | Derive $(f^{-1})'(y) = \frac{1}{f'(f^{-1}(y))}$ by reflecting tangent lines, and differentiate $\arcsin$, $\arccos$ and $\arctan$ | The tangent to $y = x^3$ at $(2, 8)$ has slope 12. What is the slope of the tangent to $y = \sqrt[3]{x}$ at $(8, 2)$? | `function-mapping` (inverse mode), `secant-tangent` (reflection overlay), `expression-input` | Trap: $(f^{-1})'(y) = \frac{1}{f'(y)}$, evaluated at the wrong point. Edge: where $f' = 0$ the inverse has a vertical tangent ($\sqrt[3]{x}$ at 0, $\arcsin$ at $\pm1$); the positive root in $\frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - x^2}}$ is fixed by $\arcsin$'s range | N, X, X, C |
| 22 | `lsn.deriv.implicit` Implicit differentiation | Differentiate an equation in $x$ and $y$ implicitly to find tangent slopes, and identify points where the slope is undefined or the curve crosses itself | Drag a point round the circle $x^2 + y^2 = 25$. The slope at $(3, 4)$ is $-\frac34$. Can you get that without solving for $y$? | `secant-tangent` (implicit curve), `expression-input`, `numeric-input` | Trap: $\frac{d}{dx}y^2$ written as $2y$, without the factor $\frac{dy}{dx}$. Edge: on the folium $x^3 + y^3 = 6xy$ the slope formula gives $\frac00$ at the origin, where the curve crosses itself | X, N, X, C |
| 23 | `lsn.deriv.parametric-derivative` Parametric curves: slope and speed | Compute $\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{dy/dt}{dx/dt}$ and the speed $\sqrt{\dot x^2 + \dot y^2}$, and find horizontal and vertical tangents | A wheel of radius 1 rolls at 1 m/s and a pebble in its tread traces a cycloid. How fast is the pebble moving at the top of the wheel? At the bottom? | `secant-tangent` (parametric), `slider` ($t$), `numeric-input` | Trap: $\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{dx/dt}{dy/dt}$. Edge: the pebble is momentarily at rest when it touches the ground (a cusp where $\dot x = \dot y = 0$) and moves at twice the wheel's speed at the top | N, N, X, C |
| 24 | `lsn.deriv.related-rates` Related rates | Write an equation linking quantities that change with time, differentiate it with respect to time, and solve for the unknown rate at the given instant | A 5 m ladder slides down a wall while its foot moves out at 0.5 m/s. How fast is the top falling when the foot is 3 m from the wall? (0.375 m/s) | `rates-scene` (ladder, cone and shadow presets), `numeric-input`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: substituting the instant's numbers before differentiating, so every rate becomes 0. Edge: as the foot nears 5 m the formula says the top falls infinitely fast; the model breaks (the ladder leaves the wall) | N, N, O, C |
| 25 | `lsn.deriv.hyperbolic-sigmoid` Hyperbolic functions, tanh and the sigmoid | Define $\sinh$, $\cosh$, $\tanh$ and the logistic sigmoid $\sigma(x) = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-x}}$ from $e^x$, derive $\sigma' = \sigma(1 - \sigma)$ and $\tanh' = 1 - \tanh^2$, and explain saturation | A hanging chain is not a parabola: its shape is $\cosh x$. And every artificial neuron squashes its input with an S-shaped curve. How steep is it at 0, and far from 0? | `derivative-tracer`, `plot-2d` with `slider` (input scale), `expression-input` | Trap: $\cosh' = -\sinh$, copying the trigonometric sign. Edge: for large $\lvert x \rvert$, $\sigma'(x) \approx 0$: a saturated neuron barely learns (the vanishing gradient of 2.1); $\sigma'$ is largest at 0, where it is $\frac14$ | X, X, N, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 25's sigmoid is logistic regression's link function (2.1) and the logistic growth curve of lesson 83; lesson 24's method prices how fast a quantity moves when a linked one is driven.

**Level D5: Derivatives computed and proved** (`lvl.deriv.machinery`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 26 | `lsn.deriv.rule-proofs` Proving the rules | Prove the sum, product and chain rules from the limit definition, including the chain-rule case where $g(x + h) = g(x)$ for small $h$ | Many books prove the chain rule by multiplying and dividing by $g(x + h) - g(x)$. Find a $g$ for which that divides by zero infinitely often near $x = 0$ | `derivation-steps` (find-the-flaw and fill modes), `free-response` | Trap: the divide-by-zero gap in the chain-rule proof (it bites for $g(x) = x^2\sin\frac1x$); a product-rule proof that uses $g(x + h) \to g(x)$ without citing lesson 9. Edge: writing $f(a + h) = f(a) + f'(a)h + \varepsilon(h)h$ with $\varepsilon(h) \to 0$ makes the chain-rule proof clean | O, O, C, M |
| 27 | `lsn.deriv.finite-differences` Derivatives from numbers: finite differences | Estimate derivatives with forward and central differences, show from an error table that the errors shrink like $h$ and $h^2$, and choose $h$ to balance truncation against round-off | A bank computes delta by bumping the price by $h$ and re-pricing. With $h = 10^{-12}$ the answer is worse than with $h = 10^{-5}$. How can a smaller bump be less accurate? | `value-table` and `plot-2d` (error against $h$ on log axes), `code-cell`, `numeric-input` | Trap: "smaller $h$ is always more accurate". Edge: in double precision the best $h$ is near $10^{-8}$ for the forward difference and near $10^{-5}$ for the central one (about $\sqrt{\epsilon}$ and $\sqrt[3]{\epsilon}$ with $\epsilon \approx 2.2 \times 10^{-16}$, times a factor set by the function) | N, C, K, N |
| 28 | `lsn.deriv.dual-numbers` Automatic differentiation with dual numbers | Compute exact derivatives by evaluating a function on dual numbers $a + b\varepsilon$ with $\varepsilon^2 = 0$, and explain why this is the chain rule carried out by the computer (forward mode) | Square $3 + \varepsilon$, where $\varepsilon$ is not zero but $\varepsilon^2 = 0$. Compare the $\varepsilon$ part with the derivative of $x^2$ at 3 | `token-tray` (expression tree with dual values on each node), `code-cell`, `numeric-input` | Trap: treating $\varepsilon$ as a small number to be ignored (it is carried exactly, so the derivative has no truncation error). Edge: forward mode needs one pass per input variable, which is why networks with millions of weights use reverse mode (lesson 116) | N, X, K, C |
| 29 | `lsn.deriv.piecewise-smoothness` Joining pieces smoothly | Choose parameters so a piecewise function is continuous and differentiable at a join, using one-sided derivatives | A road runs along $y = x$ up to $x = 1$ and then follows $y = ax^2 + b$. Choose $a$ and $b$ so a car never changes direction suddenly ($a = b = \frac12$) | `secant-tangent` (piecewise curve, one-sided tangents), `slider` ($a$, $b$), `numeric-input` | Trap: matching slopes but not values, or values but not slopes. Edge: even then the steering wheel must turn instantly at the join, because the bending $f''$ jumps from 0 to 1; railways use transition curves for that reason; $x^2\sin\frac1x$ is differentiable at 0 although $f'(x)$ has no limit there | N, N, C, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 27 is bump-and-revalue for Greeks and the gradient check every ML engineer runs; lesson 28 is what PyTorch and JAX do in forward mode; lesson 29's matching conditions build the splines used to interpolate yield curves (1.7, Stage 2).

**Course D quiz mix:** 117 items (lesson 1 has 5); choice and multi-select 34, 29%.

### Course U: Using the Derivative: Approximation, Shape and Optimisation (`crs.deriv-apps`, 5 levels, 24 lessons)

What a derivative is for. Near a point, every smooth function is almost a line (approximation, Newton's method, L'Hôpital); between two points, the mean value theorem ties average to instantaneous rates and turns derivative signs into facts about the function; across a domain, sign charts give the shape of a graph; and setting a derivative to zero finds the best box, price, bet, estimate and portfolio weight. The course ends with convexity, the property that makes optimisation safe and explains why volatility costs a compounding investor money. 0.2 level Q4 optimised quadratics by completing the square; this course handles every objective that trick cannot reach. Before the course: 3Blue1Brown, Essence of Calculus, chapters 7 and 10.

**Level U1: Approximation** (`lvl.deriv-apps.approx`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 30 | `lsn.deriv-apps.linear-approx` Linear approximation and differentials | Approximate $f(a + \Delta x)$ by $f(a) + f'(a)\Delta x$, predict the sign of the error from concavity, and use differentials $dy = f'(x)\,dx$ | Without a calculator: $\sqrt{4.1}$, $\sqrt[3]{8.06}$ and $1.01^{10}$ | `local-zoom` (tangent overlay), `numeric-input`, `choice` | Trap: using the tangent far from $a$ (estimating $\sqrt9$ from $a = 4$ gives 3.25). Edge: $(1 + x)^n \approx 1 + nx$ only while $nx$ is small: $1.01^{100} \approx 2.70$, not 2 | N, N, C, N |
| 31 | `lsn.deriv-apps.elasticity` Percentages, elasticity and error propagation | Use $d(\ln y) = \frac{dy}{y}$ to propagate relative errors through products and powers, and compute elasticities $\frac{d\ln y}{d\ln x}$ | A sphere's radius is measured with a 1% error. What is the percentage error in its volume? | `plot-2d` (log-log axes, where the slope is the elasticity), `numeric-input`, `expression-input` | Trap: a power's relative error taken as the base's (1% for $r^3$) instead of three times it. Edge: on log-log axes a power law $y = cx^k$ is a straight line of slope $k$: constant elasticity | N, N, X, C |
| 32 | `lsn.deriv-apps.quadratic-approx` Quadratic approximation | Approximate near $a$ by $f(a) + f'(a)\Delta + \frac12f''(a)\Delta^2$, derive the $\frac12$ by matching second derivatives, and use it where the linear term vanishes | A 10-year zero-coupon bond yielding 4%, continuously compounded, is priced at £67.03. It falls by £6.38 when its yield rises one point, and rises by £7.05 when its yield falls one point. Why the lopsided response? | `taylor-builder` (degree 2 only), `local-zoom`, `numeric-input` | Trap: the $\frac12$ omitted. Edge: at a minimum the linear term is 0 and the quadratic term carries everything, which is why optimisation (lesson 44) and gamma (lesson 105) live in second derivatives | N, X, N, C |
| 33 | `lsn.deriv-apps.newton-method` Newton's method | Derive $x_{n+1} = x_n - \frac{f(x_n)}{f'(x_n)}$ from the tangent line, run it by hand, observe that correct digits roughly double each step, and identify its failure modes | Find $\sqrt2$ to 11 decimal places in four steps, starting from 1 | `root-finder` (Newton mode, iterate table), `numeric-input`, `choice` | Trap: assuming Newton always converges (from 0, $x^3 - 2x + 2$ cycles between 0 and 1 for ever). Edge: for $\sqrt[3]{x}$ each step doubles the distance from the root; at a double root convergence slows to one digit at a time | N, N, C, M |
| 34 | `lsn.deriv-apps.lhopital` L'Hôpital's rule | Evaluate $\frac00$ and $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$ limits by the ratio of derivatives, justified by local linearity, and convert $0 \cdot \infty$, $\infty - \infty$, $1^\infty$, $0^0$ and $\infty^0$ forms | Zoom into $\frac{e^x - 1}{\sin x}$ at 0: top and bottom both look like straight lines through the origin. What is the ratio of their slopes? | `local-zoom` (ratio mode), `expression-input`, `numeric-input` | Trap: applying it to a form that is not indeterminate ($\lim_{x \to 0}\frac{x + 1}{x + 2} = \frac12$, but differentiating gives 1); differentiating the quotient with the quotient rule. Edge: the rule can fail to decide ($\frac{x + \sin x}{x}$ as $x \to \infty$: the ratio of derivatives oscillates, yet the limit is 1) | N, N, X, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 31's elasticity is a percentage sensitivity (a 1% move in a price produces a $k$% move in a value); lesson 32 introduces bond convexity; lesson 33 finds a bond's yield to maturity, the standard use of Newton's method in finance; lesson 34 orders growth rates for lesson 41.

**Level U2: Extremes and the mean value theorem** (`lvl.deriv-apps.mvt`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 35 | `lsn.deriv-apps.extreme-values` Critical points and the closed interval method | Prove Fermat's theorem (an interior extremum of a differentiable function has $f' = 0$), find critical points, and find global extrema on a closed interval from critical points and endpoints | Where on $[0, 3]$ is $x^3 - 3x$ largest? The derivative is zero only at $x = 1$, and that is a minimum. So where is the maximum? (At $x = 3$, value 18) | `objective-explorer` (interval mode), `sign-chart`, `numeric-input` | Trap: "$f'(c) = 0$, so $c$ is an extremum" ($x^3$ at 0); endpoints forgotten. Edge: an extremum where $f'$ does not exist ($\lvert x \rvert$ at 0) is still a critical point | N, N, M, C |
| 36 | `lsn.deriv-apps.rolle-mvt` Rolle's theorem and the mean value theorem | State Rolle's theorem and the mean value theorem with their hypotheses, prove the second from the first by tilting, and find the guaranteed point $c$ | Two average-speed cameras 10 km apart time a car at 5 minutes. The limit is 100 km/h. The driver says he never exceeded it. Prove that he did | `secant-tangent` (MVT mode), `derivation-steps`, `numeric-input` | Trap: a hypothesis dropped ($\lvert x \rvert$ on $[-1, 1]$ has equal end values and no point where $f' = 0$). Edge: the theorem promises a $c$, not a unique one or its location; for a quadratic $c$ is always the midpoint | N, P, O, C |
| 37 | `lsn.deriv-apps.mvt-consequences` What the derivative says about the function | Prove from the mean value theorem that $f' = 0$ on an interval forces $f$ constant, that $f' > 0$ forces $f$ increasing, and that equal derivatives differ by a constant; use $\lvert f(b) - f(a) \rvert \le M\lvert b - a \rvert$ | If two runners have equal speeds at every instant of a race, must they finish level? | `derivation-steps`, `secant-tangent`, `choice` | Trap: "$f'(c) > 0$ at one point, so $f$ is increasing near it" ($x + 2x^2\sin\frac1x$ has $f'(0) = 1$ and is not increasing on any interval round 0). Edge: the conclusions need an interval: $\frac1x$ and $\frac1x + \operatorname{sign}(x)$ share a derivative on $x \ne 0$ without differing by one constant | M, O, C, N |
| 38 | `lsn.deriv-apps.calculus-inequalities` Proving inequalities with derivatives | Prove inequalities such as $e^x \ge 1 + x$, $\ln(1 + x) \le x$ and $\sin x \le x$ for $x \ge 0$ from the sign of a difference function, and decide which of $e^\pi$ and $\pi^e$ is larger | Which is bigger, $e^\pi$ or $\pi^e$? Decide without evaluating either | `derivative-tracer` (the function $\frac{\ln x}{x}$), `derivation-steps`, `free-response` | Trap: checking values and calling it a proof; differentiating both sides of an inequality (a true inequality can have derivatives the other way round). Edge: $\frac{\ln x}{x}$ peaks at $x = e$, so $a^b = b^a$ with $a \ne b$ needs $a$ and $b$ on opposite sides of $e$, as in $2^4 = 4^2$ | C, O, O, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 36 separates average from instantaneous returns; lesson 37's bound $\lvert f(b) - f(a) \rvert \le M\lvert b - a \rvert$ is how numerical errors are controlled (canon-gap.md P3); lesson 38's $\ln(1 + r) \le r$ says a log return never exceeds the simple return.

**Level U3: The shape of a graph** (`lvl.deriv-apps.shape`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 39 | `lsn.deriv-apps.monotonicity` Increasing, decreasing and the first derivative test | Build a sign chart of $f'$ and classify critical points as local maxima, minima or neither | Mark where $f(x) = x^4 - 4x^3$ rises and falls using only the sign of $f'(x) = 4x^2(x - 3)$ | `sign-chart` (derivative rows), `derivative-tracer`, `region-select` | Trap: classifying by the value of $f$ instead of the sign change of $f'$; the double root of $f'$ at 0 read as a turning point. Edge: a critical point where $f'$ keeps its sign is neither a maximum nor a minimum | R, M, C, N |
| 40 | `lsn.deriv-apps.concavity` Concavity, inflection and the second derivative test | Determine concavity from the sign of $f''$, find inflection points, and use the second derivative test, knowing when it is silent | A news report says: "Unemployment is still rising, but more slowly." Which derivative is that about, and with what sign? | `derivative-tracer` ($f''$ panel), `sign-chart`, `choice` | Trap: an inflection wherever $f'' = 0$ ($x^4$ at 0 is not one). Edge: the test is silent when $f''(c) = 0$ ($x^4$, $-x^4$ and $x^3$ all have $f'(0) = f''(0) = 0$ and behave differently) | M, C, N, R |
| 41 | `lsn.deriv-apps.growth-ladder` Proving who wins: growth rates, indeterminate powers and asymptotes | Prove growth orderings ($\ln x \ll x^a \ll b^x \ll x! \ll x^x$) with L'Hôpital's rule (lesson 34), logarithms and ratios, use the ordering to settle indeterminate powers at infinity, and find oblique asymptotes of non-rational functions by expansion (0.3 lesson 58 ranked the ladder from tables; lesson 133 found asymptotes of rational functions) | As $x \to \infty$, does $x^{1/x}$ tend to 0, 1 or $\infty$? Its base grows without bound while its exponent shrinks to 0 (1) | `limit-explorer` (infinity mode, log axes), `token-tray` (order the ladder), `numeric-input` | Trap: "$\infty^0$ is always 1": $(2^x + 3^x)^{1/x} \to 3$. Edge: $xe^{1/x}$ is not rational, yet $xe^{1/x} - x \to 1$, so $y = x + 1$ is an oblique asymptote, found from $e^{1/x} \approx 1 + \frac1x$ | O, N, C, N |
| 42 | `lsn.deriv-apps.curve-sketching` Curve sketching | Sketch a function from its domain, intercepts, symmetry, asymptotes and the sign charts of $f'$ and $f''$, then check against a plot | Sketch $f(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2}$ before seeing it. Where is it highest, and why does it never exceed $\frac12$? | `sketch-pad` on `plot-2d` (graded by features), `sign-chart` | Trap: joining computed points with no regard to asymptotes or concavity. Edge: $xe^{-x}$ and $x\ln x$ need end behaviour ($x \to \infty$, $x \to 0^+$) that only L'Hôpital settles | P, P, N, C |
| 43 | `lsn.deriv-apps.graph-reading` Reading $f$, $f'$ and $f''$ together | Match graphs of a function, its derivative and its second derivative, and sketch $f$ from a graph of $f'$ | Three unlabelled curves: one is $f$, one $f'$, one $f''$. Which is which? | `derivative-tracer` (three linked panels, labels hidden), `card-sort`, `sketch-pad` | Trap: matching by similar shape rather than by "zeros of one are turning points of the next". Edge: a graph of $f'$ fixes $f$ only up to a vertical shift | S, P, C, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 41's ordering is why a normal tail, $e^{-x^2/2}$, makes a 6-standard-deviation day astronomically rarer than a power-law tail does (1.5, 2.2); lesson 40's "rising more slowly" reads P&L and economic charts; lesson 42 sketches the normal density's shape $e^{-x^2/2}$, whose inflections at $\pm1$ mark one standard deviation (1.5).

**Level U4: Optimisation** (`lvl.deriv-apps.optimise`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 44 | `lsn.deriv-apps.optimise-geometry` Optimisation: the open box | Model a geometric optimisation in one variable with its domain, solve $V'(x) = 0$, and justify the global maximum | Cut equal squares from the corners of a 30 cm by 30 cm sheet and fold up an open box. Which cut gives the biggest box? (5 cm, 2,000 cm³) | `objective-explorer` (box preset), `numeric-input`, `expression-input` | Trap: reporting a root of $V' = 0$ outside the domain ($x = 15$). Edge: the closed cylinder of least surface area for a fixed volume has height equal to its diameter, yet real cans are taller: the model left out the cost of the seams and lids | N, X, N, C |
| 45 | `lsn.deriv-apps.optimise-modelling` Modelling: the lifeguard problem | Translate a word problem into an objective of one variable with constraints, including problems whose optimum lies on a boundary | A lifeguard 20 m up the beach runs at 7 m/s and swims at 1.5 m/s. A swimmer is 10 m out and 40 m along. Where should she enter the water? (About 38.1 m along: 12.9 s, against 15.9 s on the straight line) | `objective-explorer` (lifeguard preset), `drag-point`, `numeric-input` | Trap: the straight line (shortest distance is not shortest time). Edge: the optimum satisfies $\frac{\sin\theta_1}{v_1} = \frac{\sin\theta_2}{v_2}$, Snell's law of refraction | N, P, C, X |
| 46 | `lsn.deriv-apps.marginal-analysis` Marginal analysis: price, elasticity and profit | Maximise profit where marginal revenue equals marginal cost, derive the markup rule $p = \frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon - 1}c$ for a constant demand elasticity $\varepsilon > 1$, and minimise average cost (0.2 lesson 73 maximised revenue for a straight-line demand by completing the square) | Sales fall 3% for every 1% rise in price, at every price, and each unit costs £6 to make. What price maximises profit? (£9) | `objective-explorer`, `plot-2d` (revenue and cost curves), `numeric-input` | Trap: maximising revenue when the question asks for profit (here revenue rises without limit as the price falls, so the revenue answer is "give it away"). Edge: with $\varepsilon \le 1$ profit rises with price for ever, so the model has no optimum; average cost is lowest exactly where marginal cost crosses it (lesson 16) | N, N, X, C |
| 47 | `lsn.deriv-apps.optimise-statistics` Fitting by optimisation: maximum likelihood, the mean and the median | Find maximum likelihood estimates by maximising log-likelihoods (a coin's bias, an exponential rate), recognise an optimum on the boundary, and minimise $\sum\lvert x_i - c \rvert$ by counting slopes (0.2 lesson 75 found the mean by completing the square) | A coin lands heads 7 times in 10 tosses. Which bias $p$ makes that result most likely, and can you prove it? (0.7) | `objective-explorer` (likelihood and log-likelihood curves; data mode with both loss curves), `numeric-input`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: an exponential's maximum likelihood rate taken as the mean waiting time (waits of 2, 3 and 5 minutes give $\hat\lambda = 0.3$ per minute, not $\frac{10}{3}$). Edge: 10 heads in 10 tosses gives the log-likelihood $10\ln p$, which rises all the way to $p = 1$: a maximum on the boundary, where the derivative is not 0 | N, N, X, C |
| 48 | `lsn.deriv-apps.optimise-finance` Optimisation in finance: Kelly and minimum variance | Maximise the expected log growth of repeated bets to derive the Kelly fraction, and minimise a two-asset portfolio's variance over the weight | A coin lands heads 60% of the time and pays evens. You may stake any fraction of your money on each toss, for ever. What fraction makes you richest in the long run? (20%) | `objective-explorer` (Kelly and two-asset presets), `sim-runner` (wealth paths), `numeric-input` | Trap: maximising expected wealth, which stakes everything and is ruined almost surely. Edge: at twice the Kelly fraction the growth rate is about zero (here slightly negative, $-0.0024$ per toss) and above it negative; with $\rho = 1$ and equal volatilities every weight is equally risky | N, N, X, C |
| 49 | `lsn.deriv-apps.global-checks` Is it really the best? | Justify global optima by the closed interval method, the single-critical-point argument, concavity, or limits at the ends of an open domain | $f(x) = x^3 - 3x$ has a local minimum at $x = 1$. Is it the lowest point on $(0, \infty)$? On the whole real line? | `objective-explorer` (open-domain mode), `choice`, `free-response` | Trap: a local minimum reported as global on an unbounded domain. Edge: on an interval, a lone critical point that is a local minimum is the global minimum; in two variables that fails (lesson 120) | C, M, O, N |

Transfer screens: lessons 47 and 48 are the quant uses themselves (1.6 maximum likelihood; 1.7 position sizing and portfolios); lesson 46's markup rule is the simplest version of setting a quote: the less price-sensitive the customer, the wider the margin.

**Level U5: Convexity** (`lvl.deriv-apps.convexity`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 50 | `lsn.deriv-apps.convex-functions` Convex functions | Define convexity by chords lying above the graph, prove the tests $f'' \ge 0$ and "every tangent lies below the graph", and show a local minimum of a convex function is global | Drag a chord along $e^x$, then $\ln x$, then $x^3$. Where does the chord always stay above the curve? | `convexity-chord`, `derivative-tracer`, `choice` | Trap: "convex means $f'' > 0$" ($x^4$ is convex with $f''(0) = 0$; $\lvert x \rvert$ is convex with no $f''$ at 0). Edge: sums and maxima of convex functions are convex, which is why squared and absolute losses are | M, C, N, S |
| 51 | `lsn.deriv-apps.jensen` Jensen's inequality | Prove $f(\lambda a + (1 - \lambda)b) \le \lambda f(a) + (1 - \lambda)f(b)$ for convex $f$, extend it to $E[f(X)] \ge f(E[X])$ for finitely many outcomes, and derive the AM-GM inequality | A share is equally likely to double or halve. Its expected change is $+25\%$. What is its expected log return? (0) | `convexity-chord` (two-point and $n$-point weights), `numeric-input`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: $E[f(X)] = f(E[X])$ ("plug in the average"). Edge: equality holds only when $X$ is constant or $f$ is linear on the values $X$ takes | N, C, O, N |
| 52 | `lsn.deriv-apps.convexity-finance` Convexity in finance: volatility drag, options and bonds | Explain with Jensen why long-run (geometric) growth falls short of the average (arithmetic) return by about $\frac{\sigma^2}{2}$, why a convex payoff gains from volatility, and why a convex bond price gains from yield moves | An index ends the year where it started, after daily moves with an annual volatility of 16%. A fund delivers three times each day's move. Up, flat or down for the year, and by roughly how much? (Down, by about 7%: its log growth is about $-3\sigma^2$) | `convexity-chord` (payoff preset), `sim-runner` (wealth paths), `numeric-input` | Trap: "three times flat is flat" (tripling each day's move triples the arithmetic return but multiplies the drag by nine). Edge: $\frac{\sigma^2}{2}$ is a second-order estimate and is poor for large swings: $+100\%$ then $-50\%$ averages $+25\%$ with zero growth | N, N, C, M |
| 53 | `lsn.deriv-apps.descent-1d` Descent in one dimension | Run gradient descent $x_{n+1} = x_n - \eta f'(x_n)$ on a quadratic, derive the stability condition $0 < \eta < \frac{2}{f''}$, and explain why convexity guarantees a unique minimum | Minimise $(x - 3)^2$ by stepping downhill. With step size 0.1 it creeps, with 0.9 it zigzags, with 1.1 it explodes. Why? | `descent-lab` (1D mode), `slider` ($\eta$), `numeric-input` | Trap: "a bigger step is always faster". Edge: $\eta = \frac{1}{f''}$ lands on the minimum of a quadratic in one step, which is Newton's method applied to $f'$ (lesson 33) | N, I, C, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 52 is volatility drag, the reason funds that multiply each day's move decay in volatile markets, and the reason long options are "long convexity" (1.5, 1.7); lesson 53 is the learning-rate choice behind every model in 2.1; lesson 50 explains why convex losses (least squares, logistic loss) have no false minima.

**Course U quiz mix:** 96 items; choice and multi-select 34, 35%.

### Course I: The Integral: Accumulation, Area and Probability (`crs.integral`, 6 levels, 32 lessons)

The second half of 18.01. A total is built from a rate by adding thin pieces (Riemann sums), and the fundamental theorem says that adding up rates and undoing a derivative are the same act. From there: the techniques of integration, improper integrals, probability densities and expectations (the bridge to 1.5), numerical integration for the many integrals with no formula, the geometric applications the 18.01 final tests, and first-order differential equations, where continuous compounding and mean reversion live. Before the course: 3Blue1Brown, Essence of Calculus, chapters 8 and 9.

**Level I1: Area and the definite integral** (`lvl.integral.area`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 54 | `lsn.integral.distance-from-speed` Distance from a speedometer | Approximate a total from a rate by left, right and midpoint sums, and say whether each over- or underestimates for a monotone rate (0.2 lessons 43 and 110 estimated areas with trapezia; rectangles give guaranteed bounds) | A car's speed rises throughout a minute. Its speedometer is read every 10 seconds: 0, 6, 11, 15, 18, 20, 21 m/s. What are the least and the greatest distances it can have covered? (700 m and 910 m) | `riemann-sum` (left, right, midpoint; data mode), `value-table`, `numeric-input` | Trap: adding the readings without multiplying by the 10-second step. Edge: for an increasing rate left sums underestimate and right sums overestimate, so the truth is bracketed; for a rate that rises then falls, neither is guaranteed | N, C, N, M |
| 55 | `lsn.integral.riemann-limit` The definite integral as a limit | Define $\int_a^b f(x)\,dx$ as the limit of Riemann sums, compute $\int_0^1 x^2\,dx = \frac13$ from the formula for $\sum k^2$, and say when the limit exists | With $n$ right-hand rectangles under $y = x^2$ on $[0, 1]$ the total area is $\frac{(n + 1)(2n + 1)}{6n^2}$. What happens as $n$ grows? | `riemann-sum` (symbolic sum readout), `derivation-steps`, `numeric-input` | Trap: $dx$ treated as decoration, or as a number to multiply by at the end. Edge: the function equal to 1 on rationals and 0 on irrationals has no Riemann integral on $[0, 1]$: sums at rational sample points give 1, at irrational ones 0 | N, X, O, C |
| 56 | `lsn.integral.integral-properties` Signed area and the rules of integrals | Use linearity, additivity over intervals, reversed limits, comparison and the bounds $m(b - a) \le \int_a^b f \le M(b - a)$, and interpret negative area | $\int_0^{2\pi}\sin x\,dx$: the curve encloses plenty of area. Why is the integral 0? | `riemann-sum` (signed mode), `accumulator`, `numeric-input` | Trap: integral taken as area even where $f < 0$. Edge: $\int_a^a f = 0$ and $\int_b^a f = -\int_a^b f$; displacement is $\int v$, distance travelled is $\int\lvert v \rvert$ | N, N, C, M |
| 57 | `lsn.integral.average-value` The average value of a function | Compute $\bar f = \frac{1}{b - a}\int_a^b f$, prove the mean value theorem for integrals, and tell a time average from an average of readings | Temperature over a day follows a curve. Find the level line whose rectangle has the same area as the region under the curve | `riemann-sum` (average mode with a draggable level), `numeric-input`, `choice` | Trap: averaging the end values $\frac{f(a) + f(b)}{2}$. Edge: $\sin$ averages 0 over a full period and $\frac{2}{\pi}$ over a half period; a time-weighted average price is an integral average | N, N, C, P |

Transfer screens: lesson 57 computes a time-weighted average price (TWAP), an execution benchmark (1.7); lesson 56 recovers total P&L from a P&L rate, and separates net from gross.

**Level I2: The fundamental theorem** (`lvl.integral.ftc`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 58 | `lsn.integral.accumulation` The accumulation function: the fundamental theorem, part 1 | Define $A(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$, show from a thin strip that $A'(x) = f(x)$ for continuous $f$, and read the shape of $A$ from $f$ | Drag $x$ to the right and the shaded area under $f$ grows. How fast is it growing at the moment the edge of the region is 3 units tall? | `accumulator` (FTC mode), `derivative-tracer`, `numeric-input` | Trap: the variable of integration $t$ confused with the limit $x$. Edge: $A$ increases where $f > 0$ and peaks where $f$ crosses from positive to negative | N, P, C, X |
| 59 | `lsn.integral.ftc-evaluate` The fundamental theorem, part 2: evaluating integrals | Prove $\int_a^b f = F(b) - F(a)$ for any antiderivative $F$ by adding small changes, and evaluate definite integrals | Add up the daily changes in a bank balance over a year. Why is the total just the final balance minus the opening one? | `accumulator`, `derivation-steps`, `expression-input` | Trap: $F(a) - F(b)$; applying the theorem across a discontinuity ($\int_{-1}^1\frac{1}{x^2}\,dx = -2$ is impossible for a positive integrand). Edge: the constant $C$ cancels, so any antiderivative works | N, N, O, C |
| 60 | `lsn.integral.antiderivatives` Antiderivatives and the constant | Find antiderivatives of the standard functions (powers including $x^{-1}$, exponentials, sine and cosine, $\frac{1}{1 + x^2}$, $\frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - x^2}}$), check by differentiating, and fix $C$ from a condition | A car's velocity is $3t^2 - 4$ m/s and it is at 5 m when $t = 0$. Where is it at $t = 2$? (At 5 m again) | `slope-field` (a family of antiderivatives), `expression-input`, `numeric-input` | Trap: $\int\frac1x\,dx = \frac{x^0}{0}$, or $\ln x$ without the absolute value. Edge: on a domain with a gap ($\frac1x$ on $x \ne 0$) the constant may differ on each side | X, X, N, C |
| 61 | `lsn.integral.variable-limits` Integrals with variable limits | Differentiate $\int_{a(x)}^{b(x)}f(t)\,dt$ with the chain rule, and move between a density and its cumulative distribution function | $G(x) = \int_0^{x^2}\sin t\,dt$. A friend says $G'(x) = \sin x^2$. What is missing? | `accumulator` (upper limit driven by $x^2$), `function-mapping`, `expression-input` | Trap: the factor $b'(x)$ forgotten, or the integrand differentiated. Edge: $\frac{d}{dx}\int_x^1 f = -f(x)$; a CDF $F$ and its density $f$ satisfy $F' = f$ | X, X, N, C |
| 62 | `lsn.integral.net-change` Net change: rates in, totals out | Recover a change from a rate with $\int_a^b r(t)\,dt$, distinguish net from total change, and compute quantities from flow rates | Water flows into a tank at $4 - t$ litres a minute for 6 minutes. How much more water is in it at the end? How much flowed in? (6 litres net; 8 in, 2 out) | `accumulator`, `riemann-sum` (signed), `numeric-input` | Trap: net change reported as total flow. Edge: if the tank empties part-way the model stops being true: an integral cannot see a floor | N, N, C, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 61 is the density-CDF link of 1.5; lesson 62 separates net P&L from turnover; lesson 59's telescoping sum is the same argument as cumulative returns adding up to the total log return.

**Level I3: Techniques of integration** (`lvl.integral.techniques`, 7 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 63 | `lsn.integral.substitution` Substitution: the chain rule backwards | Recognise $\int f(g(x))g'(x)\,dx$, substitute $u = g(x)$ with $du = g'(x)\,dx$, and check by differentiating | $\int 2x\cos(x^2)\,dx$: which function, differentiated by the chain rule, gives exactly this? | `function-mapping` (substitution mode: a $dx$ strip becomes a $du$ strip of the same area), `expression-input` | Trap: substituting without converting $dx$ ($\int\cos u\,dx$), or leaving an $x$ behind. Edge: $\int x\cos(x^2)\,dx$ needs a factor $\frac12$; $\int\cos(x^2)\,dx$ has no elementary antiderivative at all | X, X, C, X |
| 64 | `lsn.integral.substitution-definite` Definite substitution and symmetry | Change the limits with the substitution instead of returning to $x$, and use odd and even symmetry on symmetric intervals | $\int_{-3}^{3}x^5e^{x^2}\,dx$ looks hopeless. Look at the graph first | `function-mapping`, `plot-2d` (symmetry overlay), `numeric-input` | Trap: keeping the $x$ limits after changing to $u$. Edge: the symmetry shortcut needs each half to converge ($\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x\,dx$ is not 0; lesson 71) | N, N, C, X |
| 65 | `lsn.integral.by-parts` Integration by parts: the product rule backwards | Derive $\int u\,dv = uv - \int v\,du$ from the product rule and from a $uv$ rectangle, choose $u$ to simplify, and evaluate $\int xe^x$, $\int x\sin x$ and $\int\ln x$ | $\int xe^x\,dx$: substitution fails. Which product, differentiated, gives $xe^x$ plus something easy? | `plot-2d` with `marks` (the $uv$ rectangle split into two areas; widgets-calc-linalg.md section 8), `derivation-steps`, `expression-input` | Trap: choosing $u = e^x$, which makes the new integral harder ($\int\frac{x^2}{2}e^x\,dx$). Edge: $\int\ln x\,dx$ works with $dv = dx$: a factor of 1 is a choice you can make | X, X, C, O |
| 66 | `lsn.integral.reduction-formulas` Repeated parts and reduction formulas | Apply parts repeatedly, solve for an integral that comes back ($\int e^x\sin x\,dx$), and derive and use reduction formulas such as $I_n = \int_0^{\pi/2}\sin^n x\,dx = \frac{n - 1}{n}I_{n-2}$ | Integrate $\int e^x\sin x\,dx$ by parts twice and the integral you started with reappears. Dead end, or answer? | `derivation-steps` (fill mode), `value-table`, `expression-input` | Trap: going round in a circle (choosing $u$ the other way on the second pass undoes the first). Edge: the sine reduction formula gives Wallis's product for $\frac{\pi}{2}$ | X, X, O, N |
| 67 | `lsn.integral.trig-integrals` Trigonometric integrals and substitutions | Integrate powers of sine and cosine with identities, and use $x = a\sin\theta$ and $x = a\tan\theta$ for square roots of quadratics, including the area of a circle | Why is a circle's area $\pi r^2$? Evaluate $\int_{-r}^{r}2\sqrt{r^2 - x^2}\,dx$ | `derivation-steps`, `plot-2d` (the reference triangle for returning to $x$), `expression-input` | Trap: the factor $dx = a\cos\theta\,d\theta$ forgotten, or no return to $x$. Edge: $\int\sin^2x\,dx$ needs $\sin^2x = \frac{1 - \cos 2x}{2}$, not a substitution | X, X, N, C |
| 68 | `lsn.integral.partial-fractions-integrals` Rational functions: partial fractions | Integrate rational functions by long division and partial fractions with distinct linear, repeated linear and irreducible quadratic factors, building on 0.3 lessons 135 and 136 | The logistic model needs $\int\frac{1}{P(1 - P)}\,dP$. Split it into two easy pieces | `derivation-steps`, `expression-input` | Trap: decomposing an improper fraction without dividing first; a constant numerator over an irreducible quadratic where $Bx + C$ is needed. Edge: $\int\frac{dx}{x^2 + 1} = \arctan x$ while $\int\frac{dx}{x^2 - 1}$ is a pair of logarithms: one sign changes the family | X, X, C, X |
| 69 | `lsn.integral.integration-strategy` Choosing a method, and integrals with no formula | Choose between substitution, parts, identities and partial fractions from the form of the integrand, check every answer by differentiating, and recognise integrands with no elementary antiderivative | Sort ten integrals into piles by method. Three of them fit no pile | `card-sort`, `expression-input`, `choice` | Trap: forcing a method onto $e^{-x^2}$, $\frac{\sin x}{x}$ or $\sqrt{1 + x^3}$, which have no elementary antiderivatives. Edge: the normal CDF $\Phi(x) = \int_{-\infty}^{x}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-t^2/2}\,dt$ is defined by its integral and computed by series (lesson 103) or numerically (lesson 75) | S, X, X, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 68 integrates the logistic equation of lesson 83; lesson 69 introduces $\Phi$, the function every option-pricing formula calls.

**Level I4: Improper integrals, densities and numerical integration** (`lvl.integral.improper`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 70 | `lsn.integral.improper-infinite` Improper integrals over infinite ranges | Define $\int_a^\infty f$ as a limit, decide convergence of $\int_1^\infty x^{-p}\,dx$ and $\int_0^\infty e^{-cx}\,dx$, and show $\int_0^\infty x^ne^{-x}\,dx = n!$ | An endless region under $y = \frac{1}{x^2}$ to the right of $x = 1$: finite area or infinite? Under $\frac1x$? | `accumulator` (improper mode: push the upper limit out), `riemann-sum`, `choice` | Trap: "the curve goes to 0, so the area is finite" ($\frac1x$). Edge: $\int_0^\infty x^{t-1}e^{-x}\,dx$ defines $\Gamma(t)$ for non-integer $t$; $\Gamma(\frac12) = \sqrt\pi$ waits for lesson 133 | C, N, N, X |
| 71 | `lsn.integral.improper-unbounded` Unbounded integrands and comparison | Evaluate integrals with a vertical asymptote in the range, decide convergence by comparison and limit comparison, and treat $\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}$ as two separate limits | $\int_0^1\frac{dx}{\sqrt x}$: the curve shoots up to infinity at 0. Is the area infinite? (It is 2) | `accumulator` (lower limit pushed to 0), `limit-explorer`, `choice` | Trap: $\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x\,dx = 0$ "by symmetry" (it diverges, because each half must converge on its own); an asymptote inside the range missed ($\int_{-1}^1\frac{dx}{x^2}$). Edge: $x^{-p}$ converges on $[0, 1]$ for $p < 1$ and on $[1, \infty)$ for $p > 1$; $p = 1$ fails both | C, N, M, C |
| 72 | `lsn.integral.densities` Probability densities: area is probability | Use a density $f \ge 0$ with $\int f = 1$ to compute probabilities as areas, find normalising constants, and build the CDF by integration | A bus arrives at a random time in the next 10 minutes, more likely later than sooner, with density $f(t) = ct$. What is $c$, and the chance of waiting more than 5 minutes? ($\frac{1}{50}$ and $\frac34$) | `accumulator` (density mode with the linked CDF), `numeric-input`, `slider` | Trap: a density value read as a probability (densities can exceed 1). Edge: for a continuous variable $P(X = a) = 0$ for every single value; an exponential waiting time is memoryless | N, N, C, N |
| 73 | `lsn.integral.expectation-integrals` Expected values as integrals | Compute $E[X] = \int xf(x)\,dx$ and $\operatorname{Var}(X)$ for continuous variables, and prove the tail formula $E[X] = \int_0^\infty P(X > x)\,dx$ for $X \ge 0$ | A bulb's life is exponential with mean 1,000 hours. What is the chance it outlives its mean? ($e^{-1} \approx 0.368$) | `accumulator` (density mode with tail shading), `derivation-steps`, `numeric-input` | Trap: "it outlives the mean half the time" (that describes the median); $E[X^2] = (E[X])^2$. Edge: $f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2}$ on $[1, \infty)$ is a density with no mean, because $\int xf(x)\,dx$ diverges | N, N, O, C |
| 74 | `lsn.integral.differentiate-under-integral` Differentiating under the integral sign | Differentiate $I(a) = \int f(x, a)\,dx$ with respect to the parameter $a$, use it to evaluate integrals such as $\int_0^\infty x^ne^{-ax}\,dx$ and $\int_0^1\frac{x - 1}{\ln x}\,dx$, and read an option-pricing identity | $\int_0^\infty e^{-ax}\,dx = \frac1a$ for every $a > 0$. Differentiate both sides with respect to $a$. Which new integral did you just evaluate? | `derivation-steps`, `slider` ($a$) on `plot-2d`, `expression-input` | Trap: differentiating with respect to $x$ instead of the parameter. Edge: swapping derivative and integral needs a condition (an integrable bound on $\frac{\partial f}{\partial a}$) and can fail without it; the second strike derivative of a call price is the discounted density of the final share price (Breeden and Litzenberger, 1978) | X, N, C, X |
| 75 | `lsn.integral.numerical-integration` Numerical integration | Apply the trapezoid, midpoint and Simpson rules, and measure that doubling $n$ cuts their errors by about 4, 4 and 16 | $\int_0^1 e^{-x^2}\,dx$ has no formula. How many strips give 6 correct decimal places with each rule? (Trapezoid 351, midpoint 248, Simpson 12) | `riemann-sum` (trapezoid, midpoint and Simpson with an error inset), `code-cell`, `numeric-input` | Trap: Simpson's rule run with an odd number of strips; "more strips always help" without asking which rule. Edge: for convex $f$ the midpoint rule errs low by about half as much as the trapezoid errs high, which is where Simpson's weights $\frac{2M + T}{3}$ come from | N, N, K, C |

Transfer screens: lessons 72 and 73 are 1.5's continuous random variables; lesson 74's Breeden-Litzenberger identity reads market option prices as a probability density (1.7, Stage 2 project 3); lesson 75 previews quadrature in numerical methods (canon-gap.md P3).

Then between $x = y^2$ and $y = x - 2$, where vertical strips need two integrals and horizontal strips one (both $\frac92$) | `riemann-sum` (two-curve mode with a strip-direction toggle), `numeric-input`, `choice` | Trap: integrating top minus bottom when top and bottom swap part-way, without splitting. Where does the $\frac13$ in $\frac13\pi r^2h$ come from? | `solid-of-revolution` (disc and washer modes), `volume-slicer` (slices), `numeric-input` | Trap: a washer computed as $\pi(R - r)^2$ instead of $\pi(R^2 - r^2)$.
