Discs need $x$ in terms of $y$. Is there another way to slice? ($\frac{\pi}{6}$) | `solid-of-revolution` (shell mode with an unrolling animation), `numeric-input`, `choice` | Trap: the shell radius measured from the wrong axis (about $x = 2$ the radius is $2 - x$). Edge: both methods must agree, so computing one volume both ways is a check | N, N, C, X |
| 79 | `lsn.integral.arc-length` Arc length and surface area | Derive $L = \int\sqrt{1 + f'(x)^2}\,dx$ and its parametric form from Pythagoras on a tiny piece, compute lengths and surface areas of revolution, and meet Gabriel's horn | How long is a cable hanging in the shape $y = \cosh x$ between $x = -1$ and $x = 1$? ($2\sinh 1 \approx 2.350$) | `secant-tangent` (a polygon of chords converging on the curve), `numeric-input`, `expression-input` | Trap: $\int(1 + f'(x))\,dx$, adding instead of using Pythagoras. Edge: Gabriel's horn ($y = \frac1x$, $x \ge 1$, rotated) holds a finite volume $\pi$ of paint but has infinite surface area; most arc lengths have no closed form | N, X, N, C |
| 80 | `lsn.integral.polar-area` Polar coordinates and area | Plot polar curves, compute polar areas $\frac12\int r^2\,d\theta$ from thin sectors, and find where polar curves meet | The area inside the cardioid $r = 1 + \cos\theta$. Why do thin wedges suit this curve better than thin rectangles? ($\frac{3\pi}{2}$) | `plot-2d` (polar mode with a wedge sweep), `numeric-input`, `choice` | Trap: $\int r\,d\theta$, missing the $\frac12$ and the square; limits that trace a loop twice. Edge: polar curves can meet at the origin at different values of $\theta$, so solving $r_1 = r_2$ misses those meetings | N, N, C, M |
| 81 | `lsn.integral.work-centre-of-mass` Work, mass and centre of mass | Set up integrals for work (pumping, springs), mass from a density, and the centre of mass of a rod or a plate, by slicing, approximating and adding | How much work does it take to pump a full cylindrical tank of water (radius 1 m, depth 2 m) out over its top edge? ($19{,}600\pi \approx 61{,}600$ J with $g = 9.8$) | `volume-slicer` (slab mode), `derivation-steps`, `numeric-input` | Trap: one lifting distance used for every slice. Edge: a centre of mass is the expected position under the density (1.5); a plate's centre of mass need not lie on the plate (a crescent) | N, X, N, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 76's Gini coefficient; lesson 81's centre of mass as an expectation (1.5).

**Level I6: Differential equations** (`lvl.integral.odes`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 82 | `lsn.integral.ode-slope-fields` What a differential equation says | Read a first-order equation $y' = f(t, y)$ as a slope field, verify a proposed solution, and sketch solution curves including equilibria | $y' = y(1 - y)$: drop a ball on the field of arrows and follow them. Where does it end up from $y = 0.1$? From $y = 1.5$? | `slope-field`, `sketch-pad`, `choice` | Trap: a solution of the equation confused with a single number. Edge: under the usual conditions solution curves never cross; $y' = \sqrt{\lvert y \rvert}$ with $y(0) = 0$ has more than one solution because those conditions fail at $y = 0$ | P, C, M, N |
| 83 | `lsn.integral.separable` Separable equations: growth, decay and the logistic curve | Solve $\frac{dy}{dt} = g(t)h(y)$ by separating and integrating, including exponential growth and decay, continuous compounding at a varying rate, and the logistic equation | A balance earns interest continuously at a rate $r(t)$ that changes over time. What is the balance at time $t$? | `slope-field`, `derivation-steps`, `expression-input` | Trap: dividing by $h(y)$ and losing the equilibrium solutions ($y = 0$ for $y' = ky$); the constant put in the wrong place ($e^{kt} + C$). Edge: $B(t) = B(0)e^{\int_0^t r(s)\,ds}$, so the discount factor is $e^{-\int r}$ (1.7); logistic growth is fastest at half capacity | X, X, N, C |
| 84 | `lsn.integral.euler-method` Euler's method | Step through Euler's method by hand and in code, and observe that halving the step roughly halves the error (first order) | Step $y' = y$, $y(0) = 1$ to $t = 1$ with $h = 0.5$, then with $h = 0.1$. The answers are $1.5^2$ and $1.1^{10}$. Where have you seen these numbers before? | `slope-field` (Euler overlay), `value-table`, `code-cell` | Trap: using the slope at the end of the step, or forgetting to advance $t$. Edge: Euler on $y' = y$ is compounding $n$ times a year, and its limit is $e$ (lesson 6); too large a step on $y' = -10y$ makes the numbers explode | N, N, K, C |
| 85 | `lsn.integral.linear-first-order` Linear first-order equations and mean reversion | Solve $y' + p(t)y = q(t)$ with an integrating factor, and solve the mean-reversion equation $m' = \theta(\mu - m)$ for its long-run level and half-life | A spread between two prices is pulled back to its mean: $m'(t) = 2(5 - m)$, $m(0) = 9$. How long until it is halfway back? ($\frac{\ln 2}{2} \approx 0.347$) | `slope-field` (mean-reversion preset), `derivation-steps`, `numeric-input` | Trap: multiplying only one side by the integrating factor. Edge: the half-life $\frac{\ln 2}{\theta}$ does not depend on the starting gap; this is the mean of the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process and of the Vasicek short rate (3.2) | X, N, N, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 83's discount factor; lesson 85's pairs-trading spread (Stage 3 replication of Gatev, Goetzmann and Rouwenhorst); lesson 84's Euler step is the scheme used to simulate stochastic differential equations (3.2).

**Course I quiz mix:** 128 items; choice and multi-select 38, 30%.

### Course X: Series and Expansions (`crs.series`, 3 levels, 20 lessons)

When does adding infinitely many numbers give a number, and how do we replace a hard function by a polynomial with a known error? 0.3 level G5 summed the geometric and telescoping series; this course asks which infinite sums exist at all, and proves it. The course builds convergence from partial sums, gives each test a picture (rectangles under $\frac1x$ for the integral test, a ratio settling for the ratio test, a zigzag closing in for alternating series), and ends with Taylor series: the tool behind every approximation in quant finance, from duration and convexity to delta and gamma, and behind Euler's formula and the normal CDF. Before the course: 3Blue1Brown, Essence of Calculus, chapter 11.

**Level X1: Convergence** (`lvl.series.convergence`, 7 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 86 | `lsn.series.sequences` Iteration: when it converges, and how fast | Prove with the mean value theorem that $x_{n+1} = g(x_n)$ converges to a fixed point $L$ when $\lvert g' \rvert \le k < 1$ on an interval round $L$ that the iterates stay in, and tell linear from quadratic convergence by $g'(L)$ (0.2 lesson 107 drew cobwebs; 0.3 lesson 43 defined convergence) | Lesson 33's rule $x_{n+1} = \frac12\left(x_n + \frac{2}{x_n}\right)$ doubles its correct digits every step. The rule $x_{n+1} = 1 + \frac{1}{1 + x_n}$ also settles on $\sqrt2$, but gains under one digit a step: from 2, four steps give 3 correct decimal places. What sets the speed, and why do both converge at all? | `iteration-cobweb`, `value-table` (error column on a log scale), `derivation-steps` | Trap: "$\lvert g'(L) \rvert < 1$, so the iteration converges from any start" ($g(x) = x^2$ has $g'(0) = 0$ and runs away from $x_0 = 2$). Edge: $g'(L) = 0$ gives quadratic convergence (lesson 33's rule is Newton's method, with $g'(\sqrt2) = 0$); $-1 < g'(L) < 0$, as for the second rule ($g'(\sqrt2) \approx -0.17$), converges while overshooting | N, C, O, N |
| 87 | `lsn.series.partial-sums` What an infinite sum means | Define the sum of a series as the limit of its partial sums, use the $n$th-term test for divergence, and apply the algebra of series only to convergent series (0.3 lessons 62 to 65 summed geometric and telescoping series) | Let $S = 1 + 2 + 4 + 8 + \dots$. Then $2S = 2 + 4 + 8 + \dots = S - 1$, so $S = -1$. Every step looks like algebra. Which one is not allowed? | `series-explorer` (partial sums), `derivation-steps` (find-the-flaw mode), `numeric-input` | Trap: "the terms go to 0, so the series converges": $\sum\ln\frac{n + 1}{n}$ has terms that shrink to 0 and partial sums $\ln(n + 1)$ that grow without bound. Edge: brackets may be inserted into a convergent series without changing its sum, but not removed: $(1 - 1) + (1 - 1) + \dots$ converges to 0 while $1 - 1 + 1 - \dots$ has no sum | N, C, N, M |
| 88 | `lsn.series.integral-test` The harmonic series and the integral test | Prove $\sum\frac1n$ diverges by grouping and by comparison with $\int\frac{dx}{x}$, apply the integral test and the $p$-series rule, and bound tails by integrals | The terms $\frac1n$ shrink to 0. How many terms does it take for the sum to pass 10? (12,367) | `series-explorer` (rectangles over $\frac1x$), `accumulator`, `numeric-input` | Trap: "it grows so slowly it must level off". Edge: $H_n \approx \ln n + \gamma$ with $\gamma \approx 0.5772$; $\sum\frac{1}{n^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{6}$, but the integral test only bounds it | N, C, N, I |
| 89 | `lsn.series.comparison` Comparison and limit comparison | Decide convergence by comparing with a known series, directly or through the limit of the ratio of terms, and choose the benchmark | Does $\sum\frac{1}{n^2 + n + 1}$ converge? Does $\sum\frac{1}{2n - 1}$? Find a familiar series each one resembles | `series-explorer` (two series side by side), `choice` | Trap: comparing the wrong way (being smaller than a divergent series proves nothing). Edge: limit comparison needs positive terms; $\sum\frac{1}{n\ln n}$ diverges though its terms are smaller than $\frac1n$ | C, C, M, S |
| 90 | `lsn.series.ratio-root` The ratio and root tests | Apply the ratio and root tests, especially to factorials and powers, and recognise when they are silent | Does $\sum\frac{n^{10}}{2^n}$ converge? Does $\sum\frac{n!}{n^n}$? Watch the ratio of consecutive terms settle | `series-explorer` (ratio readout), `value-table`, `choice` | Trap: "the ratio is below 1 for every $n$, so it converges" (for the harmonic series $\frac{a_{n+1}}{a_n} = \frac{n}{n + 1} < 1$). Edge: both tests are silent, with limit 1, for every $p$-series | C, C, N, M |
| 91 | `lsn.series.alternating` Alternating series | Apply the alternating series test, bound the error by the first omitted term, and sum $1 - \frac12 + \frac13 - \dots = \ln 2$ | Pour in 1 litre, take out half a litre, pour in a third, take out a quarter, and so on. Does the level settle? | `series-explorer` (zigzag partial sums), `numeric-input` | Trap: applying the test when the terms do not decrease to 0. Edge: the error is smaller than the first omitted term and has its sign | N, N, C, M |
| 92 | `lsn.series.absolute-conditional` Absolute and conditional convergence | Distinguish absolute from conditional convergence, and see Riemann's rearrangement theorem: a conditionally convergent series can be reordered to any sum | Rearrange $1 - \frac12 + \frac13 - \frac14 + \dots$ as two positive terms then one negative. Does the sum change? (It becomes $\frac32\ln 2$) | `series-explorer` (rearrangement builder), `numeric-input`, `choice` | Trap: assuming the order of terms never matters in an infinite sum. Edge: absolutely convergent series can be reordered freely, which is why an expectation is required to satisfy $E\lvert X \rvert < \infty$ (1.5) | N, C, M, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 92 explains why 1.5 defines expectations only when $E\lvert X \rvert$ is finite; lesson 88's $\ln n$ growth is the cost of the coupon-collector problem (1.5); lesson 86's contraction condition is why fixed-point solvers for a yield or an equilibrium price converge or stall.

**Level X2: Power series and asymptotics** (`lvl.series.power`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 93 | `lsn.series.test-strategy` Choosing a convergence test | Choose and justify a test for a given series, and write the justification as a two-line argument | Sort twelve series into "converges" and "diverges", each with the test that proves it | `card-sort`, `free-response` | Trap: quoting a test without checking its conditions (positive terms, decreasing terms). Edge: some series need two steps, algebra first and then a comparison | S, M, C, O |
| 94 | `lsn.series.power-series` Power series and their radius of convergence | Find the radius and interval of convergence of a power series with the ratio test, checking the endpoints separately | $\sum\frac{x^n}{n}$: move $x$ and watch the partial sums. Where do they settle, and where do they explode? | `series-explorer` (power mode, $x$ slider), `number-line` (interval), `numeric-input` | Trap: endpoints decided by the ratio test, which is silent there. Edge: $\sum\frac{x^n}{n}$ converges at $x = -1$ and diverges at $x = 1$; the set of convergence is always an interval centred on the centre | I, N, C, I |
| 95 | `lsn.series.power-series-calculus` Calculus with power series | Differentiate and integrate power series term by term inside the radius, and derive series for $\ln(1 + x)$, $\arctan x$ and $\frac{1}{(1 - x)^2}$ (the last is 0.3 lesson 65's $\sum kr^{k-1}$, now read as a derivative) | Find the power series of $\ln(1 + x)$ without differentiating $\ln$ ten times. What is $\frac{d}{dx}\ln(1 + x)$, and do you already know its series? | `series-explorer`, `derivation-steps`, `expression-input` | Trap: using the new formula outside the radius ($\sum n2^{n-1}$ "equals" $\frac{1}{(1 - 2)^2} = 1$). Edge: integration can gain an endpoint: $\arctan x$'s series converges at $x = 1$ and gives $\frac{\pi}{4} = 1 - \frac13 + \frac15 - \dots$ | X, X, N, C |
| 96 | `lsn.series.radius-singularities` Why the radius is what it is | Predict the radius of convergence of a power series from the distance to the nearest singularity in the complex plane | $\frac{1}{1 + x^2}$ is smooth on the whole real line, yet its series $1 - x^2 + x^4 - \dots$ fails beyond $\lvert x \rvert = 1$. What stops it? | `series-explorer` (power mode), `complex-plane` (disc of convergence with the singularities at $\pm i$), `numeric-input` | Trap: "smooth everywhere, so the series converges everywhere". Edge: the series of $\frac{1}{1 + x^2}$ centred at $x = 1$ has radius $\sqrt2$, the distance from 1 to $i$ | N, C, N, M |
| 97 | `lsn.series.series-probability` Series in probability | Compute expectations and probabilities with series: the mean of the geometric distribution, the normalisation and mean of the Poisson distribution, and the St Petersburg game whose expectation diverges | A casino pays £$2^k$ if a fair coin first shows heads on toss $k$. What is a fair price to play? Would you pay £100? | `series-explorer`, `sim-runner` (sample means that keep drifting up), `numeric-input` | Trap: a finite simulated average taken as proof that the expectation exists (St Petersburg sample means keep growing). Edge: $\sum\frac{\lambda^k}{k!} = e^\lambda$ is why Poisson probabilities add to 1 | N, N, C, N |
| 98 | `lsn.series.asymptotics-stirling` Asymptotics and Stirling's formula | Use $\sim$, big-O and little-o, bound $\ln n!$ between integrals of $\ln x$ to get $\ln n! = n\ln n - n + O(\ln n)$, and use Stirling's formula $n! \sim \sqrt{2\pi n}\left(\frac{n}{e}\right)^n$ | Without a calculator, roughly how many digits does $100!$ have? (158) | `riemann-sum` (rectangles of $\ln k$ against the curve $\ln x$), `value-table`, `numeric-input` | Trap: $\sim$ read as "close in absolute terms" ($n!$ and Stirling's estimate drift apart in absolute size; only their ratio tends to 1). Edge: $\binom{2n}{n} \sim \frac{4^n}{\sqrt{\pi n}}$, so the chance of exactly $n$ heads in $2n$ tosses shrinks like $\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi n}}$ (1.5) | N, N, C, X |

Transfer screens: lesson 97 is 1.5's geometric and Poisson distributions and the classic interview question on St Petersburg; lesson 98's central binomial estimate gives random-walk return probabilities (1.5).

**Level X3: Taylor series** (`lvl.series.taylor`, 7 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 99 | `lsn.series.taylor-polynomials` Taylor polynomials by matching derivatives | Build the degree-$n$ Taylor polynomial of $f$ at $a$ by matching $f$ and its first $n$ derivatives, and explain the $\frac{1}{k!}$ | Set four coefficients by hand so a cubic hugs $e^x$ at 0 as closely as possible. Which coefficients win? | `taylor-builder` (match mode), `numeric-input`, `expression-input` | Trap: coefficients $f^{(k)}(a)$ without the $\frac{1}{k!}$; powers of $x$ instead of $(x - a)$ when $a \ne 0$. Edge: a polynomial's Taylor polynomial of high enough degree is the polynomial itself, rewritten round $a$ | N, X, X, C |
| 100 | `lsn.series.taylor-remainder` Taylor's theorem and the remainder | State Taylor's theorem with the Lagrange remainder, bound the error of an approximation, and find how many terms give a required accuracy | How many terms of $1 + 1 + \frac{1}{2!} + \frac{1}{3!} + \dots$ give $e$ to within $10^{-6}$? Prove it, rather than observe it (10 terms) | `taylor-builder` (remainder band), `value-table`, `numeric-input` | Trap: bounding the remainder at the centre instead of at the worst point between $a$ and $x$. Edge: $e^{-1/x^2}$ (with value 0 at 0) has every derivative 0 at 0, so its Taylor series is 0: it converges everywhere, to the wrong function except at 0 | N, N, C, M |
| 101 | `lsn.series.standard-series` The standard series | Derive and recognise the Maclaurin series of $e^x$, $\sin x$, $\cos x$, $\ln(1 + x)$, $\frac{1}{1 - x}$ and $(1 + x)^\alpha$, with their radii | Build $\sin x$ from its derivatives at 0: they cycle $0, 1, 0, -1$. Which powers survive, and with which signs? | `taylor-builder`, `series-explorer`, `expression-input` | Trap: $\ln(1 + x)$'s series used with $\lvert x \rvert > 1$; the alternating signs of the sine series. Edge: $(1 + x)^\alpha$ never stops for non-integer $\alpha$ (0.3's binomial series, now proved) and is a polynomial for whole $\alpha$ | X, X, I, C |
| 102 | `lsn.series.series-algebra` New series from old | Build series by substitution, multiplication, division and composition ($e^{-x^2}$, $e^x\sin x$, $\tan x$) instead of differentiating repeatedly | Find the series of $e^{-x^2}$. Differentiating it ten times is miserable. Is there a shortcut? | `taylor-builder`, `derivation-steps`, `expression-input` | Trap: substituting into a series and forgetting the new radius. Edge: the series of $\tan x$, found by dividing $\sin$ by $\cos$, has radius $\frac{\pi}{2}$, the distance to $\cos$'s first zero | X, X, N, C |
| 103 | `lsn.series.using-series` Using series: limits, integrals and approximations | Evaluate limits by expansion, integrate non-elementary integrands term by term ($\int_0^1e^{-x^2}\,dx$, the normal CDF), and approximate with error bounds | $\lim_{x \to 0}\frac{\sin x - x}{x^3}$: L'Hôpital three times, or one line of series? ($-\frac16$) | `taylor-builder`, `derivation-steps`, `numeric-input` | Trap: truncating too early, so the answer cancels back to $\frac00$. Edge: the series for $\Phi(x)$ converges for every $x$, but at $x = 5$ it needs many terms with heavy cancellation, so tail approximations are used there | N, N, X, C |
| 104 | `lsn.series.euler-formula` Euler's formula | Derive $e^{i\theta} = \cos\theta + i\sin\theta$ from the series and use it for $e^{i\pi} = -1$, de Moivre's theorem and trigonometric identities | Add the terms $1, i\theta, \frac{(i\theta)^2}{2!}, \dots$ as arrows, tip to tail. Where do they spiral to? | `series-explorer` (complex mode), `complex-plane`, `numeric-input` | Trap: $i^2$ treated as 1 when splitting real and imaginary parts. Edge: $\cos\theta = \frac{e^{i\theta} + e^{-i\theta}}{2}$, so $\cosh x = \cos(ix)$; characteristic functions in probability are $E[e^{itX}]$ | P, X, N, C |
| 105 | `lsn.series.finance-expansions` Taylor in finance: duration, convexity, delta and gamma | Approximate price changes by first- and second-order Taylor expansions (duration and convexity for bonds, delta and gamma for options), and judge when the second term matters | An option is worth £5.00 with the share at £100, delta 0.6 and gamma 0.05. Estimate its value if the share jumps to £110 (£13.50: £6 from delta, £2.50 from gamma) | `taylor-builder` (bond and option presets), `numeric-input`, `choice` | Trap: duration alone for a large move (the miss is the convexity term). Edge: a delta-hedged position earns about $\frac12\Gamma(\Delta S)^2$ minus its time decay, so long gamma gains from moves either way (1.7, 3.2) | N, N, C, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 103 computes the normal CDF that 1.5 and 1.7 call; lesson 105 is the language of every risk report; lesson 102's composition of series is how 1.6 expands a function of an estimator (the delta method).

**Course X quiz mix:** 80 items; choice and multi-select 31, 39%.

### Course M: Many Variables: Gradients, Constraints and Multiple Integrals (`crs.multivar`, 6 levels, 32 lessons)

The 18.02 material the curriculum names, and the calculus of machine learning. Functions of two or more variables are read as landscapes and contour maps; slicing gives partial derivatives, the gradient points uphill, and the chain rule run backwards through a graph is backpropagation. Critical points are classified by the Hessian, constrained problems are solved by Lagrange multipliers (with the multiplier read as a price), and double and triple integrals, with the Jacobian, turn joint densities into probabilities and give the Gaussian integral. Before the course: Khan Academy Multivariable calculus, the first unit (taught by Grant Sanderson of 3Blue1Brown) [check unit title].

**Level M1: Functions of several variables** (`lvl.multivar.surfaces`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 106 | `lsn.multivar.surfaces-contours` Surfaces and contour maps | Read functions of two variables as surfaces and as contour maps, sketch level curves, and locate peaks, pits and passes from contours alone | Find the summit of a hill using only its contour map. Then check in 3D | `surface-explorer`, `drag-point` on the contour map, `choice` | Trap: closely spaced contours read as low ground (they mean steep ground). Edge: at a pass (saddle) the contour at the pass's own height crosses itself, a level that is neither peak nor pit | P, C, M, N |
| 107 | `lsn.multivar.space-lines-planes` Coordinates, lines and planes in three dimensions | Use 3D coordinates and vectors, compute a normal with the cross product, and write a plane as $\mathbf{n}\cdot(\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}_0) = 0$ or $z = z_0 + a(x - x_0) + b(y - y_0)$, and a line in parametric form | Three points on a sloping roof. Which single equation describes the roof? | `plot-3d` (plane through three points with its normal), `projection-lab` (3D dot product), `expression-input` | Trap: the normal vector read as a direction lying in the plane. Edge: the coefficients $a$ and $b$ are the plane's slopes along $x$ and along $y$, the partial derivatives of lesson 109; a vertical plane cannot be written as $z = \dots$ | X, X, N, C |
| 108 | `lsn.multivar.limits-2d` Limits and continuity in two variables | Show a two-variable limit fails by finding two paths with different limits, and prove a limit exists by bounding in terms of $r = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$ | $f(x, y) = \frac{xy}{x^2 + y^2}$: walk towards the origin along the $x$-axis, then along $y = x$. What do you see? | `surface-explorer` (path mode), `limit-explorer` (path readout), `choice` | Trap: checking only straight lines ($\frac{x^2y}{x^4 + y^2}$ tends to 0 along every line through the origin but is $\frac12$ along $y = x^2$). Edge: bounds such as $\left\lvert\frac{x^2y}{x^2 + y^2}\right\rvert \le \lvert y \rvert$ prove a limit is 0 | C, N, M, C |
| 109 | `lsn.multivar.partial-derivatives` Partial derivatives | Compute $f_x$ and $f_y$ by holding the other variable fixed, read them as slopes of slices, and interpret them with units | Slice a hill east-west through where you stand, then north-south. How steep is each slice at your feet? | `surface-explorer` (slices), `expression-input`, `numeric-input` | Trap: treating the other variable as a function of $x$ (differentiating $y$ into $y'$). Edge: both partial derivatives exist at the origin for lesson 108's $\frac{xy}{x^2 + y^2}$ (with value 0 there), which is not even continuous there | X, X, N, C |
| 110 | `lsn.multivar.tangent-plane` Tangent planes, linear approximation and differentiability | Write the tangent plane $z = f(a, b) + f_x(a, b)(x - a) + f_y(a, b)(y - b)$, approximate with the total differential $df = f_x\,dx + f_y\,dy$, and state what differentiability requires | Zoom in on a smooth surface until it looks flat. Which plane does it look like? | `surface-explorer` (tangent-plane toggle and zoom), `expression-input`, `numeric-input` | Trap: "both partials exist, so the tangent plane approximates well". Edge: if $f_x$ and $f_y$ exist and are continuous near the point, $f$ is differentiable there | X, N, N, C |
| 111 | `lsn.multivar.second-partials` Second partial derivatives | Compute second and mixed partials, use Clairaut's theorem $f_{xy} = f_{yx}$ with its continuity condition, assemble the Hessian, and check a solution of the heat equation | Does the order of slicing matter for a rate of change of a rate of change? Compute $f_{xy}$ and $f_{yx}$ for $x^3y^2 + e^{xy}$ | `surface-explorer`, `matrix` (Hessian), `expression-input` | Trap: $f_{xy}$ read as the product $f_xf_y$. Edge: for $f = \frac{xy(x^2 - y^2)}{x^2 + y^2}$ (value 0 at the origin), $f_{xy}(0, 0) = -1$ and $f_{yx}(0, 0) = 1$, because the mixed partials are not continuous there | X, X, A, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 106 draws a machine-learning loss landscape over two weights; lesson 111 checks that the heat kernel solves $u_t = u_{xx}$, the equation Black-Scholes becomes after a change of variables (3.2).

**Level M2: The chain rule and the gradient** (`lvl.multivar.gradient`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 112 | `lsn.multivar.chain-rule-multi` The multivariable chain rule | Apply $\frac{dz}{dt} = f_x\frac{dx}{dt} + f_y\frac{dy}{dt}$ and its general forms by drawing a dependency tree and adding one product per path | You walk across a hill along a path $x(t)$, $y(t)$. How fast is your height changing? Build the tree and follow every branch | `dependency-graph`, `expression-input`, `numeric-input` | Trap: following only one path through the tree. Edge: lesson 20's $\frac{d}{dx}x^x = x^x\ln x + x \cdot x^{x-1}$ is two paths, one through the exponent and one through the base | X, N, S, X |
| 113 | `lsn.multivar.constrained-partials` Implicit functions and constrained partial derivatives | Differentiate implicitly with $\frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{F_x}{F_y}$, and compute partial derivatives when variables are linked, stating which variable is held fixed | For an ideal gas $PV = nRT$, compute $\frac{\partial P}{\partial V}\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\frac{\partial T}{\partial P}$. Is it 1, as cancelling suggests? ($-1$) | `dependency-graph` (held-fixed toggles), `expression-input`, `choice` | Trap: cancelling $\partial$s like fractions. Edge: $\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)_y$ and $\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)_z$ can differ at the same point, so naming the held variable is not pedantry (18.02's non-independent variables) | N, X, C, X |
| 114 | `lsn.multivar.directional-gradient` Directional derivatives and the gradient | Compute $D_{\mathbf u}f = \nabla f\cdot\mathbf u$, show that $\nabla f$ points in the direction of steepest ascent with rate $\lvert\nabla f\rvert$, and find the level directions | Turn a compass needle on a hillside. Which way is steepest up, how steep is it, and which way is level? | `gradient-compass`, `surface-explorer`, `numeric-input` | Trap: a direction vector that is not a unit vector. Edge: at a critical point every directional derivative is 0; all directional derivatives can exist without $f$ being differentiable | N, N, X, C |
| 115 | `lsn.multivar.gradient-normal` The gradient is perpendicular to level sets | Prove $\nabla f$ is perpendicular to level curves and surfaces, and use it for tangent lines to level curves and tangent planes to level surfaces | Draw gradient arrows on a contour map. At what angle do they cross the contours? | `gradient-compass` (contour overlay), `surface-explorer`, `expression-input` | Trap: drawing the gradient along the contour. Edge: the gradient lives in the input plane, not on the surface; for a surface $F(x, y, z) = c$, $\nabla F$ is the normal | P, X, C, X |
| 116 | `lsn.multivar.backprop` Backpropagation: the chain rule on a graph | Compute the gradient of a loss through a small computation graph with a forward pass and a backward pass (reverse mode), and verify it with a finite-difference check | One neuron: $\hat y = \sigma(wx + b)$ with loss $(\hat y - t)^2$. Nudge $w$: how much does the loss move? Pass the blame backwards node by node | `dependency-graph` (backprop mode), `code-cell`, `numeric-input` | Trap: forgetting to add the gradients where one variable feeds two nodes. Edge: reverse mode costs about one extra pass whatever the number of inputs, which is why it trains networks with billions of weights; forward mode (lesson 28) would need one pass per weight | N, N, K, C |
| 117 | `lsn.multivar.gradient-descent` Gradient descent in two dimensions | Run gradient descent on a quadratic bowl, choose a learning rate, and explain the zigzag in a long narrow valley | Roll a ball down $f(x, y) = x^2 + 10y^2$ by repeated steps against the gradient. Which learning rate reaches the bottom fastest without blowing up? (Stable below 0.1; fastest near $\frac{2}{22} \approx 0.091$) | `descent-lab`, `slider` (learning rate), `numeric-input` | Trap: "steepest descent heads straight for the minimum" (it zigzags across narrow valleys). Edge: the stable rates satisfy $\eta < \frac{2}{\lambda_{\max}}$, set by the steepest direction; the ratio of curvatures, the condition number of 1.4, sets the speed | N, C, I, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 116 is the training step of every neural network (2.1) and the first half of the Calculus Lab's part 4; lesson 114 reads a portfolio's sensitivities to several risk factors as a gradient.

**Level M3: Optimisation in several variables** (`lvl.multivar.optimise`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 118 | `lsn.multivar.critical-points` Critical points | Find critical points by solving $\nabla f = \mathbf 0$, including systems that need factorising and cases | Where could $f(x, y) = x^3 - 3x + y^2$ have peaks, pits or passes? | `surface-explorer` (critical-point markers), `numeric-input`, `choice` | Trap: dividing an equation by a variable that might be 0, losing solutions. Edge: a critical point can be degenerate: $x^4 + y^4$ and $x^4 - y^4$ look identical to second order | N, M, C, P |
| 119 | `lsn.multivar.quadratic-approx-hessian` Quadratic approximation and the Hessian | Write the second-order approximation $f(\mathbf a + \mathbf h) \approx f(\mathbf a) + \nabla f\cdot\mathbf h + \frac12\mathbf h^TH\mathbf h$ and read the local shape from the quadratic form | Near a critical point every smooth surface looks like a bowl, a dome or a saddle. Which quadratic matches this one? | `quadratic-form` (Hessian mode), `surface-explorer`, `matrix` | Trap: the $\frac12$ dropped; the mixed term counted once ($\mathbf h^TH\mathbf h$ contains $2f_{xy}h_1h_2$). Edge: $H$ is symmetric by Clairaut, which is why its eigenvalues are real (1.4) | A, X, C, N |
| 120 | `lsn.multivar.second-derivative-test` The second derivative test | Classify critical points with $D = f_{xx}f_{yy} - f_{xy}^2$ and the sign of $f_{xx}$, and recognise when the test is silent | Morph a quadratic between bowl, saddle and ridge with three sliders. Which combinations of entries make a saddle? | `quadratic-form` (morph sliders), `choice`, `numeric-input` | Trap: classifying by $f_{xx}$ alone ($D < 0$ means a saddle whatever $f_{xx}$ is). Edge: $D = 0$ decides nothing; $x^2 + y^2(1 - x)^3$ has one critical point, a local minimum, and it is not the global minimum | C, S, N, C |
| 121 | `lsn.multivar.global-closed-regions` Global extrema on closed regions | Find global extrema on a closed bounded region by comparing interior critical points with extremes on the boundary, parametrising each boundary piece | A plate covering $x^2 + y^2 \le 1$ has temperature $T = x^2 + 2y^2 - x$. Find its hottest and coldest points ($\frac94$ at $(-\frac12, \pm\frac{\sqrt3}{2})$; $-\frac14$ at $(\frac12, 0)$) | `surface-explorer` (region with its boundary path), `objective-explorer` (boundary profile), `numeric-input` | Trap: interior critical points only. Edge: the corners of a polygonal region are candidates in their own right | N, N, P, C |
| 122 | `lsn.multivar.least-squares` Least squares as minimisation | Fit $y = a + bx$ by minimising $\sum(y_i - a - bx_i)^2$, derive the normal equations from two partial derivatives, and show $b = \frac{\operatorname{cov}(x, y)}{\operatorname{var}(x)}$ | Find the bottom of the error bowl over (intercept, slope) for four data points | `least-squares-fit` (error map), `surface-explorer`, `numeric-input` | Trap: vertical and perpendicular distances confused (they are different problems with different answers). Edge: if every $x_i$ is equal the bowl becomes a trough and the slope is not determined | N, X, P, C |
| 123 | `lsn.multivar.mle-normal` Maximum likelihood with two parameters | Maximise the normal log-likelihood over $\mu$ and $\sigma^2$, and show the estimates are the sample mean and the variance with divisor $n$ | Five measurements of a length. Which bell curve makes them most likely? Slide its centre and its width | `surface-explorer` (log-likelihood over $(\mu, \sigma)$), `objective-explorer`, `expression-input` | Trap: differentiating the likelihood (a product) instead of the log-likelihood (a sum). Edge: the maximum likelihood variance divides by $n$, not $n - 1$, so it is biased low (1.6) | X, X, N, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 122 is linear regression (1.4, 1.6); lesson 123 is 1.6's maximum likelihood; lesson 120's edge case warns that a lone local minimum proves nothing in high dimensions (2.1).

**Level M4: Lagrange multipliers** (`lvl.multivar.lagrange`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 124 | `lsn.multivar.constraint-walk` Optimising along a constraint | Optimise $f$ subject to $g = c$ by parametrising or substituting the constraint, and see when that becomes hard | Walk once round the circle $x^2 + y^2 = 1$ on the hill $f = x + y$. Where are you highest? ($(\frac{1}{\sqrt2}, \frac{1}{\sqrt2})$, height $\sqrt2$) | `lagrange-explorer` (profile mode), `numeric-input`, `choice` | Trap: solving the unconstrained problem and then checking the constraint. Edge: substitution can quietly shrink the domain ($y = \sqrt{1 - x^2}$ loses the lower half of the circle) | N, P, C, M |
| 125 | `lsn.multivar.lagrange-tangency` Lagrange multipliers: tangency and parallel gradients | Explain why at a constrained optimum the level curve of $f$ touches the constraint, derive $\nabla f = \lambda\nabla g$, and solve the resulting system | At the best point on the circle the contour of $f$ just touches it. Draw both gradients there. What do you notice? | `lagrange-explorer` (contours and gradients), `derivation-steps`, `numeric-input` | Trap: dividing by $x$ or $\lambda$ in the system and losing solutions. Edge: the method misses points where $\nabla g = \mathbf 0$, and it finds maxima and minima alike, so every candidate must be evaluated | P, X, N, C |
| 126 | `lsn.multivar.shadow-price` The multiplier is a price | Show that $\lambda = \frac{df^*}{dc}$, the rate at which the optimum improves as the constraint is relaxed, and interpret it in budget and portfolio problems | Maximise output $xy$ with the budget $x + 2y = 100$ (optimum 1,250, $\lambda = 25$). The budget rises to 101. Predict the new optimum from $\lambda$, then check (1,275.125) | `lagrange-explorer` (shadow-price mode), `numeric-input`, `choice` | Trap: $\lambda$ ignored as a by-product of the algebra. Edge: the sign of $\lambda$ depends on how the constraint is written ($g = c$ or $c - g = 0$); its meaning does not | N, N, C, X |
| 127 | `lsn.multivar.lagrange-three-variables` Three variables and two constraints | Apply Lagrange multipliers with three variables and one or two constraints, including closest-point problems | Find the point on the plane $x + 2y + 2z = 9$ closest to the origin ($(1, 2, 2)$, distance 3). Then add a second constraint | `lagrange-explorer` (3D mode), `derivation-steps`, `numeric-input` | Trap: one multiplier used for two constraints; minimising the distance instead of its square and losing the algebra. Edge: with two constraints $\nabla f$ lies in the plane spanned by the two constraint gradients | N, X, N, C |
| 128 | `lsn.multivar.lagrange-portfolio-entropy` Lagrange at work: minimum variance and maximum entropy | Find the minimum-variance portfolio of uncorrelated assets ($w_i \propto \frac{1}{\sigma_i^2}$) and the maximum-entropy distribution on $n$ outcomes, uniform with no other constraint and exponential in the outcome with a fixed mean | Three uncorrelated assets with volatilities 10%, 20% and 30%. Fully invested, which weights give the least risk? ($\frac{36}{49}$, $\frac{9}{49}$, $\frac{4}{49}$; volatility $\frac{3}{35} \approx 8.6\%$) | `lagrange-explorer` (simplex mode), `numeric-input`, `expression-input` | Trap: equal weights assumed to be least risky; weights that do not add to 1. Edge: with short sales allowed and correlated assets the solution can hold negative weights (1.4, Stage 2) | N, X, N, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 128 is the first portfolio optimisation (1.7, canon-gap.md P6) and the maximum-entropy principle behind softmax and logistic models (2.1); lesson 126's shadow price reads the cost of a risk limit.

| 129 | `lsn.multivar.double-integral-rect` Double integrals as volume | Define $\iint_Rf\,dA$ as a limit of sums of columns, and evaluate over rectangles as iterated integrals (Fubini) | A tray covers $[0, 2] \times [0, 1]$ and the water in it is $1 + xy$ deep. How much water? Stack columns, then refine the grid (3) | `volume-slicer` (columns), `numeric-input` | Trap: the inner integral's variable left in the outer limits. Edge: for $f(x)g(y)$ on a rectangle the double integral factorises into $\int f \cdot \int g$, the root of independence in 1.5 | N, N, X, C |
| 130 | `lsn.multivar.general-regions` Double integrals over general regions | Describe a region by its bounds (strips in $x$ or in $y$), set up iterated integrals with variable limits, and evaluate them | The region between $y = x^2$ and $y = 2x$. Slide a vertical strip across it: where does each strip start and stop? (Area $\frac43$) | `region-bounds`, `expression-input`, `numeric-input` | Trap: constant inner limits for a non-rectangular region; outer limits that depend on the inner variable. Edge: some regions need splitting for one order and not for the other | X, X, N, R |
| 131 | `lsn.multivar.swap-order` Changing the order of integration | Redraw a region and swap the order of integration, especially when the original inner integral has no antiderivative | $\int_0^1\int_y^1e^{x^2}\,dx\,dy$: the inner integral has no formula. Is it hopeless? ($\frac{e - 1}{2}$) | `region-bounds` (strip turning), `derivation-steps`, `numeric-input` | Trap: swapping the limits without redrawing the region. Edge: Fubini needs $\iint\lvert f \rvert < \infty$; there are functions whose two orders give different answers | X, N, C, R |
| 132 | `lsn.multivar.polar-double` Double integrals in polar coordinates | Convert to polar coordinates with $dA = r\,dr\,d\theta$, choose polar when the region or integrand is round, and compute areas and averages over discs and sectors | Slice a disc into thin rings. Why does $r$ appear in $r\,dr\,d\theta$? | `region-bounds` (polar mode), `volume-slicer` (rings), `numeric-input` | Trap: $dA = dr\,d\theta$, with the $r$ missing. Edge: the average distance from the centre of a unit disc to a random point in it is $\frac23$, not $\frac12$, because outer rings hold more area | N, X, N, C |
| 133 | `lsn.multivar.gaussian-integral` The Gaussian integral | Compute $\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-x^2}\,dx = \sqrt\pi$ by squaring it into a double integral and switching to polar coordinates, and deduce the normal density's constant $\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}$ and $\Gamma(\frac12) = \sqrt\pi$ | $e^{-x^2}$ has no antiderivative, yet its total area is known exactly. One dimension is stuck. What does a second dimension buy? | `derivation-steps` (with the leap), `volume-slicer` (shells under $e^{-(x^2 + y^2)}$), `numeric-input` | Trap: $I^2$ written as $\int e^{-2x^2}\,dx$, squaring inside one integral instead of using two variables. Edge: the same constant $\sqrt{2\pi}$ appears in Stirling's formula (lesson 98), by Laplace's method | N, O, N, C |

Transfer screens: lesson 133 is the normal distribution's constant (1.5); lesson 129's factorisation is independence (1.5).

($\frac{7}{16}$) | `region-bounds` (density mode), `region-select`, `numeric-input` | Trap: integrating over the whole square instead of the event region; treating dependent variables as independent. Edge: the corner tetrahedron's volume $\frac16$ is the probability that three independent uniforms on $[0, 1]$ add to less than 1 | N, X, N, C |

**Course M quiz mix:** 128 items; choice and multi-select 37, 29%.

**Coverage check for 1.3.** Curriculum cover list: limits (D1, building on 0.3 R5), derivatives (D2 to D5), optimisation (U4, U5, M3), integrals (I1 to I5), Taylor series (X3, with lesson 32), partial derivatives (M1, M2), gradients (M2, with lessons 117 and 122), multiple integrals (M5, M6), Lagrange multipliers (M4). What an 18.01 final also tests: related rates (24), Newton's method (33), the mean value theorem (U2), curve sketching (U3), techniques of integration (I3), volumes, arc length, polar area and work (I5), separable equations (83), L'Hôpital's rule and improper integrals (34, 70, 71), series tests and power series (X1, X2), hyperbolic functions (25). What the 18.02 problem sets on partial derivatives and Lagrange multipliers also test: planes (107), non-independent variables (113), least squares (122), the second derivative test with boundaries (120, 121). Added for a top quant and ML programme: $\varepsilon$-$\delta$ (4, 5), the existence of $e$ by monotone convergence (6), the extreme value theorem with its hypotheses (7), proofs of the rules (26), the contraction condition for iteration (86), finite differences and automatic differentiation (27, 28), convexity and Jensen (U5), maximum likelihood, boundary optima and the median (47, 123), the markup rule under constant elasticity (46), Kelly and minimum variance (48, 128), volatility drag in funds that multiply each day's move (52), densities and expectations (72, 73, 135), differentiating under the integral sign (74), asymptotics and Stirling (98), the radius from complex singularities (96), Euler's formula (104), finance expansions (105), backpropagation and gradient descent (116, 117), the Gaussian integral (133), the Jacobian (134). Left out on purpose: line and surface integrals and the theorems of Green, Stokes and Gauss (outside the cover list and rarely used in quant or ML work); inequality constraints and KKT conditions (canon-gap.md P4); second-order and systems of differential equations (canon-gap.md P8, 45 h); uniform convergence and integrability proofs (canon-gap.md P16).

---

## 3. Homework blueprint per level

### 3.0 Rules for every set

- **Issue, due and marking** follow assessment.md 2.5: issued when the level's last lesson is done, due 7 days later, no feedback before submission, code items run their visible examples freely and their hidden tests at submission, long-form items self-marked against the rubric with Bertie's suggested mark and evidence quote beside the learner's (ai-tutor.md 2.7; the rubric's `genre` is `proof`, `derivation` or `written`).
- **Size and mix.** Levels of 4 or 5 lessons get **8 items: 3 at difficulty 3, 3 at difficulty 4, 2 at difficulty 5**, about 1 hour 45 minutes. Levels of 6 or 7 lessons get **10 items: 4 at difficulty 3, 4 at difficulty 4, 2 at difficulty 5**, about 2 hours 15 minutes. Exactly one long-form item (a derivation, proof or written explanation with a 10-point rubric, usually difficulty 4) and exactly one code drill (difficulty 3 or 4) sit inside those counts. At least half the items are generator slots, so a redo is a fresh variant. At most two choice items per set. Items marked "exact" ban the calculator, so the set rehearses the finals-style paper.
- **Long-form marking.** Every rubric has 4 to 10 points totalling 10 marks, and each point names the model-answer lines that evidence it (content-format.md 3.4). 7 of 10 is the bar wherever an outcome cites a rubric.
- **Code drills** list their tests here so CI can check that the reference solution passes and each known-wrong solution fails its named test (content-format.md 3.9 and 6.11). Every drill meets the `Drill` schema: the first test listed is the visible example run on Run, at least two tests are named edge cases, and at least one known-wrong solution names the test it fails and the misconception or slip it encodes. All 25 homework drills and the 8 practice drills use the standard library only and declare `needs: []`, so they run under Pyodide on both targets (`arch-decision.md` decision 15); a device that cannot load Python hands the drill to the web app and takes the result back by code (`assessment.md` 14.3). Comparisons use `zqtest`'s `isclose` with relative tolerance $10^{-9}$ unless the test states another, and no test compares a computed sum for exact equality across runtimes, because built-in `sum` is compensated from Python 3.12 and gives different last digits on CPython 3.11 and on Pyodide's 3.14 (`stage1-python-tools.md` draft 2 measured it). Tests check exception types, never messages, which also changed between those versions. Seeded drills draw from `random.Random(seed).random()` only (decision 10). Every value below was reproduced on 6 October 2026 by a reference solution, and every known-wrong solution was run and failed its named test (section 6).
- **The 18.02 exit link.** Sets M1 to M4 (`set.multivar.surfaces`, `set.multivar.gradient`, `set.multivar.optimise`, `set.multivar.lagrange`) are the in-app half of the 18.02 part of the exit (section 4.3): each must be submitted and every item below full marks redone correctly once (`sets` instrument, `assessment.md` decision 29).

Below, each level gives its set id, item mix, long-form item, code drill with its tests, and two example problems in full: example A is the long-form item with its model answer and rubric, example B a difficulty-5 item with its worked answer and decision point.

### 3.1 The 25 sets

**D1 Rates and limits** (`set.deriv.limits`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: an $\varepsilon$-$\delta$ proof. Code drill `drl.calc.bisection` (d3, `needs: []`): `bisect(f, a, b, tol)` halves until $b - a \le$ `tol` and returns `(midpoint, iterations)`. Tests: $x^3 - 2$ on $[1, 2]$ with `tol = 1e-9` returns a root within $10^{-9}$ of $\sqrt[3]{2} = 1.2599210498948732$ in exactly 30 iterations (visible). Edge tests: $f(a)f(b) > 0$ raises `ValueError`; $f(a) = 0$ returns `(a, 0)`; `tol <= 0` raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: one that skips the sign check (fails the `ValueError` test; `mc.calc.ivt-without-sign-change`), one that keeps the wrong half (fails the root test; slip).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove from the definition that $\lim_{x \to 3}(x^2 - x) = 6$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Let $\varepsilon > 0$. We must find $\delta > 0$ such that $0 < \lvert x - 3 \rvert < \delta$ implies $\lvert x^2 - x - 6 \rvert < \varepsilon$. (2) Factorise: $\lvert x^2 - x - 6 \rvert = \lvert x - 3 \rvert\,\lvert x + 2 \rvert$. (3) Tame the second factor: if $\lvert x - 3 \rvert < 1$ then $2 < x < 4$, so $\lvert x + 2 \rvert < 6$. (4) Choose $\delta = \min\left(1, \frac{\varepsilon}{6}\right)$, which depends on $\varepsilon$ only. (5) Then $0 < \lvert x - 3 \rvert < \delta$ gives $\lvert x^2 - x - 6 \rvert < 6\lvert x - 3 \rvert < 6 \cdot \frac{\varepsilon}{6} = \varepsilon$, using $\delta \le 1$ for the first inequality and $\delta \le \frac{\varepsilon}{6}$ for the second. (6) As $\varepsilon$ was arbitrary, the limit is 6.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the goal with the quantifiers in the right order (line 1). 1: the factorisation (line 2). 3: the bound on $\lvert x + 2 \rvert$ together with the restriction that produces it (line 3), the most-missed point. 2: a $\delta$ that depends on $\varepsilon$ alone (line 4). 2: the chain of inequalities using both parts of the minimum (line 5). 1: the conclusion (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Show that $x = \cos x$ has exactly one real solution, and find it to two decimal places.
  *Answer.* Let $g(x) = x - \cos x$, continuous everywhere. $g(0) = -1 < 0$ and $g(1) = 1 - \cos 1 > 0$, so by the intermediate value theorem there is a root in $(0, 1)$. There is no other: for $x > 1$, $x > 1 \ge \cos x$; for $x < -1$, $x < -1 \le \cos x$; for $-1 \le x \le 0$, $\cos x \ge \cos 1 > 0 \ge x$; and on $[0, 1]$, $x$ increases while $\cos x$ decreases, so they cross at most once. Bisection from $[0, 1]$: after eight halvings the root lies in $[0.73828, 0.74219]$, both of whose ends round to 0.74. (The root is $0.739085\ldots$) Decision point: the theorem gives existence only; uniqueness needs its own argument, and with no derivatives yet it comes from splitting the line into regions.

**D2 The derivative** (`set.deriv.derivative`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: differentiable implies continuous. Code drill `drl.calc.difference-quotient` (d3, `needs: []`): `avg_rate(f, a, h)`, `rate_table(f, a, hs)` returning `(h, rate)` pairs, and `central_estimate(f, a, h=1e-5)`. Tests: `avg_rate` of $x^2$ at 3 with $h = 0.1$ is 6.1 (visible); `central_estimate` of $x^3$ at 2 is within $10^{-6}$ of 12. Edge tests: $h = 0$ raises `ValueError`; $h = -0.1$ gives 5.9 (a window to the left). Known-wrong: a solution computing $\frac{f(a + h) - f(a)}{a}$ fails the first test (`mc.calc.divide-by-point`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove that if $f$ is differentiable at $a$ then $f$ is continuous at $a$, and show by an example that the converse is false.
  *Model answer.* (1) For $x \ne a$, $f(x) - f(a) = \frac{f(x) - f(a)}{x - a} \cdot (x - a)$. (2) As $x \to a$ the first factor tends to $f'(a)$, which exists, and the second to 0. (3) By the product law for limits, $f(x) - f(a) \to f'(a) \cdot 0 = 0$. (4) So $\lim_{x \to a}f(x) = f(a)$: $f$ is continuous at $a$. (5) Converse: $f(x) = \lvert x \rvert$ is continuous at 0, since $\lvert x \rvert \to 0$. (6) But $\frac{f(h) - f(0)}{h} = \frac{\lvert h \rvert}{h}$ is $1$ for $h > 0$ and $-1$ for $h < 0$; the one-sided limits differ, so $f'(0)$ does not exist.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the rewrite, valid because $x \ne a$ (line 1). 2: both factor limits (line 2). 2: the product law cited (line 3). 1: the conclusion (line 4). 1: continuity of $\lvert x \rvert$ (line 5). 2: the one-sided quotients (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Let $g(x) = x^2$ for rational $x$ and $g(x) = 0$ for irrational $x$. Show that $g'(0)$ exists and find it, and show that $g$ is not differentiable at any $a \ne 0$.
  *Answer.* $\frac{g(h) - g(0)}{h} = \frac{g(h)}{h}$ is $h$ for rational $h$ and $0$ for irrational $h$, so $\left\lvert\frac{g(h)}{h}\right\rvert \le \lvert h \rvert \to 0$ and, by the squeeze theorem, $g'(0) = 0$. For $a \ne 0$: every interval round $a$ contains rationals, where $g$ is close to $a^2 \ne 0$, and irrationals, where $g = 0$; with $\varepsilon = \frac{a^2}{2}$ no $\delta$ works, so $g$ is not continuous at $a$ and, by example A, not differentiable there. Decision point: "it jumps everywhere, so no derivative anywhere" fails at 0, where $\lvert g(h) \rvert \le h^2$ wins.

**D3 Rules of differentiation** (`set.deriv.rules`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5), six of them "exact" differentiation items from generators. Long-form: $\frac{d}{dx}\ln x$ and the general power rule. Code drill `drl.calc.gradient-check` (d4, `needs: []`): `check_derivative(f, df, xs, h=1e-5, rtol=1e-6)` returns the points where the central difference disagrees with `df(x)` by more than `rtol * max(1, abs(df(x)))`. Tests: `(sin, cos, [0, 1, 2])` returns `[]` (visible); `(x*exp(x), exp, [0, 1, 2])` returns `[1, 2]` (the wrong derivative agrees only at 0); `(log, 1/x, [0.5, 1, 10])` returns `[]`. Edge tests: an empty list returns `[]`; `(exp, exp, [20])` returns `[]`, because at $e^{20} \approx 4.9 \times 10^8$ only a relative tolerance is fair. Known-wrong: an absolute tolerance fails the $e^{20}$ test (slip); a forward difference fails the sine test, its error near $5 \times 10^{-6}$ (`mc.calc.forward-is-accurate-enough`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Starting from $\frac{d}{dx}e^x = e^x$, derive $\frac{d}{dx}\ln x = \frac1x$, then use logarithmic differentiation to prove $\frac{d}{dx}x^r = rx^{r-1}$ for every real $r$ and $x > 0$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Let $y = \ln x$ for $x > 0$, so $e^y = x$ (taking, as lesson 20 showed by reflection, $\ln$ to be differentiable). (2) Differentiate both sides with respect to $x$, using the chain rule on the left: $e^y\frac{dy}{dx} = 1$. (3) So $\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{e^y} = \frac1x$. (4) Now let $f(x) = x^r$ with $x > 0$, so $f > 0$ and $\ln f(x) = r\ln x$. (5) Differentiate: $\frac{f'(x)}{f(x)} = \frac{r}{x}$, by the chain rule on the left and line 3 on the right. (6) So $f'(x) = f(x)\frac{r}{x} = rx^{r-1}$. (7) The argument needs $x > 0$ for the logarithms; for whole $r$ the rule holds for every $x$ by lesson 11.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the inverse relation and the chain rule (lines 1, 2). 1: the result (line 3). 2: taking logarithms, with the condition $x > 0$ (line 4). 2: differentiating $\ln f$ (line 5). 2: the conclusion (line 6). 1: the domain remark (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* For which real $k$ does $e^x = kx$ have no real solution, exactly one, or two?
  *Answer.* The line $y = kx$ is tangent to $y = e^x$ at $x = a$ when $e^a = ka$ and $e^a = k$, so $a = 1$ and $k = e$. Let $h(x) = e^x - kx$. If $k < 0$: $h'(x) = e^x - k > 0$, $h \to -\infty$ as $x \to -\infty$ and $h(0) = 1$, so exactly one (negative) solution. If $k = 0$: none. If $0 < k < e$: $h$ has its minimum at $x = \ln k$, with value $k(1 - \ln k) > 0$, so none. If $k = e$: the minimum is 0, at $x = 1$: one. If $k > e$: the minimum is negative and $h \to \infty$ in both directions: two. Decision point: the tangency gives the threshold $e$, but the case $k < 0$ is easily missed because the picture draws the right half-plane.

**D4 Inverses, implicit curves and related rates** (`set.deriv.implicit`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the derivative of $\arcsin$. Code drill `drl.calc.ladder` (d3, `needs: []`): `top_rate(L, x, v)` returns $\frac{dy}{dt} = -\frac{xv}{\sqrt{L^2 - x^2}}$ for a ladder of length $L$ whose foot is $x$ from the wall and moving out at $v$. Tests: $(5, 3, 0.5) \to -0.375$ (visible); $(5, 4, 0.5) \to -\frac23$; the height $\sqrt{25 - x^2}$ at foot positions 0.5 mm either side of 3 m (one millisecond of motion at 0.5 m/s), divided by $10^{-3}$ s, agrees within $10^{-3}$. Edge tests: $x \ge L$ raises `ValueError`; $x = 0$ gives 0 (the foot at the wall). Known-wrong: a solution that returns $+0.375$ fails the first test (`mc.calc.rate-sign-lost`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Derive $\frac{d}{dx}\arcsin x = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - x^2}}$ for $-1 < x < 1$, justify the sign of the root, and explain what happens as $x \to \pm1$.
  *Model answer.* (1) $y = \arcsin x$ means $\sin y = x$ with $-\frac{\pi}{2} \le y \le \frac{\pi}{2}$. (2) Differentiate implicitly: $\cos y\,\frac{dy}{dx} = 1$. (3) So $\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\cos y}$ wherever $\cos y \ne 0$. (4) $\cos y = \pm\sqrt{1 - \sin^2 y} = \pm\sqrt{1 - x^2}$. (5) For $-\frac{\pi}{2} < y < \frac{\pi}{2}$, $\cos y > 0$, so the positive root applies: $\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - x^2}}$. (6) As $x \to \pm1$, $y \to \pm\frac{\pi}{2}$, $\cos y \to 0$ and the slope grows without bound: $\arcsin$ has vertical tangents at its ends, the reflections of $\sin$'s horizontal tangents at $\pm\frac{\pi}{2}$.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the definition with its range (line 1). 2: implicit differentiation (line 2). 1: the condition $\cos y \ne 0$ (line 3). 2: the Pythagorean identity (line 4). 2: the sign fixed by the range (line 5). 2: the end behaviour with the reflection (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* On the folium $x^3 + y^3 = 6xy$, find every point other than the origin where the tangent is horizontal, and explain what happens at the origin.
  *Answer.* Implicitly, $3x^2 + 3y^2y' = 6y + 6xy'$, so $y' = \frac{2y - x^2}{y^2 - 2x}$. A horizontal tangent needs $2y = x^2$ with $y^2 \ne 2x$. Substituting $y = \frac{x^2}{2}$: $x^3 + \frac{x^6}{8} = 3x^3$, so $x^3(x^3 - 16) = 0$. Away from the origin, $x = 16^{1/3} = 2^{4/3} \approx 2.520$ and $y = 2^{5/3} \approx 3.175$; there $y^2 - 2x = 2^{7/3} > 0$, so the point counts. At the origin both numerator and denominator vanish: the formula gives $\frac00$ because the curve crosses itself there, with one branch tangent to the $x$-axis and one to the $y$-axis (the lowest-order terms $6xy = 0$). Decision point: the formula is silent exactly where the curve is most interesting; the branches have to be read from the equation's lowest-order terms.

**D5 Derivatives computed and proved** (`set.deriv.machinery`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: a proof of the chain rule. Code drill `drl.calc.dual-numbers` (d4, `needs: []`): a class `Dual(a, b)` with `+`, `-`, `*`, `/` (including an `int` or `float` on either side), functions `dsin`, `dexp`, `dlog`, and `derivative(f, x)`. Tests: $x^3$ at 2 gives 12 (visible); $\sin x \cdot e^x$ at 0 gives 1; $\frac{x}{1 + x^2}$ at 1 gives 0. Edge tests: `3 - x` at 5 gives $-1$ (reflected subtraction); $\frac1x$ at 0 raises `ZeroDivisionError`. Known-wrong: a class without `__rsub__` fails the reflected test (slip); one with the product's dual part as `a1*b2` only fails the visible test (it returns 4 for $x^3$ at 2) and the second (it returns 0) (`mc.calc.product-rule-one-term`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove the chain rule: if $g$ is differentiable at $a$ and $f$ at $b = g(a)$, then $(f \circ g)'(a) = f'(b)g'(a)$. Your proof must work when $g(a + h) = g(a)$ for arbitrarily small $h$.
  *Model answer.* (1) $f$ is differentiable at $b$ exactly when $f(b + k) = f(b) + f'(b)k + \eta(k)k$ for a function $\eta$ with $\eta(k) \to 0$ as $k \to 0$ and $\eta(0) = 0$ (define $\eta(k) = \frac{f(b + k) - f(b)}{k} - f'(b)$ for $k \ne 0$). (2) Put $k = g(a + h) - g(a)$; then $k = g'(a)h + \gamma(h)h$ with $\gamma(h) \to 0$, by line 1 applied to $g$. (3) $f(g(a + h)) - f(g(a)) = f(b + k) - f(b) = f'(b)k + \eta(k)k$, which holds even when $k = 0$, because $\eta(0) = 0$. (4) Divide by $h \ne 0$: $\frac{f(g(a + h)) - f(g(a))}{h} = \left(f'(b) + \eta(k)\right)\left(g'(a) + \gamma(h)\right)$. (5) As $h \to 0$: $k \to 0$ because $g$ is continuous at $a$ (D2 example A), so $\eta(k) \to 0$; and $\gamma(h) \to 0$. (6) The quotient tends to $f'(b)g'(a)$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the linear-approximation form (line 1). 1: the same for $g$ (line 2). 2: the identity with the remark that $k$ may be 0, the gap in the naive proof (line 3). 2: the factorisation (line 4). 2: $k \to 0$ by continuity of $g$ (line 5). 1: the conclusion (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A central difference $\frac{f(a + h) - f(a - h)}{2h}$ has total error at most about $E(h) = \frac{\lvert f'''(a) \rvert}{6}h^2 + \frac{\epsilon\lvert f(a) \rvert}{h}$, truncation plus round-off, with $\epsilon = 2^{-52} \approx 2.2 \times 10^{-16}$. For $f = e^x$ at $a = 0$, find the $h$ that minimises $E$ and the minimum, and compare $h = 10^{-10}$.
  *Answer.* Here $E(h) = \frac{h^2}{6} + \frac{\epsilon}{h}$. $E'(h) = \frac{h}{3} - \frac{\epsilon}{h^2} = 0$ gives $h = (3\epsilon)^{1/3} \approx 8.7 \times 10^{-6}$, and $E_{\min} \approx 1.27 \times 10^{-11} + 2.54 \times 10^{-11} \approx 3.8 \times 10^{-11}$. (Measured: the error at $h = 10^{-5}$ is about $1.2 \times 10^{-11}$, inside the bound.) At $h = 10^{-10}$ the round-off term alone is $2.2 \times 10^{-6}$ (measured error about $8 \times 10^{-8}$), thousands of times worse. Decision point: "as small as possible" is the naive choice; the two error sources pull in opposite directions and the best $h$ balances them.

**U1 Approximation** (`set.deriv-apps.approx`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: log returns against simple returns. Code drill `drl.calc.newton` (d3, `needs: []`): `newton(f, df, x0, tol=1e-12, max_iter=50)` stops when $\lvert f(x) \rvert \le$ `tol` and returns `(root, iterations)`; raises `ZeroDivisionError` when `df(x) == 0` and `RuntimeError` after `max_iter`. Tests: $x^2 - 2$ from 1 reaches $\sqrt2$ within $10^{-12}$ in 5 iterations (visible); $\cos x - x$ from 1 gives 0.7390851332151607. Edge tests: $x^3 - 2x + 2$ from 0 raises `RuntimeError` (the iterates run 0, 1, 0, 1, ...); $x^2 - 1$ from 0 raises `ZeroDivisionError`. Known-wrong: a loop with no `max_iter` guard fails the cycling test by its time limit (`mc.calc.newton-always-converges`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Show that the log return $\ln(1 + r)$ is $r - \frac{r^2}{2}$ to second order in the simple return $r$. (b) Give the gap for $r = 1\%$ and $r = 20\%$, and comment. (c) Explain which return adds across time and which adds across the assets of a portfolio.
  *Model answer.* (1) At $r = 0$: $\ln(1 + r) = 0$, its first derivative $\frac{1}{1 + r} = 1$, its second $-\frac{1}{(1 + r)^2} = -1$. (2) So $\ln(1 + r) \approx r - \frac{r^2}{2}$ (lesson 32). (3) $r = 0.01$: the gap is about $0.00005$; indeed $\ln 1.01 = 0.0099503$. (4) $r = 0.20$: the estimate is 0.02, but $\ln 1.2 = 0.18232$, a gap of 0.0177; the cubic term $\frac{r^3}{3} \approx 0.0027$ is no longer small. (5) Across time log returns add: $\ln\frac{S_2}{S_0} = \ln\frac{S_2}{S_1} + \ln\frac{S_1}{S_0}$, while simple returns compound. (6) Across assets simple returns add with the weights: a portfolio's simple return is $\sum w_ir_i$; log returns do not combine that way. (7) So models of one asset through time use log returns, and portfolio aggregation at one date uses simple returns.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the expansion (lines 1, 2). 2: both numbers with the cubic-term comment (lines 3, 4). 2: adding over time (line 5). 2: adding over assets (line 6). 2: the conclusion (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Evaluate $\lim_{x \to 0}\left(\frac{1}{\sin^2x} - \frac{1}{x^2}\right)$.
  *Answer.* Combine and factorise: $\frac{x^2 - \sin^2x}{x^2\sin^2x} = \frac{x - \sin x}{x^3} \cdot \frac{x + \sin x}{x} \cdot \frac{x^2}{\sin^2x}$. As $x \to 0$: $\frac{x - \sin x}{x^3} \to \frac16$ (L'Hôpital three times: $\frac{1 - \cos x}{3x^2}$, $\frac{\sin x}{6x}$, $\frac16$), $\frac{x + \sin x}{x} \to 2$ and $\frac{x^2}{\sin^2x} \to 1$. The limit is $\frac13$. Decision point: L'Hôpital straight on the combined fraction needs four rounds of growing derivatives; factorising first isolates one standard limit.

**U2 Extremes and the mean value theorem** (`set.deriv-apps.mvt`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the mean value theorem from Rolle's theorem. Code drill `drl.calc.mvt-points` (d3, `needs: []`): `mvt_points(f, df, a, b, n=1000)` returns every $c$ in $(a, b)$ with $f'(c)$ equal to the chord slope $m$, by scanning $n$ subintervals for zeros and sign changes of $f' - m$ and bisecting. Tests: $x^3$ on $[0, 3]$ gives $[\sqrt3]$ within $10^{-9}$ (visible); $\sin$ on $[0, 2\pi]$ gives $[\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{3\pi}{2}]$. Edge tests: $x^2$ on $[1, 5]$ gives $[3]$, a zero that falls exactly on a scan point; $a \ge b$ raises `ValueError`; a linear $f$ raises `ValueError`, because every point works. Known-wrong: one that returns only the first sign change fails the sine test (`mc.calc.mvt-point-unique`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove the mean value theorem from Rolle's theorem, and say where each hypothesis is used.
  *Model answer.* (1) Let $f$ be continuous on $[a, b]$ and differentiable on $(a, b)$. (2) Let $m = \frac{f(b) - f(a)}{b - a}$, the chord's slope, and $g(x) = f(x) - f(a) - m(x - a)$, the vertical gap between the curve and the chord. (3) $g$ is continuous on $[a, b]$ and differentiable on $(a, b)$, being $f$ minus a linear function. (4) $g(a) = 0$ and $g(b) = f(b) - f(a) - m(b - a) = 0$. (5) By Rolle's theorem there is $c \in (a, b)$ with $g'(c) = 0$. (6) $g'(c) = f'(c) - m$, so $f'(c) = \frac{f(b) - f(a)}{b - a}$. (7) Continuity on the closed interval gives Rolle its maximum or minimum (the extreme value theorem); differentiability inside gives $g'(c)$.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: hypotheses (line 1). 3: the auxiliary function with its meaning, the creative step (line 2). 1: its properties (line 3). 2: the end values (line 4). 1: Rolle applied (line 5). 1: the conclusion (line 6). 1: where each hypothesis is used (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Prove that $e^\pi > \pi^e$.
  *Answer.* Taking logarithms, the claim is $\pi > e\ln\pi$, that is $\frac{\ln\pi}{\pi} < \frac1e = \frac{\ln e}{e}$. Let $g(x) = \frac{\ln x}{x}$; $g'(x) = \frac{1 - \ln x}{x^2} < 0$ for $x > e$, so $g$ is decreasing on $[e, \infty)$ (lesson 37). Since $\pi > e$, $g(\pi) < g(e)$, which is the claim. Decision point: $23.14 > 22.46$ is a check, not a proof; one function's monotonicity turns the comparison of two numbers into one derivative.

**U3 The shape of a graph** (`set.deriv-apps.shape`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5); two sketch items graded by features. Long-form: a full analysis of $x^2e^{-x}$. Code drill `drl.calc.turning-points` (d4, `needs: []`): `turning_points(a, b, c, d)` for $ax^3 + bx^2 + cx + d$ returns a sorted list of `(x, 'max' | 'min')`. Tests: $(1, 0, -3, 0) \to [(-1, \text{max}), (1, \text{min})]$ (visible); $(-1, 0, 3, 0) \to [(-1, \text{min}), (1, \text{max})]$. Edge tests: $(1, 0, 0, 0) \to []$ (a stationary inflection is neither); $(1, -3, 3, -1) \to []$; $a = 0$ raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: one that classifies every root of $f'$ by the sign of $f''$ alone, calling $f'' = 0$ a maximum, fails the $(1, 0, 0, 0)$ test (`mc.calc.critical-means-extremum`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Analyse $f(x) = x^2e^{-x}$ and sketch it, justifying every feature.
  *Model answer.* (1) Domain all reals; $f \ge 0$, with $f = 0$ only at $x = 0$. (2) $f'(x) = (2x - x^2)e^{-x} = x(2 - x)e^{-x}$: negative for $x < 0$, positive on $(0, 2)$, negative for $x > 2$. (3) So a minimum at $(0, 0)$, which is global by line 1, and a local maximum at $(2, 4e^{-2}) \approx (2, 0.541)$. (4) $f''(x) = (x^2 - 4x + 2)e^{-x}$, zero at $x = 2 \pm \sqrt2 \approx 0.586$ and $3.414$; $f''$ changes sign at each, so both are inflections, with $f$ concave down between them. (5) As $x \to \infty$, $f \to 0$ because $e^x$ outgrows $x^2$ (lesson 41): the line $y = 0$ is an asymptote on the right; as $x \to -\infty$, $f \to \infty$. (6) The sketch falls steeply from the left to touch the axis at the origin, rises to the hump at $x = 2$, and decays towards the axis, with an inflection either side of the hump.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: domain and sign (line 1). 2: the sign chart of $f'$ (line 2). 2: the classification (line 3). 2: inflections with the sign change checked (line 4). 2: end behaviour with its reason (line 5). 1: a sketch consistent with every feature (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* For each real $c$, how many real solutions does $x^4 - 4x^3 + 4x^2 = c$ have?
  *Answer.* $f(x) = x^2(x - 2)^2 \ge 0$ and $f'(x) = 4x(x - 1)(x - 2)$: minima $f(0) = f(2) = 0$, a local maximum $f(1) = 1$, and $f \to \infty$ in both directions. So $c < 0$: none; $c = 0$: two ($x = 0, 2$); $0 < c < 1$: four; $c = 1$: three ($x = 1$ and $x = 1 \pm \sqrt2$, from $x(x - 2) = \pm1$); $c > 1$: two. Decision point: the algebra of a general quartic stalls; the shape answers every case at once.

**U4 Optimisation** (`set.deriv-apps.optimise`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the Kelly fraction. Code drill `drl.calc.kelly` (d3, `needs: []`): `kelly_fraction(p, b)` returns $\max\left(0, p - \frac{1 - p}{b}\right)$; `growth_rate(f, p, b)` returns $p\ln(1 + bf) + (1 - p)\ln(1 - f)$. Tests: $(0.6, 1) \to 0.2$ (visible); `growth_rate(0.2, 0.6, 1)` is $0.0201355135506889$ within $10^{-12}$; $(0.5, 2) \to 0.25$; `growth_rate(0.4, 0.6, 1)` is negative (twice Kelly). Edge tests: $(0.4, 1) \to 0$ (no edge, no bet); $f \ge 1$ raises `ValueError`; $p$ outside $[0, 1]$ raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: $p - \frac{1 - p}{b}$ without the floor at 0 fails the $(0.4, 1)$ test with $-0.2$ (`mc.calc.negative-stake`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A bet pays $b$ to 1 and wins with probability $p$ ($q = 1 - p$). You stake a fixed fraction $f$ of your wealth on every bet. Show that the long-run growth rate per bet is $g(f) = p\ln(1 + bf) + q\ln(1 - f)$, find the $f$ that maximises it, prove the maximum is global on $[0, 1)$, and say when not to bet.
  *Model answer.* (1) After $n$ bets with $W$ wins, wealth is $W_0(1 + bf)^W(1 - f)^{n - W}$. (2) Growth per bet is $\frac1n\ln\frac{W_n}{W_0} = \frac{W}{n}\ln(1 + bf) + \left(1 - \frac{W}{n}\right)\ln(1 - f)$, which tends to $g(f)$ because $\frac{W}{n} \to p$ (the law of large numbers, 1.5). (3) $g'(f) = \frac{pb}{1 + bf} - \frac{q}{1 - f}$. (4) $g'(f) = 0 \iff pb(1 - f) = q(1 + bf) \iff f^* = \frac{pb - q}{b} = p - \frac{q}{b}$. (5) $g''(f) = -\frac{pb^2}{(1 + bf)^2} - \frac{q}{(1 - f)^2} < 0$: $g$ is concave on $[0, 1)$, so its critical point is the global maximum (lessons 49 and 50). (6) If $pb \le q$ there is no edge: $g'(0) = pb - q \le 0$, $g$ decreases from 0, and the best stake is nothing. (7) For $p = 0.6$, $b = 1$: $f^* = 0.2$ and $g(0.2) \approx 0.0201$ per bet.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the growth rate set up (lines 1, 2). 1: the derivative (line 3). 2: the critical point (line 4). 2: global by concavity (line 5). 2: the no-edge case (line 6). 1: the example (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Show that $S(c) = \sum_i\lvert x_i - c \rvert$ is minimised at a median of the data, and find every minimiser for the data 1, 2, 7, 10.
  *Answer.* $S$ is piecewise linear; between data points its slope is (number of points below $c$) minus (number above $c$). For the data: slope $-4$ for $c < 1$, $-2$ on $(1, 2)$, $0$ on $(2, 7)$, $+2$ on $(7, 10)$, $+4$ above 10. So $S$ falls, is flat at its minimum value 14 on $[2, 7]$, then rises: every $c \in [2, 7]$ minimises it, and the median (4.5 by the usual convention) is one of them. In general the slope is negative while fewer than half the points lie below $c$ and positive once more than half do, so the minimum is at a median. Decision point: "set $S'(c) = 0$" fails, because $S'$ does not exist at the data and is zero on a whole interval; counting slopes works.

**U5 Convexity** (`set.deriv-apps.convexity`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Jensen for three points and the AM-GM inequality. Code drill `drl.calc.descent-1d` (d3, `needs: []`): `descend(df, x0, lr, steps)` returns the iterates; `classify_lr(curvature, lr)` returns `'converges'`, `'oscillates'` or `'diverges'` for a quadratic, from $\lvert 1 - \eta c \rvert$ below, at or above 1. Tests: $f' = 2(x - 3)$ from 0 with `lr = 0.1` is within $10^{-8}$ of 3 after 100 steps (visible); `classify_lr(2, 0.5)` is `'converges'`, `classify_lr(2, 0.75)` is `'converges'`. Edge tests: with `lr = 1.0` the iterates run 0, 6, 0, 6 and `classify_lr(2, 1.0)` is `'oscillates'`; with `lr = 1.1`, $\lvert x_{50} - 3 \rvert > 1000$ and `classify_lr(2, 1.2)` is `'diverges'`. Known-wrong: the stability bound taken as $\eta < \frac1c$ instead of $\frac2c$ fails the `0.75` test (`mc.calc.lr-bound-one-over-curvature`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Starting from the chord definition of convexity, prove Jensen's inequality for three points, and deduce that $\frac{a + b + c}{3} \ge \sqrt[3]{abc}$ for positive $a$, $b$, $c$, with equality only when $a = b = c$.
  *Model answer.* (1) $f$ is convex on an interval $I$ when $f(\lambda u + (1 - \lambda)v) \le \lambda f(u) + (1 - \lambda)f(v)$ for all $u, v \in I$ and $\lambda \in [0, 1]$: this is the two-point inequality. (2) For weights $\lambda_1 + \lambda_2 + \lambda_3 = 1$ with $\lambda_3 < 1$, write $\lambda_1x_1 + \lambda_2x_2 + \lambda_3x_3 = (1 - \lambda_3)y + \lambda_3x_3$, where $y = \frac{\lambda_1x_1 + \lambda_2x_2}{1 - \lambda_3}$ is a weighted average of $x_1$ and $x_2$, so $y \in I$. (3) Two-point Jensen twice: $f((1 - \lambda_3)y + \lambda_3x_3) \le (1 - \lambda_3)f(y) + \lambda_3f(x_3) \le \lambda_1f(x_1) + \lambda_2f(x_2) + \lambda_3f(x_3)$. (4) $-\ln$ is convex on $(0, \infty)$: its second derivative is $\frac{1}{x^2} > 0$. (5) With equal weights $\frac13$: $-\ln\frac{a + b + c}{3} \le -\frac{\ln a + \ln b + \ln c}{3}$. (6) Multiply by $-1$ and apply the increasing function $\exp$: $\frac{a + b + c}{3} \ge \sqrt[3]{abc}$. Equality in Jensen for a strictly convex function needs all points equal: $a = b = c$.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the definition (line 1). 3: the regrouping, the creative step (line 2). 1: the double application (line 3). 2: convexity of $-\ln$ (line 4). 2: the deduction (lines 5, 6). 1: the equality case (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Each year, independently, a fund returns $+50\%$ or $-40\%$ with equal chance. Show that its expected return is positive but its typical long-run wealth shrinks, and quantify both after 20 years.
  *Answer.* $E[R] = \frac12(0.5) + \frac12(-0.4) = 0.05$, so expected wealth after 20 years is $1.05^{20} \approx 2.65$ times the start. But $E[\ln(1 + R)] = \frac12(\ln 1.5 + \ln 0.6) = \frac12\ln 0.9 \approx -0.0527$ per year: by Jensen ($\ln$ concave), $E[\ln(1 + R)] \le \ln(1 + E[R])$, and here the gap flips the sign. The median path (10 up, 10 down) ends at $1.5^{10} \times 0.6^{10} = 0.9^{10} \approx 0.349$ of the start; the expected 2.65 is carried by rare lucky paths. Decision point: averaging returns predicts growth; averaging log returns predicts what a typical investor experiences.

**I1 Area and the definite integral** (`set.integral.area`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: $\int_0^b x^2\,dx$ from the definition. Code drill `drl.calc.riemann` (d3, `needs: []`): `riemann(f, a, b, n, rule)` with `rule` in `'left'`, `'right'`, `'mid'`. Tests: $x^2$ on $[0, 1]$ with $n = 4$ gives 0.46875 (right; visible), 0.21875 (left) and 0.328125 (mid); the midpoint sum with $n = 100$ is within $10^{-5}$ of $\frac13$ (its error is $-8.3 \times 10^{-6}$). Edge tests: $n = 0$ raises `ValueError`; an unknown rule raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: a left sum that samples $a + kh$ for $k = 1, \dots, n$ (the right endpoints) fails the left test (`mc.calc.left-right-swapped`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Using right-endpoint Riemann sums, prove that $\int_0^b x^2\,dx = \frac{b^3}{3}$ for $b > 0$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Split $[0, b]$ into $n$ strips of width $\frac{b}{n}$ with right endpoints $x_k = \frac{kb}{n}$. (2) $R_n = \sum_{k=1}^n\left(\frac{kb}{n}\right)^2\frac{b}{n} = \frac{b^3}{n^3}\sum_{k=1}^nk^2$. (3) $\sum_{k=1}^nk^2 = \frac{n(n + 1)(2n + 1)}{6}$ (0.3 lesson 130). (4) $R_n = \frac{b^3}{6}\left(1 + \frac1n\right)\left(2 + \frac1n\right)$. (5) As $n \to \infty$, $R_n \to \frac{b^3}{6} \cdot 2 = \frac{b^3}{3}$. (6) The left sum is $L_n = R_n - \frac{b}{n} \cdot b^2$, with the same limit; since $x^2$ is increasing on $[0, b]$, $L_n \le$ area $\le R_n$, so the squeeze fixes the integral at $\frac{b^3}{3}$.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the partition (line 1). 2: the sum (line 2). 1: the formula cited (line 3). 2: simplification (line 4). 2: the limit (line 5). 2: the squeeze by the left sum (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* $f$ is increasing on $[a, b]$. With $n$ equal strips, prove $0 \le R_n - \int_a^bf \le \frac{(b - a)(f(b) - f(a))}{n}$.
  *Answer.* On each strip $[x_{k-1}, x_k]$, $f(x_{k-1}) \le f(x) \le f(x_k)$, so $L_n \le \int_a^bf \le R_n$. And $R_n - L_n = \frac{b - a}{n}\sum_{k=1}^n\left(f(x_k) - f(x_{k-1})\right) = \frac{b - a}{n}\left(f(b) - f(a)\right)$, a telescoping sum. Hence $0 \le R_n - \int_a^bf \le R_n - L_n$, which is the bound. Picture: the strips' excess rectangles slide sideways into one column of width $\frac{b - a}{n}$ and height $f(b) - f(a)$. Decision point: bounding each strip's error on its own leaves $n$ unknown terms; telescoping collapses them into one.

**I2 The fundamental theorem** (`set.integral.ftc`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: a proof of the fundamental theorem, part 1. Code drill `drl.calc.cdf-quantile` (d3, `needs: []`): `cdf(density, a, x, n=1000)` by Simpson's rule and `quantile(density, a, b, q)` by bisection on the CDF. Tests: for the density $3x^2$ on $[0, 1]$, `cdf(..., 0.5)` is 0.125 within $10^{-9}$ (visible; Simpson is exact for this integrand); the median is $0.5^{1/3} = 0.7937005259840998$ within $10^{-6}$. Edge tests: $q$ outside $(0, 1)$ raises `ValueError`; $x \le a$ gives 0. Known-wrong: bisection on `density(x) - q` instead of the CDF fails the median test (`mc.calc.density-as-cdf`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Let $f$ be continuous on $[a, b]$ and $A(x) = \int_a^xf(t)\,dt$. Prove that $A'(x) = f(x)$ for $a < x < b$.
  *Model answer.* (1) For small $h \ne 0$, $A(x + h) - A(x) = \int_x^{x + h}f(t)\,dt$ (additivity). (2) $f$ is continuous on the closed interval between $x$ and $x + h$, so it has a minimum $m_h$ and a maximum $M_h$ there (the extreme value theorem). (3) By comparison, $m_h \le \frac{A(x + h) - A(x)}{h} \le M_h$; for $h < 0$ both the integral and $h$ change sign, so the same holds. (4) As $h \to 0$ the interval shrinks to $x$; by continuity, $m_h \to f(x)$ and $M_h \to f(x)$. (5) By the squeeze theorem the difference quotient tends to $f(x)$: $A'(x) = f(x)$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: additivity (line 1). 2: the extreme value theorem named (line 2). 2: the bounds, with the $h < 0$ case (line 3). 2: continuity used (line 4). 2: the squeeze (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* Evaluate $\lim_{n \to \infty}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{n}{n^2 + k^2}$.
  *Answer.* $\frac{n}{n^2 + k^2} = \frac1n \cdot \frac{1}{1 + (k/n)^2}$, so the sum is a right Riemann sum of $f(x) = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}$ on $[0, 1]$ with $n$ strips. $f$ is continuous, so the sum tends to $\int_0^1\frac{dx}{1 + x^2} = \arctan 1 - \arctan 0 = \frac{\pi}{4}$. Decision point: the sum has no closed form and does not telescope; factoring out $\frac1n$ shows $\frac{k}{n}$ as a sample point.

**I3 Techniques of integration** (`set.integral.techniques`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5), all "exact". Long-form: the sine reduction formula. Code drill `drl.calc.antiderivative-check` (d3, `needs: []`): `is_antiderivative(F, f, xs, h=1e-5, rtol=1e-6)` and `differ_by_constant(F1, F2, xs)`. Tests: $F = \sin(x^2)$ for $f = 2x\cos(x^2)$ is `True` (visible); $F = x\ln x - x$ for $\ln x$ on $[0.5, 1, 2]$ is `True`; $F = x^2e^x$ for $xe^x$ is `False`; $\sin^2x$ and $-\cos^2x$ differ by a constant (`True`); $\sin^2x$ and $\cos 2x$ do not (`False`). Edge tests: an empty list of points raises `ValueError`; $F = \ln\lvert x \rvert$ for $\frac1x$ at $[-2, -1]$ is `True`. Known-wrong: `differ_by_constant` that tests $F_1 - F_2 = 0$ fails the $\sin^2x$, $-\cos^2x$ test (`mc.calc.antiderivative-unique`).
