- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Show that $I_n = \int_0^{\pi/2}\sin^nx\,dx$ satisfies $I_n = \frac{n - 1}{n}I_{n-2}$ for $n \ge 2$, and evaluate $I_4$ and $I_5$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Write $\sin^nx = \sin^{n-1}x\sin x$ and integrate by parts with $u = \sin^{n-1}x$, $dv = \sin x\,dx$, so $du = (n - 1)\sin^{n-2}x\cos x\,dx$ and $v = -\cos x$. (2) $I_n = \left[-\sin^{n-1}x\cos x\right]_0^{\pi/2} + (n - 1)\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin^{n-2}x\cos^2x\,dx$. (3) The bracket is 0 at both ends for $n \ge 2$. (4) With $\cos^2x = 1 - \sin^2x$: $I_n = (n - 1)(I_{n-2} - I_n)$. (5) So $nI_n = (n - 1)I_{n-2}$. (6) $I_0 = \frac{\pi}{2}$ and $I_1 = 1$, so $I_4 = \frac34 \cdot \frac12 \cdot \frac{\pi}{2} = \frac{3\pi}{16}$ and $I_5 = \frac45 \cdot \frac23 \cdot 1 = \frac{8}{15}$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the choice of parts (line 1). 1: the formula applied (line 2). 1: the boundary term (line 3). 2: the integral reappearing (line 4). 2: solving for it (line 5). 2: both values (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Evaluate $\int_0^{\pi/2}\frac{\sin x}{\sin x + \cos x}\,dx$.
  *Answer.* Call it $I$ and let $J = \int_0^{\pi/2}\frac{\cos x}{\sin x + \cos x}\,dx$. Then $I + J = \int_0^{\pi/2}1\,dx = \frac{\pi}{2}$. The substitution $x = \frac{\pi}{2} - u$ swaps $\sin$ and $\cos$ and maps the interval to itself, so $I = J$. Hence $I = \frac{\pi}{4}$. Decision point: substitution, parts and partial fractions stall (the half-angle substitution $t = \tan\frac{x}{2}$ works, after a page of algebra); the symmetry of $[0, \frac{\pi}{2}]$ under $x \mapsto \frac{\pi}{2} - x$ does it in two lines.

**I4 Improper integrals, densities and numerical integration** (`set.integral.improper`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the tail formula for expectations. Code drill `drl.calc.simpson` (d3, `needs: []`): `simpson(f, a, b, n)`. Tests: $\int_0^\pi\sin x\,dx$ with $n = 10$ has error between $5 \times 10^{-5}$ and $2 \times 10^{-4}$ (visible; it is $1.10 \times 10^{-4}$); the ratio of the errors at $n = 10$ and $n = 20$ lies in $[15, 17]$ (it is 16.1). Edge tests: $\int_0^2x^3\,dx$ with $n = 2$ is exactly 4; odd $n$ raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: the trapezoid rule fails the ratio test (its ratio is about 4; `mc.calc.simpson-is-trapezoid`); weights 2 and 4 swapped fails the cubic test (slip).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* $X \ge 0$ has a continuous density $f$ and a finite mean. Prove $E[X] = \int_0^\infty P(X > x)\,dx$, and use it to find the mean of an exponential variable with rate $\lambda$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Let $G(x) = P(X > x) = 1 - F(x)$, so $G' = -f$. (2) $E[X] = \lim_{T \to \infty}\int_0^Txf(x)\,dx$. (3) By parts with $u = x$ and $v = -G$: $\int_0^Txf(x)\,dx = \left[-xG(x)\right]_0^T + \int_0^TG(x)\,dx = -TG(T) + \int_0^TG(x)\,dx$. (4) $0 \le TG(T) = T\int_T^\infty f(x)\,dx \le \int_T^\infty xf(x)\,dx \to 0$, because the mean is finite. (5) So $E[X] = \int_0^\infty G(x)\,dx$. (6) For the exponential, $G(x) = e^{-\lambda x}$ and $E[X] = \int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda x}\,dx = \frac{1}{\lambda}$.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the tail function (line 1). 1: the mean as a limit (line 2). 3: parts with $v = -G$, not $v = F$, the creative step (line 3). 2: the boundary term killed by the finite mean (line 4). 1: the conclusion (line 5). 2: the exponential (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Evaluate $\int_0^1\frac{x - 1}{\ln x}\,dx$.
  *Answer.* Embed it in a family: $I(a) = \int_0^1\frac{x^a - 1}{\ln x}\,dx$ for $a \ge 0$, so $I(0) = 0$ and the integral is $I(1)$. Differentiating under the integral sign (allowed: $\frac{\partial}{\partial a}\frac{x^a - 1}{\ln x} = x^a$ is bounded by 1 on $[0, 1]$): $I'(a) = \int_0^1x^a\,dx = \frac{1}{a + 1}$. So $I(a) = \ln(a + 1) + C$ and $I(0) = 0$ gives $C = 0$. The integral is $\ln 2 \approx 0.6931$. Decision point: no substitution or parts tames $\frac{1}{\ln x}$; a parameter whose derivative cancels it does.

**I5 Geometry and physics of integrals** (`set.integral.applications`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: Gabriel's horn. Code drill `drl.calc.arc-length` (d3, `needs: []`): `arc_length(x, y, t0, t1, n)` returns the length of the polygon through $n + 1$ equally spaced parameter points. Tests: the quarter circle $(\cos t, \sin t)$ on $[0, \frac{\pi}{2}]$ with $n = 1000$ is within $10^{-6}$ of $\frac{\pi}{2}$ (visible); $(t, \cosh t)$ on $[-1, 1]$ with $n = 2000$ is within $10^{-6}$ of $2\sinh 1 = 2.3504023872876028$ (the miss is $7 \times 10^{-8}$). Edge tests: the segment $(t, 2t)$ on $[0, 1]$ with $n = 1$ is $\sqrt5$; $n < 1$ raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: summing $\lvert\Delta x\rvert + \lvert\Delta y\rvert$ fails the quarter-circle test (`mc.calc.arc-length-adds-legs`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* The curve $y = \frac1x$, $x \ge 1$, is rotated about the $x$-axis. Show the solid has finite volume and infinite surface area, and explain why this is not a contradiction.
  *Model answer.* (1) Discs of radius $\frac1x$. (2) $V = \int_1^\infty\pi\frac{1}{x^2}\,dx = \pi\lim_{T \to \infty}\left(1 - \frac1T\right) = \pi$. (3) $S = \int_1^\infty2\pi y\sqrt{1 + y'^2}\,dx$ with $y' = -\frac{1}{x^2}$. (4) $\sqrt{1 + \frac{1}{x^4}} \ge 1$, so $S \ge 2\pi\int_1^\infty\frac{dx}{x}$. (5) $\int_1^T\frac{dx}{x} = \ln T \to \infty$, so $S = \infty$ by comparison. (6) The "paint paradox": $\pi$ units of paint fill the horn, yet its surface cannot be painted. There is no contradiction: a coat of fixed thickness over an infinite area has infinite volume, while the paint filling the horn is thinner, far out, than any fixed coat.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the setup (line 1). 2: the volume (line 2). 2: the surface-area formula (line 3). 2: the comparison (lines 4, 5). 3: the explanation (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* (a) Show that the part of a ball of radius $R$ between the planes $x = a$ and $x = b$ ($-R \le a < b \le R$) has volume $\pi\left[R^2(b - a) - \frac{b^3 - a^3}{3}\right]$, and deduce that a cap of height $h$ has volume $\frac{\pi h^2(3R - h)}{3}$. (b) 0.3's homework S2 left this open: water stands 1 cm deep in a cylinder of radius 4 cm, and a solid ball of radius 3 cm is dropped in and rests on the bottom. Find the new depth.
  *Answer.* (a) Cross-sections perpendicular to the $x$-axis are discs of radius $\sqrt{R^2 - x^2}$, so the volume is $\int_a^b\pi(R^2 - x^2)\,dx = \pi\left[R^2(b - a) - \frac{b^3 - a^3}{3}\right]$; with $a = R - h$, $b = R$ this simplifies to $\frac{\pi h^2(3R - h)}{3}$. (b) Assuming full submersion gives a depth of 3.25 cm, below the ball's top at 6 cm: a contradiction, so the ball is only partly under water. For a depth $d < 6$, the water fills the cylinder up to $d$ minus the cap of the ball below $d$ (a cap of height $d$): $16\pi d - \frac{\pi d^2(9 - d)}{3} = 16\pi$. This simplifies to $d^3 - 9d^2 + 48d - 48 = 0$, whose left side is increasing (its derivative $3(d^2 - 6d + 16)$ has no real roots), so there is one root; Newton's method from 1.25 gives $d \approx 1.2536$ cm. Decision point: the displaced-volume shortcut silently assumes full submersion; the cap integral is what the honest answer needs.

**I6 Differential equations** (`set.integral.odes`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: mean reversion solved. Code drill `drl.calc.euler` (d3, `needs: []`): `euler(f, t0, y0, h, steps)` returns the list of `(t, y)`. Tests: $y' = y$ from $(0, 1)$ with $h = 0.1$ for 10 steps ends at $1.1^{10} = 2.5937424601$ (visible); the errors against $e$ for $h = 0.1$ and $h = 0.05$ have ratio in $[1.8, 2.2]$ (it is 1.92); $y' = t$ from $(0, 0)$ with $h = 0.5$ for 2 steps ends at $(1.0, 0.25)$; $y' = y(1 - y)$ from 0.1 with $h = 0.1$ for 100 steps stays in $(0, 1)$ and ends above 0.99. Edge tests: $y' = -10y$ with $h = 0.25$ grows in size (factor $-1.5$ per step); 0 steps returns `[(t0, y0)]`. Known-wrong: one that never advances $t$ fails the $y' = t$ test, which an equation without $t$ cannot catch (slip).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Solve $m'(t) = \theta(\mu - m)$, $m(0) = m_0$, with $\theta > 0$, using an integrating factor; show that $m \to \mu$ and that the time to close half the gap is $\frac{\ln 2}{\theta}$ whatever $m_0$ is.
  *Model answer.* (1) Rewrite as $m' + \theta m = \theta\mu$. (2) Multiply by $e^{\theta t}$: the left side becomes $(e^{\theta t}m)'$, so $(e^{\theta t}m)' = \theta\mu e^{\theta t}$. (3) Integrate: $e^{\theta t}m = \mu e^{\theta t} + C$. (4) So $m(t) = \mu + Ce^{-\theta t}$, and $m(0) = m_0$ gives $C = m_0 - \mu$. (5) $m(t) = \mu + (m_0 - \mu)e^{-\theta t} \to \mu$ as $t \to \infty$, because $\theta > 0$. (6) The gap $m - \mu$ halves when $e^{-\theta t} = \frac12$, at $t = \frac{\ln 2}{\theta}$, with no $m_0$ in it. (7) Check: $m' = -\theta(m_0 - \mu)e^{-\theta t} = \theta(\mu - m)$.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: standard form (line 1). 2: the integrating factor and why it works (line 2). 1: integration (line 3). 2: the constant (line 4). 1: the limit (line 5). 2: the half-life (line 6). 1: the check (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A fish population (in units of the habitat's capacity) follows $P' = P(1 - P) - h$ under a constant harvest $h$. Find the equilibria and their stability for $h = \frac{3}{16}$, and describe what happens for $h > \frac14$.
  *Answer.* Equilibria solve $P - P^2 - h = 0$: $P = \frac{1 \pm \sqrt{1 - 4h}}{2}$, which exist only for $h \le \frac14$. For $h = \frac{3}{16}$: $P = \frac14$ and $\frac34$. The sign of $g(P) = P - P^2 - h$ is negative below $\frac14$, positive between, negative above $\frac34$: populations between rise to $\frac34$ and populations above fall to it (stable), while a population below $\frac14$ falls to extinction ($\frac14$ is unstable). For $h > \frac14$, $g(P) < 0$ for every $P$: the population declines whatever its size and is wiped out in finite time. Decision point: solving the equation in closed form is possible and unnecessary; the sign of the right-hand side answers every question, and it shows how a harvest just above $\frac14$ collapses a fishery that looked healthy at $h = 0.24$.

**X1 Convergence** (`set.series.convergence`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the integral test for the harmonic series. Code drill `drl.calc.harmonic` (d3, `needs: []`): `terms_to_exceed(T)` returns the least $n$ with $H_n > T$; `harmonic_bounds(n)` returns $(\ln(n + 1), 1 + \ln n)$. Tests: $T = 2 \to 4$ (visible); $T = 3 \to 11$; $T = 10 \to 12{,}367$; $H_{100} = 5.187\ldots$ lies inside `harmonic_bounds(100)`. Edge tests: $T = 0.5 \to 1$; $T = 1.5 \to 3$, because $H_2 = 1.5$ exactly and the inequality is strict. Known-wrong: returning the last $n$ with $H_n \le T$ fails the first test with 3 (slip).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove $\ln(n + 1) \le H_n \le 1 + \ln n$, where $H_n = \sum_{k=1}^n\frac1k$, deduce that the harmonic series diverges, and bound $H_{100}$.
  *Model answer.* (1) $f(x) = \frac1x$ is positive, continuous and decreasing on $[1, \infty)$. (2) On $[k, k + 1]$, $\frac{1}{k + 1} \le \frac1x \le \frac1k$, so $\frac{1}{k + 1} \le \int_k^{k + 1}\frac{dx}{x} \le \frac1k$: each rectangle sits above or below the curve. (3) Summing the right inequality for $k = 1, \dots, n$: $H_n \ge \int_1^{n + 1}\frac{dx}{x} = \ln(n + 1)$. (4) Summing the left one for $k = 1, \dots, n - 1$: $H_n - 1 \le \int_1^n\frac{dx}{x} = \ln n$. (5) So $\ln(n + 1) \le H_n \le 1 + \ln n$, and since $\ln(n + 1) \to \infty$, the series diverges. (6) For $n = 100$: $4.615 \le H_{100} \le 5.605$; the true value is $5.187$.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the conditions (line 1). 3: the rectangle comparison, with a picture (line 2). 2: both sums (lines 3, 4). 2: divergence (line 5). 2: the numbers (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* For which $p$ does $\sum_{n \ge 2}\frac{1}{n(\ln n)^p}$ converge?
  *Answer.* For $p \le 0$ the terms are at least $\frac1n$ once $\ln n \ge 1$, so the series diverges by comparison. For $p > 0$, $f(x) = \frac{1}{x(\ln x)^p}$ is positive, continuous and decreasing on $[2, \infty)$, and with $u = \ln x$, $du = \frac{dx}{x}$: $\int_2^T\frac{dx}{x(\ln x)^p} = \int_{\ln 2}^{\ln T}\frac{du}{u^p}$, which has a finite limit as $T \to \infty$ exactly when $p > 1$. So the series converges if and only if $p > 1$. Decision point: comparison with $\frac{1}{n^{1 + \varepsilon}}$ fails for every $\varepsilon > 0$ (the terms are eventually larger), and the ratio test is silent; the integral test with $u = \ln x$ is the tool.

**X2 Power series and asymptotics** (`set.series.power`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the mean of the geometric distribution. Code drill `drl.calc.stirling` (d3, `needs: []`): `stirling(n)`, `rel_error(n)` computed through `math.lgamma` and `math.expm1` so that large $n$ neither overflows nor cancels, and `digits_of_factorial(n)`. Tests: `rel_error(10)` is $0.008365$ within $10^{-6}$ (visible); $12n \cdot$ `rel_error(n)` is within $10^{-3}$ of 1 at $n = 1000$; `digits_of_factorial(100)` is 158. Edge tests: $n < 1$ raises `ValueError`; `digits_of_factorial(1)` is 1. Known-wrong: $\lfloor\log_{10}n!\rfloor$ without the $+1$ fails the 158 test with 157 (`mc.calc.digits-is-log`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* $N$ counts the trials up to and including the first success, with success probability $p \in (0, 1]$ on each independent trial. Prove $E[N] = \frac1p$ by differentiating a geometric series, and justify the step.
  *Model answer.* (1) $P(N = k) = q^{k-1}p$ for $k \ge 1$, where $q = 1 - p$. (2) $E[N] = \sum_{k \ge 1}kq^{k-1}p$. (3) For $\lvert x \rvert < 1$, $\sum_{k \ge 0}x^k = \frac{1}{1 - x}$. (4) A power series may be differentiated term by term inside its radius of convergence (here 1), so $\sum_{k \ge 1}kx^{k-1} = \frac{1}{(1 - x)^2}$ for $\lvert x \rvert < 1$. (5) At $x = q$, which satisfies $0 \le q < 1$: $\sum_{k \ge 1}kq^{k-1} = \frac{1}{p^2}$. (6) So $E[N] = p \cdot \frac{1}{p^2} = \frac1p$. (7) Checks: a fair coin needs 2 tosses on average for its first head; $p = 1$ gives 1.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the distribution (line 1). 1: the expectation as a series (line 2). 1: the geometric series (line 3). 3: term-by-term differentiation justified by the radius (line 4). 2: evaluation (lines 5, 6). 2: checks (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Find the radius and the exact interval of convergence of $\sum_{n \ge 0}\binom{2n}{n}x^n$.
  *Answer.* The ratio of coefficients is $\frac{(2n + 2)(2n + 1)}{(n + 1)^2} \to 4$, so the radius is $\frac14$. At $x = \frac14$ the terms are $\frac{\binom{2n}{n}}{4^n} \sim \frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi n}}$ (lesson 98), so the series diverges by limit comparison with $\sum\frac{1}{\sqrt n}$. At $x = -\frac14$ the terms alternate, their sizes decrease (ratio $\frac{2n + 1}{2n + 2} < 1$) and tend to 0, so the series converges, conditionally. The interval is $\left[-\frac14, \frac14\right)$; the sum is $\frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - 4x}}$, which gives $\frac{1}{\sqrt2}$ at $x = -\frac14$. Decision point: the ratio test fixes the radius and is silent at both ends; Stirling's estimate settles them.

**X3 Taylor series** (`set.series.taylor`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the exponential series converges to $e^x$. Code drill `drl.calc.my-exp` (d4, `needs: []`): `my_exp(x)` by range reduction $x = k\ln 2 + r$ with $\lvert r \rvert \le \frac{\ln 2}{2}$ and a Taylor polynomial in $r$. Tests: relative error against `math.exp` below $10^{-14}$ at $x = -50, -1, 0.5, 1, 10, 50$ (visible: $x = 1$; the largest error measured is $1.2 \times 10^{-15}$ at 50); at most 20 terms are used for $\lvert r \rvert \le 0.35$ (13 are). Edge tests: `my_exp(0) == 1.0` exactly; `my_exp(1000)` raises `OverflowError` from `math.ldexp` (measured on CPython 3.11; CI confirms it on Pyodide, where WebAssembly has no floating-point flags [check]). Known-wrong: a plain Taylor sum without range reduction fails the $x = -50$ test (catastrophic cancellation; `mc.calc.series-safe-everywhere`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Use Taylor's theorem with the Lagrange remainder to show that the Maclaurin series of $e^x$ converges to $e^x$ for every real $x$, and find how many terms give $e$ to within $10^{-6}$.
  *Model answer.* (1) $e^x = \sum_{k=0}^n\frac{x^k}{k!} + R_n(x)$ with $R_n(x) = \frac{e^c x^{n+1}}{(n + 1)!}$ for some $c$ between 0 and $x$. (2) So $\lvert R_n(x) \rvert \le \frac{e^{\lvert x \rvert}\lvert x \rvert^{n+1}}{(n + 1)!}$. (3) For fixed $x$, $\frac{\lvert x \rvert^{n+1}}{(n + 1)!} \to 0$ (factorials outgrow powers, lesson 41), so $R_n(x) \to 0$: the series converges to $e^x$. (4) At $x = 1$: $0 < R_n(1) \le \frac{e}{(n + 1)!} < \frac{3}{(n + 1)!}$, using $e < 3$ (lesson 6). (5) $\frac{3}{(n + 1)!} < 10^{-6}$ needs $(n + 1)! > 3 \times 10^6$: $9! = 362{,}880$ is too small and $10! = 3{,}628{,}800$ is enough, so $n = 9$, ten terms ($k = 0$ to 9). (6) Check: $e - \sum_{k=0}^9\frac{1}{k!} \approx 3.0 \times 10^{-7}$, while nine terms leave $3.1 \times 10^{-6}$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the theorem (line 1). 1: the bound (line 2). 2: convergence for every $x$ (line 3). 2: the bound at 1 with $e < 3$ justified (line 4). 2: the count (line 5). 1: the check (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Prove that $e$ is irrational.
  *Answer.* Suppose $e = \frac{a}{b}$ with whole $a, b \ge 1$. Let $x = b!\left(e - \sum_{k=0}^b\frac{1}{k!}\right)$. Then $b!e = (b - 1)!\,a$ is whole and each $\frac{b!}{k!}$ with $k \le b$ is whole, so $x$ is a whole number; and $x > 0$, because the omitted terms are positive. But $x = b!\sum_{k > b}\frac{1}{k!} = \frac{1}{b + 1} + \frac{1}{(b + 1)(b + 2)} + \dots < \frac{1}{b + 1} + \frac{1}{(b + 1)^2} + \dots = \frac1b \le 1$. A whole number strictly between 0 and 1 is impossible, so $e$ is irrational. Decision point: comparing $e$ with fractions directly leads nowhere; multiplying by $b!$ makes every early term whole and leaves a tail that a geometric series bounds below 1.

**M1 Functions of several variables** (`set.multivar.surfaces`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: partial derivatives without continuity. Code drill `drl.calc.numeric-partials` (d3, `needs: []`): `grad(f, p, h=1e-5)` and `hessian(f, p, h=1e-4)` by central differences, for any number of variables. Tests: for $f = x^2y + y^3$ at $(1, 2)$, `grad` is $(4, 13)$ within $10^{-6}$ (visible) and `hessian` is $\begin{pmatrix}4 & 2\\ 2 & 12\end{pmatrix}$ within $10^{-4}$; for $\sin x\,e^y$ at the origin, `grad` is $(1, 0)$; `hessian` is symmetric to $10^{-6}$. Edge tests: a constant function has gradient $(0, 0)$; $xyz$ at $(1, 2, 3)$ has gradient $(6, 3, 2)$. Known-wrong: forward differences fail the first test (errors about $2 \times 10^{-5}$ in $f_x$ and $6 \times 10^{-5}$ in $f_y$; `mc.calc.forward-is-accurate-enough`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Let $f(x, y) = \frac{xy}{x^2 + y^2}$ for $(x, y) \ne (0, 0)$ and $f(0, 0) = 0$. Show that both partial derivatives exist at the origin, that $f$ is not continuous there, and explain what this says about partial derivatives.
  *Model answer.* (1) $f(x, 0) = 0$ for every $x$, so $f_x(0, 0) = \lim_{h \to 0}\frac{f(h, 0) - f(0, 0)}{h} = 0$. (2) Likewise $f(0, y) = 0$, so $f_y(0, 0) = 0$. (3) Along $y = x$ with $x \ne 0$: $f(x, x) = \frac{x^2}{2x^2} = \frac12$. (4) So there are points as close to the origin as we like where $f = \frac12$, while $f(0, 0) = 0$: $f$ is not continuous at the origin. (5) A differentiable function is continuous (the two-variable version of D2's example A), so $f$ is not differentiable at the origin even though both partials exist. (6) Partial derivatives look only along the two axes; differentiability needs one linear approximation that works in every direction at once.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: both partials (lines 1, 2). 2: the diagonal values (line 3). 2: discontinuity (line 4). 2: not differentiable, with the reason (line 5). 2: the interpretation (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Verify that $u(x, t) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi t}}e^{-x^2/(4t)}$ satisfies $u_t = u_{xx}$ for $t > 0$, and describe $u$ as $t \to 0^+$ and as $t$ grows.
  *Answer.* $\ln u = -\frac12\ln(4\pi t) - \frac{x^2}{4t}$, so $\frac{u_t}{u} = -\frac{1}{2t} + \frac{x^2}{4t^2}$. Also $\frac{u_x}{u} = -\frac{x}{2t}$, so $u_x = -\frac{x}{2t}u$ and $u_{xx} = -\frac{1}{2t}u + \frac{x^2}{4t^2}u = u_t$. For each $t$, $u$ is a normal density with mean 0 and variance $2t$: as $t \to 0^+$ it concentrates into a spike of total area 1 at the origin (area 1 by the Gaussian integral, lesson 133), and as $t$ grows it spreads and flattens: heat diffusing from a point. Decision point: direct differentiation by the product and chain rules is long and error-prone; logarithmic differentiation (lesson 20) makes each side one line. The Black-Scholes equation becomes exactly this equation after a change of variables (3.2).

**M2 The chain rule and the gradient** (`set.multivar.gradient`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the gradient's two geometric facts. Code drill `drl.calc.neuron-backprop` (d4, `needs: []`): `forward_backward(w, b, x, t)` returns `(loss, dL_dw, dL_db)` for $\hat y = \sigma(wx + b)$ and $L = (\hat y - t)^2$. Tests: $(0, 0, 1, 1)$ gives loss $0.25$ and gradients $(-0.25, -0.25)$ (visible); at 20 points drawn from `random.Random(7)` the gradients agree with central differences within $10^{-6}$. Edge tests: at $(20, 0, 1, 0)$ both gradients are below $10^{-7}$ in size (a saturated neuron); at $(0, 0, 1, 0.5)$ loss and gradients are exactly 0. Known-wrong: a solution that forgets $\sigma'$ fails the first test (`mc.calc.chain-factor-dropped`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove that $\nabla f$ at a point is perpendicular to the level curve through it (when $\nabla f \ne \mathbf 0$), and that among unit vectors $\mathbf u$ the directional derivative $D_{\mathbf u}f$ is largest when $\mathbf u$ points along $\nabla f$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Let $\mathbf r(t) = (x(t), y(t))$ trace the level curve $f(x, y) = c$ through the point $P = \mathbf r(0)$, with $\mathbf r'(0) \ne \mathbf 0$. (2) Then $f(\mathbf r(t)) = c$ for all $t$; by the chain rule, $f_xx'(t) + f_yy'(t) = 0$, that is $\nabla f \cdot \mathbf r'(t) = 0$. (3) $\mathbf r'(0)$ is tangent to the level curve at $P$, so $\nabla f(P)$ is perpendicular to it. (4) For a unit vector $\mathbf u$, $D_{\mathbf u}f = \nabla f \cdot \mathbf u = \lvert\nabla f\rvert\cos\theta$, where $\theta$ is the angle between them. (5) This is largest, $\lvert\nabla f\rvert$, at $\theta = 0$, smallest, $-\lvert\nabla f\rvert$, at $\theta = \pi$, and 0 at $\theta = \frac{\pi}{2}$, along the level curve, which agrees with line 3.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the path on the level curve (line 1). 2: the chain rule (line 2). 2: perpendicularity (line 3). 2: the dot-product form (line 4). 2: the extremes and the consistency check (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* Let $w = x^2 + y^2 + z^2$, where the variables are linked by $z = x^2 + y^2$. At $(1, 1, 2)$ find $\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)_y$ and $\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)_z$.
  *Answer.* Holding $y$ fixed, $z$ moves with $x$: $w = x^2 + y^2 + (x^2 + y^2)^2$, so $\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)_y = 2x + 2(x^2 + y^2)(2x) = 2 + 8 = 10$. Holding $z$ fixed, $y^2 = z - x^2$ moves with $x$: $w = x^2 + (z - x^2) + z^2 = z + z^2$, which does not depend on $x$, so $\left(\frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\right)_z = 0$. Same point, same $w$, two answers, because "how does $w$ change as $x$ moves" depends on what else is held still. Decision point: $\frac{\partial w}{\partial x} = 2x = 2$, treating $x$, $y$ and $z$ as free, is wrong for both, because the constraint ties them.

**M3 Optimisation in several variables** (`set.multivar.optimise`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the least squares line. Code drill `drl.calc.fit-line` (d3, `needs: []`): `fit_line(xs, ys)` returns $(a, b)$ for $y = a + bx$. Tests: five points on $y = 2 + 3x$ give $(2, 3)$ (visible); the points $(0, 1), (1, 3), (2, 2), (3, 5)$ give $(1.1, 1.1)$. Edge tests: all $x_i$ equal raises `ValueError`; lists of different lengths raise `ValueError`. Known-wrong: the slope $\frac{\sum x_iy_i}{\sum x_i^2}$ of a line forced through the origin fails the second test with $\frac{11}{7}$ (`mc.calc.fit-through-origin`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Fit $y = a + bx$ to data $(x_i, y_i)$, $i = 1, \dots, n$, by minimising $S(a, b) = \sum(y_i - a - bx_i)^2$. Derive the normal equations, show $b = \frac{\sum(x_i - \bar x)(y_i - \bar y)}{\sum(x_i - \bar x)^2}$, and prove the critical point is the global minimum unless all $x_i$ are equal.
  *Model answer.* (1) $S(a, b) = \sum(y_i - a - bx_i)^2$. (2) $\frac{\partial S}{\partial a} = -2\sum(y_i - a - bx_i) = 0$ gives $\bar y = a + b\bar x$. (3) $\frac{\partial S}{\partial b} = -2\sum x_i(y_i - a - bx_i) = 0$ gives $\sum x_iy_i = a\sum x_i + b\sum x_i^2$. (4) Substituting $a = \bar y - b\bar x$ into line 3 and rearranging: $b = \frac{\sum(x_i - \bar x)(y_i - \bar y)}{\sum(x_i - \bar x)^2} = \frac{\operatorname{cov}(x, y)}{\operatorname{var}(x)}$. (5) The Hessian is $\begin{pmatrix}2n & 2\sum x_i\\ 2\sum x_i & 2\sum x_i^2\end{pmatrix}$ with $S_{aa} = 2n > 0$ and determinant $4\left(n\sum x_i^2 - (\sum x_i)^2\right) = 4n\sum(x_i - \bar x)^2 > 0$ unless all $x_i$ are equal. (6) So the critical point is a local minimum; $S$ is a quadratic with this constant positive definite Hessian, so it is convex and the minimum is global. If every $x_i$ is equal the determinant is 0 and the slope is not determined.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the objective (line 1). 2: both normal equations (lines 2, 3). 2: the slope formula (line 4). 3: the Hessian test (line 5). 2: global, and the degenerate case (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Find and classify the critical points of $f(x, y) = x^3 + y^3 - 3xy$, and find the global maximum and minimum of $f$ on the square $0 \le x, y \le 2$.
  *Answer.* $f_x = 3x^2 - 3y = 0$ and $f_y = 3y^2 - 3x = 0$ give $y = x^2$ and $x = y^2$, so $x = x^4$: $(0, 0)$ and $(1, 1)$. Hessian $\begin{pmatrix}6x & -3\\ -3 & 6y\end{pmatrix}$: at $(0, 0)$, $D = -9 < 0$, a saddle; at $(1, 1)$, $D = 27 > 0$ with $f_{xx} > 0$, a local minimum, $f = -1$. Boundary: on $x = 0$, $f = y^3 \in [0, 8]$; on $y = 0$, $f = x^3 \in [0, 8]$; on $x = 2$, $f = 8 + y^3 - 6y$, with a critical point at $y = \sqrt2$ giving $8 - 4\sqrt2 \approx 2.34$ and end values 8 and 4; $y = 2$ is the same by symmetry. Global maximum 8 at $(2, 0)$ and $(0, 2)$; global minimum $-1$ at $(1, 1)$. Decision point: neither the interior saddle nor the boundary minimum $8 - 4\sqrt2$ is the answer; only a full list of candidates settles it.

**M4 Lagrange multipliers** (`set.multivar.lagrange`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the minimum-variance portfolio. Code drill `drl.calc.min-variance` (d4, `needs: []`): `min_var_weights(sigmas)` for uncorrelated assets, `portfolio_vol(weights, sigmas)` and `shadow_price(sigmas)` returning $\frac{dV^*}{dc}$ at $c = 1$. Tests: $(0.1, 0.2, 0.3) \to (\frac{36}{49}, \frac{9}{49}, \frac{4}{49})$ within $10^{-12}$, with volatility $\frac{3}{35}$ (visible); `shadow_price((0.1, 0.2, 0.3))` is $\frac{18}{1225} \approx 0.014694$ and matches a central difference in $c$ within $10^{-8}$. Edge tests: equal sigmas give equal weights; a zero sigma raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: weights proportional to $\frac1\sigma$ instead of $\frac1{\sigma^2}$ fail the first test (`mc.calc.inverse-vol-weights`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* $n$ uncorrelated assets have volatilities $\sigma_1, \dots, \sigma_n > 0$. Minimise the variance $V = \sum w_i^2\sigma_i^2$ of a fully invested portfolio ($\sum w_i = 1$) with a Lagrange multiplier; find the weights, the minimum variance, and show that the multiplier is the rate at which the minimum variance grows with the budget.
  *Model answer.* (1) Minimise $V(\mathbf w) = \sum w_i^2\sigma_i^2$ subject to $g(\mathbf w) = \sum w_i = 1$. (2) $\nabla V = \lambda\nabla g$: $2w_i\sigma_i^2 = \lambda$ for every $i$. (3) So $w_i = \frac{\lambda}{2\sigma_i^2}$: weights proportional to $\frac{1}{\sigma_i^2}$. (4) The constraint gives $\frac{\lambda}{2}\sum\frac{1}{\sigma_j^2} = 1$, so $\lambda = \frac{2}{\sum 1/\sigma_j^2}$ and $w_i = \frac{1/\sigma_i^2}{\sum 1/\sigma_j^2}$. (5) $V^* = \sum\left(\frac{\lambda}{2\sigma_i^2}\right)^2\sigma_i^2 = \frac{\lambda^2}{4}\sum\frac{1}{\sigma_i^2} = \frac{1}{\sum 1/\sigma_j^2}$. (6) $V$ is a sum of convex squares, so the critical point on the linear constraint is the global minimum. (7) With budget $c$, the same steps give $V^*(c) = \frac{c^2}{\sum 1/\sigma_j^2}$, so $\frac{dV^*}{dc}\big|_{c=1} = \frac{2}{\sum 1/\sigma_j^2} = \lambda$. (8) For 10%, 20%, 30%: weights $\frac{36}{49}, \frac{9}{49}, \frac{4}{49}$ and volatility $\frac{3}{35} \approx 8.6\%$, below the least risky asset's 10%.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the problem (line 1). 2: the Lagrange conditions and their meaning (lines 2, 3). 2: the multiplier and weights (line 4). 1: the minimum (line 5). 1: global (line 6). 2: the shadow price (line 7). 1: the example (line 8).
- *Example B (d5).* A six-sided die has mean face value 4.5 (not the fair 3.5). Among all distributions $(p_1, \dots, p_6)$ with that mean, find the one with the greatest entropy $H = -\sum p_i\ln p_i$.
  *Answer.* Lagrange with two constraints, $\sum p_i = 1$ and $\sum ip_i = 4.5$: $-\ln p_i - 1 = \lambda + \mu i$, so $p_i \propto e^{\beta i}$ with $\beta = -\mu$, and $p_i = \frac{e^{\beta i}}{\sum_je^{\beta j}}$. The mean of this family increases with $\beta$ and is 3.5 at $\beta = 0$, so $\beta > 0$; solving for mean 4.5 numerically gives $\beta \approx 0.371$ and $p \approx (0.054, 0.079, 0.114, 0.165, 0.240, 0.347)$. $H$ is strictly concave, so this is the global maximum. Decision point: the Lagrange conditions fix the form, exponential in the face value, but not the number; the multiplier comes from a one-dimensional root search (lesson 33). The same form is the softmax function of 2.1.

**M5 Double integrals** (`set.multivar.double`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the Gaussian integral. Code drill `drl.calc.monte-carlo-disc` (d3, `needs: []`): `mc_disc_integral(f, n, seed)` estimates $\iint_{x^2 + y^2 \le 1}f\,dA$ from points $x = 2u_1 - 1$, $y = 2u_2 - 1$ with $u_1, u_2$ drawn in turn from `random.Random(seed).random()`, and reports its standard error. Tests: $f = 1$ with $n = 100{,}000$ and seed 1 is within 0.021 of $\pi$, four standard errors (visible; the estimate is 3.13944); $f = x^2 + y^2$ is within four standard errors of $\frac{\pi}{2}$. Edge tests: the same seed reproduces the same estimate; $n \le 0$ raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: forgetting the square's area 4 fails the first test with about $\frac{\pi}{4}$ (`mc.calc.mc-forgets-volume`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove $\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-x^2}\,dx = \sqrt\pi$, and deduce the constant in the standard normal density.
  *Model answer.* (1) The integral $I$ converges: $e^{-x^2} \le e^{-\lvert x \rvert}$ for $\lvert x \rvert \ge 1$, and $\int e^{-\lvert x \rvert}\,dx$ converges. (2) $e^{-x^2}$ has no elementary antiderivative (lesson 69), so the one-variable route is blocked. (3) The leap: $I^2 = \int e^{-x^2}dx\int e^{-y^2}dy = \iint_{\mathbb R^2}e^{-(x^2 + y^2)}\,dA$, by the factorisation of lesson 129 (Fubini, valid as the integrand is positive). (4) The integrand depends only on $x^2 + y^2 = r^2$, so use polar coordinates: $dA = r\,dr\,d\theta$. (5) $I^2 = \int_0^{2\pi}\int_0^\infty e^{-r^2}r\,dr\,d\theta$; the extra $r$ makes the inner integral elementary: $\int_0^\infty re^{-r^2}\,dr = \frac12$. (6) So $I^2 = 2\pi \cdot \frac12 = \pi$, and $I > 0$ gives $I = \sqrt\pi$. (7) With $x = \frac{t}{\sqrt2}$: $\int e^{-t^2/2}\,dt = \sqrt{2\pi}$, which is why the standard normal density is $\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-t^2/2}$.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: convergence (line 1). 1: why the direct route fails (line 2). 3: the squaring step and its justification, the creative step (line 3). 1: the switch to polar (line 4). 2: the role of $r$ (line 5). 1: the sign (line 6). 1: the normal constant (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* $X$ and $Y$ are independent standard normal variables and $R = \sqrt{X^2 + Y^2}$. Find $P(R \le r)$, the density of $R$, and $E[R]$.
  *Answer.* The joint density is $\frac{1}{2\pi}e^{-(x^2 + y^2)/2}$. In polar coordinates, $P(R \le r) = \int_0^{2\pi}\int_0^r\frac{1}{2\pi}e^{-s^2/2}s\,ds\,d\theta = 1 - e^{-r^2/2}$. Differentiating, $f_R(r) = re^{-r^2/2}$ for $r \ge 0$. Then $E[R] = \int_0^\infty r^2e^{-r^2/2}\,dr$; by parts with $u = r$ and $dv = re^{-r^2/2}dr$ this is $\int_0^\infty e^{-r^2/2}\,dr = \frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{2} = \sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}} \approx 1.2533$. Decision point: in $x$ and $y$ the disc gives square-root limits and an integrand with no antiderivative; in polar coordinates the probability takes one line.

**M6 Change of variables and probability** (`set.multivar.change`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the polar Jacobian. Code drill `drl.calc.jacobian` (d3, `needs: []`): `jacobian_det(F, u, v, h=1e-6)` by central differences, and `area_factor` as its absolute value. Tests: the polar map at $(r, \theta) = (2, 0.7)$ gives 2 within $10^{-6}$ (visible); the map $(u + v, u - v)$ gives $-2$ with area factor 2. Edge tests: the identity map gives 1; the map $(u + v, 2u + 2v)$ gives 0 (it squashes the plane onto a line). Known-wrong: `area_factor` without the absolute value fails the $(u + v, u - v)$ test (`mc.calc.jacobian-sign`).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For $x = r\cos\theta$, $y = r\sin\theta$, compute the Jacobian determinant, explain geometrically why $dA = r\,dr\,d\theta$, why the absolute value is taken in general, and how a joint density changes under the map.
  *Model answer.* (1) The map $(r, \theta) \mapsto (r\cos\theta, r\sin\theta)$. (2) $J = \begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta & -r\sin\theta\\ \sin\theta & r\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}$. (3) $\det J = r\cos^2\theta + r\sin^2\theta = r$. (4) A small rectangle $dr \times d\theta$ maps to an almost-parallelogram spanned by $J$'s columns times $dr$ and $d\theta$, whose area is $\lvert\det J\rvert\,dr\,d\theta$ (0.3's determinant as an area scale factor); here $r\,dr\,d\theta$, a ring sector of width $dr$ and arc length $r\,d\theta$. (5) The absolute value: areas are positive, but a map that reverses orientation (such as swapping the two coordinates) has a negative determinant. (6) If $(X, Y)$ has density $f_{X,Y}$, then $(R, \Theta)$ has density $f_{X,Y}(r\cos\theta, r\sin\theta)\,r$: probability in a small region is preserved while the region's area is scaled by $r$.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the map (line 1). 2: the matrix (line 2). 1: the determinant (line 3). 3: the geometric reason (line 4). 1: the absolute value (line 5). 2: the density rule (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Find the volume of the region inside the sphere $x^2 + y^2 + z^2 = 4$ and above the cone $z = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$.
  *Answer.* In spherical coordinates the sphere is $\rho = 2$ and the cone is $\phi = \frac{\pi}{4}$, so every limit is constant: $V = \int_0^{2\pi}\int_0^{\pi/4}\int_0^2\rho^2\sin\phi\,d\rho\,d\phi\,d\theta = 2\pi\left(1 - \cos\frac{\pi}{4}\right)\frac83 = \frac{8\pi}{3}(2 - \sqrt2) \approx 4.907$. Check in cylindrical coordinates: $\int_0^{2\pi}\int_0^{\sqrt2}\left(\sqrt{4 - r^2} - r\right)r\,dr\,d\theta$ gives the same. Decision point: in Cartesian coordinates the bounds are square roots inside square roots; the region is a rectangle in spherical coordinates.

### 3.2 The Calculus Lab project (1.3, 14.75 hours)

A repository the learner builds on their own machine and the web app, with pytest tests and a one-page results note, as every project from 1.2 onwards (curriculum 1.2 exit; Stage 2 rules). Its drills declare `needs: ['numpy', 'scipy', 'matplotlib']`. NumPy runs in the Artifact [measured: `spikes/README.md`]; whether SciPy and matplotlib ship there is `arch-decision.md`'s per-course budget decision (open item 3), so until it is settled the project is built on the web app or locally, as projects are anyway. Its tests check `np.isfinite` and never rely on a NumPy warning, because in WebAssembly NumPy raises no overflow, divide or invalid warnings (`stage1-python-tools.md` draft 2, request 4). Each part has a checklist with paste-verify of `pytest -q` and its figures (`setup-checklist` project mode, assessment.md 2.9).

| Part | Hours | After level | Builds | Tests and figures |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1. Derivatives three ways | 3.5 | D5 | Forward and central differences, a dual-number class (from the D5 drill, extended to NumPy arrays), and a comparison against hand-derived derivatives for six functions | Error against $h$ on log-log axes showing slopes 1 and 2 and the round-off floor; the best $h$ for each rule within a factor of 10 of $\sqrt\epsilon$ and $\sqrt[3]\epsilon$; dual-number derivatives equal to hand derivatives to $10^{-14}$ |
| 2. Roots and yields | 3.75 | U1 | Bisection, Newton and the secant method; a bond's yield to maturity from its price | Iteration counts against tolerance (linear, quadratic, superlinear); a failing Newton start caught and reported; yields agree with `scipy.optimize.brentq` to $10^{-10}$ |
| 3. Integrals and expectations | 3.75 | I4 | Trapezoid, Simpson and Monte Carlo integration; the normal CDF and $E[\max(Z - k, 0)]$ for a standard normal $Z$ | Error against $n$ on log-log axes with slopes $-2$, $-4$ and $-\frac12$; $\Phi$ against `math.erf` to $10^{-10}$; the expectation against its closed form $\varphi(k) - k(1 - \Phi(k))$ |
| 4. Gradients for learning | 3.75 | M4 | A scalar reverse-mode automatic differentiation class (about 60 lines, in the spirit of Karpathy's micrograd) with a gradient check; logistic regression on a seeded two-dimensional data set by gradient descent; the minimum-variance portfolio of three correlated assets by Lagrange multipliers in closed form against `scipy.optimize.minimize` | Gradient check within $10^{-6}$; training loss falls monotonically for a stable learning rate and diverges above the bound of lesson 117; portfolio weights agree to $10^{-6}$ |

The results note answers four questions in plain words: which finite-difference step a bank should use and why; when Newton's method is worth its derivative; why Monte Carlo is slow in one dimension and still used in many; and what the learning-rate bound says about the data's curvature.

### 3.3 Practice drills (4 hours)

Eight drills of about 30 minutes, standard library only (`needs: []`), offered under Practice once their level is done and counted in the projects-and-drills block (decision 18). They are not homework: no due date, no gate, and the Redo queue takes only a failed submission. Each is a numerical method a quant uses, placed at a level whose homework drill does something else. Every value below was reproduced by a reference solution, and every known-wrong solution failed its named test (section 6).

| Drill | After | Task | Tests (first visible) and edge tests | Known-wrong |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `drl.calc.richardson` | D5 | `richardson(f, a, h)` returns $\frac{4D(h/2) - D(h)}{3}$, where $D$ is the central difference | For $e^x$ at 0 the errors at $h = 0.1$ and $0.05$ are $2.1 \times 10^{-7}$ and $1.3 \times 10^{-8}$, ratio in $[15, 17]$ (16.0: fourth order). Edge: $h = 0$ raises `ValueError`; a cubic is differentiated to within $10^{-12}$ | Weights 2 and $-1$ in place of $\frac43$ and $-\frac13$ fail the ratio test |
| `drl.calc.secant-yield` | U1 | `secant_yield(price, coupon, years)` finds an annual-pay bond's yield by the secant method from $y = 0$ and $0.1$ | A 5-year 5% bond at 100 yields 0.05; at 95.78763621443427 it yields 0.06, both within $10^{-10}$. Edge: a one-year zero at 80 yields 0.25; a price of 130 gives a negative yield; equal function values raise `ZeroDivisionError` | Adding the face value undiscounted fails the par test |
| `drl.calc.golden-section` | U4 | `golden(f, a, b, tol=1e-8)` minimises a unimodal function without derivatives | $(x - 2)^2$ on $[0, 5]$ gives 2 within $10^{-8}$ in 44 evaluations. Edge: $f(x) = x$ on $[0, 1]$ gives 0 (a minimum at the end); `tol <= 0` raises `ValueError` | Keeping the wrong sub-interval fails the first test |
| `drl.calc.adaptive-simpson` | I4 | `adaptive_simpson(f, a, b, tol)` splits where the two-level Simpson estimates disagree by more than $15\,$`tol`, and adds the Richardson correction | $\int_0^1\sqrt x\,dx$ within $10^{-8}$ of $\frac23$ with fewer than 2,000 evaluations (985 at `tol = 1e-10`). Edge: $\int_{-1}^1\lvert x \rvert\,dx = 1$ to $10^{-12}$ (a kink at a split point); $a = b$ gives 0; reversed limits give $-\int_0^\pi\sin x\,dx = -2$ | One Simpson panel without splitting fails the $\sqrt x$ test |
| `drl.calc.heun` | I6 | `heun(f, t0, y0, h, steps)`: Euler's slope, then the slope at the predicted end, averaged | For $y' = y$ to $t = 1$, the error ratio between $h = 0.1$ and $0.05$ lies in $[3.6, 4.4]$ (3.85: second order, against Euler's 1.92). Edge: 0 steps returns `[(t0, y0)]`; $y' = 0$ stays constant | Plain Euler fails the ratio test |
| `drl.calc.cos-horner` | X3 | `taylor_cos(x, n)` by Horner's rule in $x^2$; `terms_needed(x, eps)` from the Lagrange bound $\frac{\lvert x \rvert^{2n+2}}{(2n + 2)!} < \varepsilon$ | `terms_needed(1, 1e-10)` is 6 and `terms_needed(3, 1e-10)` is 10, and each meets its accuracy at $x = 0.5, 1, 2, 3$. Edge: `taylor_cos(0, 5) == 1.0`; $n = 0$ gives 1.0 | The bound with $(2n + 1)!$ fails the count test with 7 |
| `drl.calc.constraint-scan` | M4 | `scan_extrema(f, param, t0, t1, n)` checks a Lagrange answer by scanning a parametrised constraint | $x + y$ on the unit circle: $\pm\sqrt2$ within $10^{-9}$; $xy$ on $x + 2y = 100$: 1,250. Edge: lesson 121's plate $x^2 + 2y^2 - x$ on its boundary circle has largest value $\frac94$ and smallest 0 (the interior minimum $-\frac14$ is not on the boundary) | Scanning half the circle fails the $-\sqrt2$ test |
| `drl.calc.ball-volume` | M6 | `mc_ball_volume(d, n, seed)` estimates the unit ball's volume in $d$ dimensions from points in $[-1, 1]^d$, against $\frac{\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(\frac d2 + 1)}$ | $d = 2$ and $d = 5$ within four standard errors of $\pi$ and 5.2638 at $n = 100{,}000$, seed 1. Edge: $d = 1$ gives exactly 2; at $d = 10$ under 1% of points land in the ball (0.23%), the curse of dimensionality of lesson 137 | Forgetting the factor $2^d$ fails the $d = 2$ test |

## 4. Topic exam blueprint and the 1.3 exit test

### 4.1 The finals-style paper (`exm.calc.finals`)

One paper is both the 1.3 topic exam and the in-app gate of the 1.3 exit (decision 12). It is of assessment.md's `finals` kind (3.1), with two untimed sections on one clock and a section bar (decision 27).

| Property | Value |
|---|---|
| Duration | 180 minutes on one clock (wall-clock, with the 15-minute pause budget of assessment.md decision 3). Suggested split shown on the rules screen: Section A 125 minutes, Section B 55 minutes |
| Items | 11 multi-part questions, 100 marks, marked per part: Section A (in the style of an MIT 18.01 final), 8 questions, 70 marks; Section B (in the style of the 18.02 partial-derivative and Lagrange problem sets, with a double integral), 3 questions, 30 marks |
| Difficulty mix, by marks | 20 at d2, 50 at d3, 20 at d4, 10 at d5 (design research 8.4), checked per form by CI within 5 percentage points |
| Format mix, by marks | Expression 28; numeric 21; numeric with a short choice part 7; interval 2; point or curve on a graph 2; long-form 40 (four questions of 10, meeting the `finals` kind's 40% floor). Choice appears only inside mixed parts (at most 2 marks a part) |
| Calculator | None. Answers in exact form ($\frac{3\pi}{16}$, $\sqrt[3]{2}$, $\ln 2$); numeric items accept exact forms and decimals to 4 significant figures |
| Pass mark | **70 of 100, and at least 49 of Section A's 70** (the curriculum's bar on the 18.01 half), set as `pass: 0.7` and `sectionPass: { A: 0.7 }` on the paper (`assessment.md` decision 27) |
| Forms | At least 3 parallel forms; 60 of 100 marks come from generator slots (above assessment.md's 50% floor), so a resit never repeats a parameter point; each long-form slot draws from an authored pool of at least six items |
| Practice | Two practice papers from the practice pool, never the exam reserve: **P1, the 18.01 rehearsal** (180 minutes, single-variable only, 100 marks: Section A's eight slots scaled up plus three single-variable questions on related rates, Newton's method or the mean value theorem, and arc length or polar area, the shape of a real 18.01 final as closely as the item pool allows) and **P2, the finals shape** (this paper's shape) |
| Resit | 7-day cool-down and a remediation set on the failed skills (assessment.md 3.8 for finals-style papers); a resit draws the freshest form and unseen generator points from the exam reserve (assessment.md decision 4) |
| Help | None: Bertie is `away` app-wide while the paper runs, shown in the exam top bar (ai-tutor.md 2.12, bertie.md 7) |

**Slots.**

| Slot | Section | Levels | Content | Format | Difficulty by part | Marks |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| A1 | A | D3, D4 | Differentiate four functions: a product inside a chain; a quotient or a logarithm; $x^{g(x)}$ by logarithmic differentiation; the tangent line to an implicit curve | X, X, X, X | d2 4, d3 4 | 8 |
| A2 | A | D2, D1, U1 or X3 | Limits: (a) a limit recognised as a derivative from first principles; (b) a $1^\infty$ or $0 \cdot \infty$ form; (c) a limit by series or by repeated L'Hôpital | N, N, N | (a) d2 2, (b) d3 2, (c) d4 2 | 6 |
| A3 | A | U2, U3 | Graph analysis: critical points and their type, inflection points, asymptotes, a sketch graded by features | N, I, P, N | d2 2, d3 6 | 8 |
| A4 | A | U4, U5 | Applied optimisation with a written argument that the optimum is global (pool: box or can, lifeguard, pricing, Kelly, closest point on a curve, cost of a pipeline route) | Long-form | d3 | 10 |
| A5 | A | I3 | Integration: substitution, parts, partial fractions, a trigonometric integral | X, X, X, X | d2 2, d3 6 | 8 |
| A6 | A | I1 or I2, I4, I5, I6 | (a) the derivative of an integral with a variable limit, or an average value, by form; (b) an improper integral: decide convergence and find the value; (c) a volume by discs or shells, or a polar area, by form; (d) a separable differential equation with a condition | X, N with a C part, N, X | (a) d2 2, (b) d3 3, (c) d3 3, (d) d4 2 | 10 |
| A7 | A | X1, X2, X3 | Series and Taylor: a Taylor polynomial, a Lagrange remainder bound with the number of terms needed for an accuracy, and an interval of convergence with both endpoints justified (pool) | Long-form | d4 | 10 |
| A8 | A | D1, D5, U2, I2, X3, by form | A proof from the pool: an $\varepsilon$-$\delta$ limit; the chain rule; the mean value theorem from Rolle; an inequality by the mean value theorem; the fundamental theorem, part 1; the irrationality of $e$ | Long-form | d5 | 10 |
| B1 | B | M1, M2 | (a) partial derivatives and the tangent plane at a point; (b) a chain rule through a dependency tree; (c) the direction and rate of steepest ascent; (d) a constrained partial derivative with the held variable named | X, X, N, N | (a) d2 4, (b) d3 2, (c) d3 2, (d) d4 2 | 10 |
| B2 | B | M4 | Lagrange multipliers in two or three variables with one constraint: every candidate found and compared, and the multiplier interpreted as a rate (pool) | Long-form | d3 | 10 |
| B3 | B | M3, M5 or M6 | (a) find and classify the critical points of a polynomial in two variables; (b) set up a double integral over a region between two curves; (c) evaluate an integral by swapping the order or by polar coordinates, by form | N with a C part, X, N | (a) d2 4, (b) d3 2, (c) d4 4 | 10 |

**Totals.** Marks 70 + 30 = 100. By difficulty: d2 $4 + 2 + 2 + 2 + 2 + 4 + 4 = 20$; d3 $4 + 2 + 6 + 10 + 6 + 6 + 4 + 10 + 2 = 50$; d4 $2 + 2 + 10 + 2 + 4 = 20$; d5 10. Long-form 40. Generator slots 60.

**Coverage.** Nineteen levels are tested on every form; the rotating parts (A2(b) and (c), A6(a), A8 and B3(c)) add D1, D5, U1, I1, I2 or M6, and CI requires every form to reach at least 22 of the 25 levels, above assessment.md's 80% rule. By course, marks run D 12, U 20, I 18, X 10, M 30, with A8's 10 rotating among D, U, I and X; against the lesson-hour shares (D 21%, U 17.5%, I 23%, X 15%, M 23%), course M is over-weighted by about 7 marks on purpose, because the exit names the 18.02 half separately and it deserves a full section, and course D is under-weighted where A8 rotates away from it, because its differentiation skills are tested again inside every other question.

**Worked example of one slot (A8, the mean value theorem variant, d5).** "Let $f$ be differentiable on $\mathbb R$ with $\lvert f'(x) \rvert \le 1$ for every $x$, and $f(0) = 0$. Prove $\lvert f(x) \rvert \le \lvert x \rvert$ for all $x$, and show that $g(x) = \sin x$ satisfies the hypotheses. Then prove that $\lvert \sin a - \sin b \rvert \le \lvert a - b \rvert$." Rubric (10): 2 for applying the mean value theorem on $[0, x]$ (or $[x, 0]$) with its hypotheses checked; 2 for $f(x) - f(0) = f'(c)x$; 2 for the bound; 1 for $x = 0$; 1 for $\lvert\cos x\rvert \le 1$; 2 for the second inequality on $[b, a]$. The model answer's algebraic lines are checked by SymPy in CI (section 6).

### 4.2 What the real exit papers are, and how ours differ

- **MIT 18.01 final.** A three-hour, closed-book, no-calculator paper covering the whole single-variable course [recalled; check the duration and rules printed on the specific final chosen]. The 18.01SC (Fall 2010) final is posted with solutions [searched by `canon-gap.md`]; the 18.01 Fall 2006 exam materials may hold more [check which have full solutions]. The four 18.01SC unit exams are companion work at D5, U5, I5 and I6, with the 18.01 Fall 2006 Problem Set 8B sat timed at X3 (decision 17), so the learner meets MIT's standard five times before the real final. Section A copies its breadth (differentiation, limits, curve sketching, optimisation, integration techniques and applications, differential equations, series) and its no-calculator, exact-answer rule. It differs in three honest ways: it is shorter (125 suggested minutes, not 180); it adds a proof slot (A8) that an 18.01 final may not have, because the outcomes of 1.3 include proofs; and its method marks are approximated by per-part marking, which is stricter than a human examiner on unusual but valid methods. Practice paper P1 removes the first difference, the unit exams calibrate the learner against MIT's own papers on the way, and the real final confirms the rest (assessment.md decision 1).
- **MIT 18.02 problem sets on partial derivatives and Lagrange multipliers.** The curriculum asks that they be done, with no score bar. In 18.02SC these are the problem sets of Unit 2 (Partial Derivatives), Part A (Functions of Two Variables, Tangent Approximation and Optimization) and Part C (Lagrange Multipliers and Constrained Differentials) [both titles searched], and Part B (chain rule, gradient and directional derivatives) [check title and problem-set names]. Our homework sets M1 to M4 cover the same ground, written in that style: multi-part problems, non-independent variables, least squares, boundary checks.

### 4.3 The 1.3 exit, rebuilt in the app

| Part | Curriculum line | Gate (in the app) | Confirm (logged) | Taught in | Runs on |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `1.3.exit` | The MIT 18.01 final exam at 70%, plus the 18.02 problem sets on partial derivatives and Lagrange multipliers | (1) `exm.calc.finals` at 70%, whose own pass includes Section A at 70% (`sectionPass`, decision 27); (2) the homework sets M1 to M4 submitted, and every item below full marks redone correctly once (`sets` instrument, decision 29) | (1) A real 18.01 final, timed at its printed length, closed book, self-marked against MIT's posted solutions, at 70% (`real-paper`, `mit-1801-final`); (2) the 18.02SC Unit 2 problem sets, Parts A to C, logged as done, each wrong problem entered in the Redo queue (`problem-sets`) | All of 1.3; Section B and the sets from course M | Both |

```ts
// exit data for 1.3, in assessment.md 2.10's ExitPart schema (draft 2: nothing here is a proposal any more)
{ id: '1.3.exit',
  text: 'The MIT 18.01 final exam at 70%, plus the 18.02 problem sets on partial derivatives and Lagrange multipliers',
  gate: [ { kind: 'exam', exam: 'exm.calc.finals', pass: 0.7 },        // the paper's own pass includes sectionPass { A: 0.7 }
          { kind: 'sets', sets: ['set.multivar.surfaces', 'set.multivar.gradient',
                                 'set.multivar.optimise', 'set.multivar.lagrange'], rule: 'submitted-and-redone' } ],
  confirm: [ { kind: 'real-paper', paper: 'mit-1801-final', count: 1, pass: 0.7, timedMinutes: 180 },
             { kind: 'problem-sets', refs: ['18.02SC Unit 2 Part A problem set', '18.02SC Unit 2 Part B problem set',
                                            '18.02SC Unit 2 Part C problem set'] } ],
  estHours: 15 }

// the paper's fields that carry the section bar (content-format.md 3.2 Exam, with assessment.md 3.1's additions)
{ zq: 1, id: 'exm.calc.finals', kind: 'finals', title: 'Calculus: finals-style paper', minutes: 180, marks: 100,
  pass: 0.7, calculator: 'none', mix: { '2': 0.2, '3': 0.5, '4': 0.2, '5': 0.1 },
  sectionPass: { A: 0.7 },
  forms: [ /* at least 3; each holds section 'A' (slots A1 to A8, 70 marks) and section 'B' (B1 to B3, 30 marks), untimed */ ] }
```

**Order and test-out.** "Sit each stage's exit test first" applies at stage level; within the stage, a learner may sit `exm.calc.finals` before the 1.3 lessons to test out (assessment.md 7.3). A pass credits the topic's lesson hours as "tested out", and the planner then offers the real 18.01 final and the 18.02SC problem sets. Real finals are single-use, so the planner offers the confirm only after the in-app paper is passed (assessment.md decision 1).

**Self-marking the real final.** The learner marks against MIT's solutions using a short guide shown beside the logging form: full marks for a part only when the method and the answer are both right; half marks for a correct method with an arithmetic slip, if the posted solution's structure allows partial credit; nothing for an answer with no working. The logged percentage is labelled "self-marked" (assessment.md decision 18), and a confirm below 70% sends the learner back to remediation on the paper's weakest skills before a second real final is offered [open item 2: how many public finals exist].

## 5. Reading and resource map per level

### 5.0 How to use this map

- **One primary resource per topic** (curriculum rule 1). For 1.3 the route is: **3Blue1Brown, Essence of Calculus** before each course for intuition, as the curriculum says; **MIT 18.01 lectures** (Jerison, Fall 2006, the videos 18.01SC uses) and **MIT 18.02 lectures** (Auroux, Fall 2007, the videos 18.02SC uses) as the lecture parallel; **OpenStax Calculus volumes 1 to 3** as the book and problem bank; **18.01SC and 18.02SC** problem sets and exams, with MIT's solutions, as the external problems that set the exit's standard. **Stewart, Calculus: Early Transcendentals, 9th edition** is the alternative book; its sections are given in brackets beside OpenStax's. A learner who owns Stewart reads Stewart instead of OpenStax, not as well.
- **University and quant resources** (Spivak, Thompson, STEP, Mathematics for Machine Learning, Goodfellow et al., Boyd and Vandenberghe, Baydin et al., Zhou) are listed where they teach the level's idea better or show its use. Blitzstein and Hwang and Hull are previews here, because 1.5 and 1.7 read them in full.
- **Companion hours** (`Level.companion`, content-format.md 3.8). Every row carries its hours, and each level's rows sum to the level's companion total in the table below; the 25 totals sum to the external block of section 1.4, 61.5 h. The counting rules are decision 16's: 40 minutes per lecture at 1.25 times speed, rounded to the quarter hour per row (two lectures 1.25 h, three 2.0 h, four 2.75 h), 15 minutes per 3Blue1Brown chapter (30 for the long Taylor-series chapter), 7.5 minutes per OpenStax exercise, 30 minutes per 18.01SC part problem set, 1 hour per 18.01SC unit exam and for the timed 18.01 Problem Set 8B, 2 hours per 18.02SC exit set (Part A's split between M1 and M3, 1 hour each); the two 18.01 lectures of I5 are watched at 1.5 times speed (1.0 h). Rows marked stretch, optional, preview or UK route carry 0 and sit outside the budget.
- **Problems over reading** (rule 2). Every row says what to do. Problems are 29.0 of the 61.5 hours; with homework, exams, practice and projects, 84% of 1.3 is solving (section 1.4).
- **Roles and licences.** The "Id and role" column gives the `src.*` id for `content/sources.yaml` and the `Citation.use` role (basis, parallel-reading, problem-inspired, further). Ids are `canon-gap.md` revision 2's where it has one; ids marked † are proposed here. Nothing in this item is adapted (`problem-adapted`): OpenStax Calculus and MIT OpenCourseWare are CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 [searched], so homework mirrors their problem types with new wording and numbers, and every other source is cited only.
- **[check]** marks a chapter, section, lecture or unit number or title recalled but not confirmed here. **MIT lecture numbers** follow OCW's video numbering, which counts the exam sessions that have no video (18.01 lecture 26 is an exam session, so the integration-technique lectures are 27 to 30, and the last lectures are 35 L'Hôpital, 36 improper integrals, 37 series, 38 Taylor's series, 39 final review). Rows tag each number [searched] or [check]; OCW itself could not be opened from this environment, so the searched numbers come from OCW page titles in search results. **OpenStax section numbers** are from the first edition of the three volumes [check against the current edition]. **Stewart section numbers** follow the 8th and 9th editions of Early Transcendentals, which I believe share them for these chapters [check].

**Links** (free unless stated). OpenStax Calculus: https://openstax.org/details/books/calculus-volume-1, https://openstax.org/details/books/calculus-volume-2, https://openstax.org/details/books/calculus-volume-3. MIT 18.01SC Single Variable Calculus (Fall 2010): https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-01sc-single-variable-calculus-fall-2010/. MIT 18.01 Single Variable Calculus (Fall 2006): https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-01-single-variable-calculus-fall-2006/. Strang, Calculus (MIT OCW resource, free PDF): https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/res-18-001-calculus-online-textbook-spring-2005/ [check current address]. MIT RES.18-005 Highlights of Calculus (Strang's short videos): https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/res-18-005-highlights-of-calculus-spring-2010/. 3Blue1Brown: https://www.3blue1brown.com/topics/calculus. Khan Academy: https://www.khanacademy.org/math/multivariable-calculus. Paul's Online Math Notes (Calculus I to III): https://tutorial.math.lamar.edu/.
